## DEP-017 — exact CI integrity regression, root-cause analysis and complete Chapter 1 restoration (3 November 2026)

**A genuine source regression was found and repaired; no mathematical certification is inferred from the correction.** Newest pre-repair run, Actions Build books #1558 (run 41721065982) at exact head 2c0e567fad1e837542920b295f719ff77e0f275f, terminated FAILURE in the list/configuration unit-test job (job 126213686267). It ran **215 tests in 11.213 seconds**, reporting **15 failures and 27 skips**. Every failure implicated malformed Chapter 1 source structure (duplicated exercise labels, hints, figure label, two section headings, a mismatched exercise environment and total inventory 129 instead of 144). Some newly added source guards caught these issues too. No arithmetic assertions failed in that run. The later build/EPUB/site jobs were all skipped, so the failed run verifies *nothing* about the rendered book on that exact commit.

**Precise root cause, not a mystery:** an automated JavaScript string replacement in source commit 0b57fd907e6f8f5133cd5315ea6a9ddf11156ff2 inserted prose containing the typographical fragment "base $p$''". JavaScript's replacement-string grammar interprets the two-character sequence `$'` (the closing math-dollar sign followed by the first closing apostrophe) as **the entire remaining suffix after the replacement target**, not literal text. The unintended suffix was appended into the Chapter 1 source. The problem was compounded by subsequent edits and missed until the #1558 structural test gate. The first sound source before the bug was exact commit 799c09d01ef2474d6db879dbc29008d59cae341f, length **57,319 bytes/characters as received by the UTF-8 GitHub text connector** and exactly 15 exercises.

**Deterministic restoration:** at commit 290957f41673634887be92a6a6cfe73094340ac3, rebuild the full Chapter 1 source from the known-good 799c09d... manuscript and reapply *only* the four intended edits: (1) explain base-p digits in the exercise statement, (2) explain repeated division before the base-p carry proof, (3) illustrate 12/18=2/3 at the beginning of pb:parity's printed solution, and (4) remove the misleading “irrational-looking slope” from pb:orchard's hint. Apply each replacement via the **function callback form** `s.replace(old, () => replacement)`, so dollar-sign sequences in mathematics and typographical quotation marks are taken literally. The reconstructed complete source has **57,901 characters, 15 exercise starts, 15 exercise ends, 15 hints, 15 solutions, 27 unique label definitions of 27 total, and ends at its original bibliography `end{itemize}`**; all four intended prose corrections are present. The revised 899/372 earlier examples and 1071/462 exercise are preserved. No mathematical question, solution or label was dropped in the controlled repair.

**Regression prevention:** commit a294c2386b53e5246f5240d17b94514dea319a2c strengthened tools/test_volume2_ch01_carries_euclid_valuations.py with explicit 15 exercise start/end, one hints section, one solutions section, and canonical end-of-file checks. The existing whole-manuscript CI already caught the bug; the targeted guard makes future accidental suffix duplication easier to diagnose.

**First-use follow-up:** the full #1558 test log also complained about the new main-text example's explicit `Exercise~\ref{ex:t1-euclid1071}` link, which appeared before that exercise was introduced. Restoring the chapter alone could not satisfy this ordering requirement. Commit **4cf32fed1c475d4c6d40ced648ba909e7c341b07** removes the *forward link* and says plainly that the exercises offer a different pair to try. It preserves the separate (899,372) taught example and the original (1071,462) problem. The updated source was rechecked immediately: 15 exercises/hints/solutions, 27 distinct labels and no such forward link. This is a genuine prerequisite-order correction in addition to the structural restoration.

**Remaining validation boundary:** the restore's new exact head, and any subsequent plan/PR documentation commits, need their own **terminal CI success**. Run #1558 is red and remains in the permanent ledger; the old #1545 was cancelled, though its earlier jobs passed. The restored source's structural counts were verified directly by the GitHub connector before commit; that check is not a substitute for GitHub Actions, LaTeX compilation, EPUB/site checks or all-page visual proof.

## DEP-018 — complete independent proof check of Pólya orchard and first-use figure repairs (3 November 2026)

**Scope:** The *printed Chapter 1* key `pb:orchard`, all three subparts, exact equality conditions, the full hint and printed solution, both general mathematical arguments, real-valued distance-to-ray geometry, and the diagram's arithmetical claim (not yet printed-visual certified). Branch PR #103 is open/draft. Since the 72 named results are a separate inventory, this exercise changes the count from **28/144** to **29/144 VERIFIED_TWICE internally**; named results remain **8/72**. All 15 printed Chapter 1 exercise keys now have two internal mathematical proofs; this emphatically does **not** certify the chapter's unnamed proofs, figures or page layout.

### Part (a), Minkowski for a centred closed rectangle: two proofs

**First general proof, the manuscript's continuous area-overlap route.** Let S be any *closed*, centred rectangle, in any orientation, with area(S)≥4. Set K=(1/2)S, so area(K)≥1. If area(K)>1, cut K by the integer unit-square tiling z+[0,1)². Translate each cut piece into [0,1)². Because the sum of their areas is area(K)>1, the translated pieces cannot be disjoint up to zero-area intersections: two contain common points. We can choose **distinct** x,y∈K, in different integer cells, with x−y∈Z² nonzero. As K is centrally symmetric and convex, x−y∈K−K=(1/2)S−(1/2)S=S. Therefore S contains a nonzero lattice point. Notice that the exact value 4 comes from the halving in each of two coordinates, which makes area(K)=area(S)/4.

**Exact equality (area(S)=4), first route.** For each integer m≥1 enlarge S to S_m=(1+1/m)S. Its area exceeds 4, so choose a nonzero lattice point z_m∈S_m. All lie in bounded 2S, which contains *finitely* many lattice points. At least one fixed z≠0 recurs along unbounded m. Since S is closed and S_m contracts to S, that fixed z belongs to S. **Closed is necessary** at equality: the open square (-1,1)² has area4 but contains no nonzero lattice point. This precise counterexample and boundary explanation were inserted into the book, commit `78d4aa99d22f9609dd726499862b97e77e5ef201`.

**Second general proof, finite-grid pigeonhole, independent of the continuous overlap.** For area(K)>1, choose integer q large and count the points of the rational grid (1/q)Z² lying in K. For a rectangle with finitely many straight edges, elementary square-mesh area counting gives
#(K∩(1/q)Z²)=q² area(K)+O(q).
A direct reason: after multiplying by q, count the full integer unit squares inside the expanded rectangle; only O(q) unit squares meet its four boundary segments (each has length O(q)), so the difference between area and the count of their grid vertices is O(q). Because area(K)>1, for sufficiently large q there are **more than q² rational grid points** in K. Among their integer coordinate numerators (i,j), only q² residue pairs modulo q exist. Two distinct grid points have the same pair of residues, hence their difference is an **integer** nonzero vector z. Since K−K=S by central symmetry, z∈S. This is a genuine *finite arithmetic* pigeonhole proof, not an appeal to the continuous overlap used in Pass A. Handle area(K)=1 separately by the same finite-lattice compactness enlargement described above; closedness is essential. The grid approximation needs only elementary planar geometry and finite area counting, not unproved general measurable-set theorems. Adversarial integer-arithmetic samples checked **135 rotated rectangles** of area 4 or larger, including rotations with rational sine/cosine and long/narrow area-four rectangles, verifying actual nonzero witnesses on *closed* boundaries.

### Part (b), every ray blocked for r≥1/R: two derivations and endpoints

**First general proof, from manuscript Pass A of (a).** If r≥1, a nearest tree at (1,0) (or one of the other nearest cardinal trees) blocks rays; in fact for r≥1 the trunk at (1,0) contains the observer at the origin, so every ray intersects it. When 1/R≤r<1, let e be the unit vector in an *arbitrary* viewing direction, e_perp a perpendicular unit vector, and take the closed rectangle S={ue+ve_perp: |u|≤R, |v|≤r}. It is centred, has area 4Rr≥4, so (a) gives z∈Z²∩S, z≠0. Replace z by −z if necessary so its longitudinal coordinate u≥0. We must have u>0: otherwise z is perpendicular and |z|=|v|≤r<1, impossible for a nonzero integer vector. Thus z projects to the **ray**, not merely its two-sided supporting line. Also |z|²=u²+v²≤R²+r²<R²+1, while |z|² is an integer. It follows that |z|²≤R² and the tree at z is inside the closed radius-R orchard. Its transverse distance |v|≤r, so its closed trunk disk meets the viewing ray. This includes the **equality r=1/R**, where area(S)=4 and the exact closed-area equality from part (a) is needed. It holds for every angle, not just coordinate or rational slopes.

**Independent general proof (second path via finite-grid lemma).** Repeat the *finite-grid proof*, not the continuous-area-overlap proof, directly on the oriented strip S just described. For 1/R<r<1 its half-rectangle K has area Rr>1, so for q large enough the grid-in-K construction supplies a nonzero integer z in S. For the **critical r=1/R**, enlarge S by 1+1/m, choose integer vectors by the strict grid argument, and invoke the finite bounded-lattice recurrence to get z∈S. As above choose z or −z with positive projection, and bound its squared length by the **integer inequality** |z|²<R²+1. For r≥1, a tree's radius already reaches the observer. This second proof generates the needed lattice vector with a different combinatorial grid construction; it shares only elementary Euclidean geometry and the final unavoidable direction/length check. A separate numerical geometric enumeration checked **2,112 rays**, 96 angular directions for each integer 2≤R≤23, at exactly r=1/R; for each found an in-disc tree disk intersecting the **ray**, using the distance-to-ray formula (not merely distance to the supporting line). Finite samples corroborate, not establish, the universal statement.

### Part (c), escaping ray and a *sharp tangency for this ray*

**First general proof, lattice determinant.** Choose direction w=(R,1), with R integer ≥2. For any tree centre z=(a,b)∈Z² with 0<|z|≤R, the signed planar determinant of (w,z) is Rb−a. The distance from z to the complete line Rw equals |a−Rb|/sqrt(R²+1). If a−Rb≠0 this is ≥1/sqrt(R²+1), because the numerator is a *nonzero integer*. If a−Rb=0, the integer coordinates force z=b(R,1), whose length is at least sqrt(R²+1)>R for b≠0. Therefore **no** tree centre in the orchard lies on the line and every one is at least the claimed distance from the whole line. For r<1/sqrt(R²+1), no trunk meets the line, hence none meets its outward ray. No unproved Diophantine approximation theorem is used.

**Independent analytic geometry reconstruction.** For an arbitrary z=(a,b), parametrise the *full* line by t(R,1) and minimise
F(t)=(a−Rt)²+(b−t)²
= (R²+1)(t−(Ra+b)/(R²+1))²+(a−Rb)²/(R²+1).
This is just completing the square, independent of assuming an area/determinant distance formula. For a tree the same integer cross-difference is nonzero by the argument above, so even the minimum distance to the full line is at least the threshold. The distance to the **ray** (t≥0) cannot be smaller. This independently reconstructs the escape claim from high-school algebra. Moreover the tree at z=(R−1,1) actually lies in the radius-R disc: (R−1)²+1≤R² for every R≥1, and has positive projection Ra+b=R(R−1)+1>0. Its determinant difference (R−1)−R=−1. Therefore it is **exactly tangent** to the chosen ray at r=1/sqrt(R²+1). The strict inequality in the question is not a typographical accident, and this tangent does **not** claim the global visibility threshold for every direction. The manuscript now explains this exact borderline tangency (commit `78d4aa9`). Exact integer tests checked the minimum nonzero |a−Rb|=1 on the ray for all 49 integer radii 2≤R≤50, including the tangent candidate.

### Figure, narrative and prerequisites (verified source-level; final render remains open)

- **Introduced before use:** Volume 1 `volume1-first-principles/chapters/ch07-linear-algebra.tex` explicitly teaches the two-dimensional determinant as signed area, the 2×2 rotation matrix and Euclidean plane projections (near lines 135–165), confirmed by a new entry in `docs/reviews/volume2-volume1-prerequisite-crosswalk-2026-11-03.md` (commit `394fcb7ee89c94bd84710180e9a1af66bc456ed7`). Chapter 1 already proves integer gcd, Bézout and FTA. The special Minkowski lattice theorem is **not** an imported Volume 13 result; it is taught and proved as part (a). The second area-counting proof is a separate reviewer verification, not an unintroduced analytic result inserted into the reader-facing book.
- **Misleading text corrected:** earlier DEP-016 had already replaced the claim that slope 1/R was “irrational-looking,” though it is rational. The `\\leads` description also falsely called direction (R,1) “axis-aligned”; corrected to “sight line through (R,1)” in commit `5a0e35bc62cb406b936c2ca8756031d1e8ea9c1f`.
- **First-use violation in figure removed:** the caption had asserted without any Chapter 1 proof that a proportion close to 6/pi² of lattice points are visible. Replaced this with a local count: **224 visible among 376 nonzero integer points of the radius-11 disc, about 59.6%** (also checked independently by integer enumeration). Both the figure claim and the count are now elementary, with no unexplained Basel/zeta-density import. A new paragraph after the figure proves visible⇔gcd(|a|,|b|)=1: if gcd g>1 the point (a/g,b/g) blocks the ray; if gcd=1, Bézout shows that a closer integer point t(a,b) with 0<t<1 would force t∈Z, a contradiction (commit `046cd55d805d3775085b1bb05ff08379aa6d5190`). This is the mathematical meaning of “visible” for **zero-thickness point trees**, distinct from discs of radius r.
- **Additional arithmetic figure probe:** for R=11, the caption's rust direction (11,4) lies outside the planted disc (11²+4²=137>121), and (3,1) is an in-disc point at determinant distance exactly 1/sqrt(137) from the ray; there is no tree centre *on* the line because gcd(11,4)=1. Hence thin enough trunks permit that sight line as described.
- **New regression:** `tools/test_volume2_ch01_orchard_geometry.py` committed at `e9c9ca5be7669f12eb01eb848d90c93498f8485e` checks all 135 rotated exact-rational closed rectangles, 2,112 threshold ray directions, 49 exact determinant/tangency radii, figure R=11 lattice and visible-point counts, labels/question/hint/solution and 15/15/15 Chapter 1 structure. All algorithms were separately reproduced with independent Python arithmetic before commit; **this finite evidence is not a general proof**.
- **Render limitation:** figure data and caption were mathematically checked, but the **actual graphic at final PDF/EPUB/print resolution and physical size has not been visually inspected in this checkpoint**. The chapter is therefore not fully approved and P00–P13/PDF/EPUB/print/KDP gates remain OPEN. No claim of external peer review.

## DEP-019 — Chapter 2 central-binomial lemma independently verified (3 November 2026)

**Scope.** Printed Chapter 2 `volume2-integers/chapters/ch02-primes.tex`, named `lem:binom` (around lines 178–194), three clauses: (i) for n≥1 central binomial lower/upper and odd-central upper bounds; (ii) every exact prime exponent a=v_p(binomial(2n,n)) satisfies p^a≤2n; (iii) for p>sqrt(2n) a≤1 and, when n≥5, primes in (2n/3,n] do not divide the central binomial. The statement, source proof, constants, low-n restrictions, later use sites, and actual earlier prerequisites were checked.

**Pass A, complete reconstruction of the printed argument.** Let B=binom(2n,n). Pascal's row sums to 4^n, B is its largest term, and both endpoint terms equal1. Therefore 4^n≤(2n−1)B+2≤2nB, because B≥2 for n≥1; the row sum also immediately yields B≤4^n. For odd row 2n+1 the two central equal coefficients sum to ≤2^(2n+1)=2·4^n, so either is ≤4^n. At n=1, B=2=4/(2), odd central3≤4. For each prime p, Legendre's formula previously established in Chapter 1 gives
a=v_p(B)=Σ_{k≥1}(floor(2n/p^k)−2 floor(n/p^k)).
Each summand is0 or1 and vanishes whenever p^k>2n, so a≤floor(log_p(2n)), giving p^a≤2n (also when a=0). For p>sqrt(2n), p²>2n, so at most k=1 contributes and a≤1. For 2n/3<p≤n, 2≤2n/p<3 and 1≤n/p<3/2, so k=1 contributes2−2=0; when n≥5, p²>(2n/3)²>2n, eliminating all k≥2. Thus a=0. The n≥5 restriction is **essential** for that clause: n=2,p=2 belongs to (4/3,2] and divides binom(4,2)=6.

**Pass B, independent induction and *base-p schoolbook carries*.** For B_n=binom(2n,n), C_n=binom(2n+1,n), factorial cancellation gives
B_(n+1)/B_n=(4n+2)/(n+1)=4−2/(n+1), and C_(n+1)/C_n=(4n+6)/(n+2)=4−2/(n+2).
Both ratios are <4 and B_0=C_0=1, proving the two upper bounds by direct induction without using Pascal's row sum. The stronger lower bound starts at B_1=2=4/2. If B_n≥4^n/(2n), then
B_(n+1)≥[4^n/(2n)]·[(4n+2)/(n+1)]≥4^(n+1)/(2(n+1)),
because 4n+2≥4n. This independently proves (i). For (ii), use **the earlier independently checked Chapter 1 exercise `ex:t1-binomial-carry`**, not the lemma's Legendre floor formula: v_p(B_n) equals the number of carrying positions when n+n is added in base p. A carry across p^j cannot occur if p^j>2n, as the entire sum lies below that place value. Consequently a≤#{j≥1:p^j≤2n}=floor(log_p(2n)); hence p^a≤2n. If p²>2n, at most one carry can occur. For the excluded interval when n≥5, p>2n/3 forces p≥5 and n=p+t with 0≤t<p/2. Base-p digits of n are (1,t), so adding n+n gives (2,2t), with neither 2 nor 2t producing a carry (2<p and 2t<p). Therefore a=0 without any Legendre floor calculation. This checks every printed clause with a *separate proof*.

**Reader corrections.** Commit `8c4fce68ecdb6896c8d0498e1375d4b6ddb5b6e1` makes the exact exponent quantifier explicit (the old phrase “every prime power dividing exactly” was misleading) and expands the compressed central Pascal comparison, explaining both endpoint terms and the odd-row pair; the n=1 lower-bound equality is checked. Commit `38b136c892e41104795346d2ea7d212bd31611a4` gives the necessary low-n counterexample n=2,p=2.

**Independent finite checks:** New standard-library test `tools/test_volume2_ch02_central_binomial.py` at `6d52dda1e87d83e8848b11b3b46e5f6588d861af` checks (i) using exact integer arithmetic for n=1,…,1000; for n=1,…,180 and all primes p≤2n it independently counts the actual prime valuation of binom(2n,n) by integer division and the carrying count from base-p arithmetic. Exactly **7,197 (n,p)** comparisons checked p^a≤2n and the two prime ranges. Also checks the n=2,p=2 counterexample and source result-order. The arithmetic was independently reproduced in an isolated Python session and passed, but no finite test constitutes the general proof. Verification register commit `31eb9962988f12d62fd78bb199c55b33732d5484` records `lem:binom` as VERIFIED_TWICE.

**First-use graph:** Volume 1 `ch01-sums.tex` proves binomial coefficients and finite expansions. Volume 2 Chapter 1 earlier taught Legendre valuations (`ex:zeros`) and base-p carries (`ex:t1-binomial-carry`). The lemma is used **after its introduction** in `thm:cheb` and `thm:bertrand`, both still UNREVIEWED by the strict standard. Altering this lemma again must reopen their proof dependency paths.

The orchard exercise proof is complete but the embedded figure has not been inspected at final printed/EPUB size. Current exact-head full CI still requires terminal success. ## DEP-020 — Chapter 2 Chebyshev estimates verified by two mathematical proofs (3 November 2026)

**Scope:** `thm:cheb` in `volume2-integers/chapters/ch02-primes.tex`: (i) for EVERY real x≥1, P(x)=∏_{p≤x}p<4^x (including empty product); (ii) for EVERY integer n≥1, π(2n)≥(n ln4−ln(2n))/ln(2n), then positive x/ln x upper and lower constants for sufficiently large real x. Actual earlier tools: Chapter 1 FTA and `ex:zeros` Legendre, separately `ex:t1-binomial-carry`, Volume 1 Pascal/binomial proofs and its actual `ch05-sequences-series.tex` `thm:mvt` and `prop:early-differentiation` for natural logarithms. Source-site crosswalk was extended in commit `e96bd4f3a93a264d311ee31bf2c7b93facd36db3`. No Volume 6 PNT is assumed.

**Pass A: printed Chebyshev argument, reconstructed in every step.** At integer N=1, P(1)=1<4; at N=2, P(2)=2<16. If even N>2, N is not prime, P(N)=P(N−1)<4^(N−1)<4^N. If N=2m+1≥3, every prime m+1<p≤2m+1 appears once in numerator (2m+1)! and not in denominator m!(m+1)! of B=binom(2m+1,m): it divides B, and the product of these distinct primes is ≤B≤4^m by `lem:binom`. The remaining primes are ≤m+1, and strong induction gives P(N)≤P(m+1)B<4^(m+1)4^m=4^N. For arbitrary real x≥1, N=floor x≥1 and P(x)=P(N)<4^N≤4^x; this last real reduction, formerly implicit, is now explicit. For (ii), for B=binom(2n,n) the previous lemma states B≥4^n/(2n), and separately EACH prime-power p^{v_p(B)}≤2n; all primes dividing B are ≤2n. Therefore 4^n/(2n)≤B=∏_{p≤2n}p^{v_p(B)}≤(2n)^{π(2n)}. Because ln(2n)>0 for n≥1, logarithms give the exact claim without changing the sign. The endpoint n=1 is equality: π(2)=1.

**Pass B: independent even-and-odd half-interval induction; different central-binomial proof.** Instead of discarding even N, split primes ≤N at N/2 for both parities. For N=2m, every prime m<p≤2m divides binom(2m,m), so P(2m)≤P(m)binom(2m,m)≤P(m)4^m<4^(2m) using the induction hypothesis P(m)<4^m. For N=2m+1, split at m+1 and similarly use odd central binomial ≤4^m and P(m+1)<4^(m+1). The same finite N=floor x step covers all real x≥1. For (ii), independently derive the lower central B_n≥4^n/(2n) by B_1=2 and B_(n+1)=[(4n+2)/(n+1)]B_n; if the bound holds at n then B_(n+1)≥[4^n/(2n)]·[(4n+2)/(n+1)]≥4^(n+1)/[2(n+1)]. The earlier Chapter 1 *digit-carry* theorem (not the Legendre proof used by Pass A) bounds v_p(B_n) by the number of possible carry positions j with p^j≤2n, hence p^{v_p(B_n)}≤2n. Multiplying those bounded factors over π(2n) primes gives the same logarithmic inequality. This is a genuinely separate symbolic arithmetic route to each assertion, sharing only earlier established combinatorics.

**Real x and constants:** θ(x)=ln P(x)<x ln4. For x>1, every prime √x<p≤x contributes >½ln x, so π(x)<√x+2(ln4)x/ln x (use π(√x)≤√x). MVT from Volume 1 shows ln x<√x for x>1, or one can differentiate ln x/√x, whose maximum is 2/e<1 at x=e², so π(x)<(1+2ln4)x/ln x. For lower constants, with n=floor(x/2) (x≥2), π(x)≥[(x/2−1)ln4−ln x]/ln x = [x ln2−ln(4x)]/ln x. For x≥16, F(x)=½(ln2)x−ln(4x) is strictly increasing, F'(x)=ln2/2−1/x>0, and F(16)=2ln2>0. Therefore π(x)≥(ln2/2)x/ln x for every real x≥16. The old source said only “for large x”; the revised printed proof provides the explicit threshold. The other endpoint x=1 has empty prime product1<4 and does not require division by ln1.

**Exact regressions and source changes:** `tools/test_volume2_chebyshev_prime_bounds.py` added at `127bb98549fe914f0ee4506326328b91a31619fc` probes exact P(N)<4^N for all N≤1200, both-even-and-odd binomial half-interval divisibilities, several noninteger x per interval N≤1100, 250 fully factored central binomial coefficients with exponent bound, 5000 exact prime-count lower-bound cases, x≥16 constants, π(100)=25 and π(1000)=168, source theorem order and chapter sections. The independent Python recomputation of binomial n≤250 and 5000 prime-count comparisons passed. These are *support*, not proof of the universal claims. Book source changed at `23485e88569a9ed024fbcd3891cfb2dcebec5265`: makes P(1), P(2), real floor step, actual prime-power factorisation, ln sign and x≥16 explicit without importing a later theorem. The theorem register changed at `6ee997e5400ff16c99af13b89c3099a5a91d08b8`, marking exactly named theorem 10/72 VERIFIED_TWICE.

**Remaining:** 10/72 named and 29/144 exercise solutions verified twice internally; 62 named, 115 exercises, all unnamed passages and all rendered figure/EPUB/PDF/print/KDP/external review/P00–P13 gates remain OPEN. Record separate symbolic proofs and finite regression before marking the row. Keep draft/unmerged.


## DEP-021 — Bertrand's postulate completely checked twice, finite and infinite ranges (3 November 2026)

**Claim and exact scope:** Printed Chapter 2 named `thm:bertrand`: for **every integer n≥1**, a prime p exists with n<p≤2n; for n>1, automatically p<2n as 2n is composite. This contains two separate regimes, one finite (1≤n≤630) and one infinite (n≥631). First-use analysis: Chapter 2's independently double-checked `lem:binom` and `thm:cheb` are presented **before** the proof, Chapter 1 covers prime valuations and factorials, Volume 1 `ch05-sequences-series.tex` supplies logarithms, derivatives and `thm:mvt`; no later-volume result imported. The mathematical result is now `11/72 VERIFIED_TWICE` internally; 61 named results remain.

**Pass A: reconstruct the printed proof without skipping either regime.** For n=1, p=2 satisfies the statement, with upper equality p=2n. For 2≤n<631, take the ordered finite list
2,3,5,7,13,23,43,83,163,317,631.
Every listed integer is prime (trial division up to its square root), and every consecutive ratio is strictly less than 2. For n in this finite range, let q be the largest listed prime ≤n, and q' the next. It is guaranteed to exist even at n=630 because 631 follows 317. Since q≤n<q' and q'<2q≤2n, the prime q' lies in (n,2n]. For n=1 use the auxiliary q=1 and q'=2, so p=2. Thus this list covers **all 630 integers**, not merely the eleven examples. The manuscript now explains both the coverage and n=1 boundary (source `37495951fb28e767a2673131d487934443b5faf0`).

For n≥631 suppose no prime exists in (n,2n]. Put B=binom(2n,n). By `lem:binom`, p>sqrt(2n) divides B at most once; primes in (2n/3,n] do **not** divide B because n≥5; and none in (n,2n] divide B under the supposition. Thus all prime divisors of B lie ≤2n/3. Those p≤sqrt(2n) contribute prime powers p^{v_p(B)}≤2n, with fewer than sqrt(2n) such primes, giving total <(2n)^{sqrt(2n)}. All remaining primes sqrt(2n)<p≤2n/3 contribute at most p each; the full product is ≤∏_{p≤2n/3}p<4^{2n/3} by fully verified `thm:cheb`. Hence
4^n/(2n)≤B<(2n)^{sqrt(2n)}4^{2n/3}.
Taking logarithms yields
h(n):=(n/3)ln4−[1+sqrt(2n)]ln(2n)<0.
However at the *real* starting point 512,
h(512)=[1024/3−33·10]ln2=(34/3)ln2>0.
For all real n≥512, differentiate:
h'(n)=(2/3)ln2−ln(2n)/sqrt(2n)−[1+sqrt(2n)]/n.
The first variable term is decreasing because u↦ln(u)/sqrt(u) has derivative (1−(1/2)ln u)/u^{3/2}<0 for u≥1024>e². At n=512 it is (10ln2)/32<0.22. The other term 1/n+sqrt(2)/sqrt(n) also decreases and at512 equals 33/512<0.07. Since (2/3)ln2>0.46, h'(n)>0.46−0.22−0.07>0 for **every real n≥512**, not just sampled integers. Thus h(n)≥h(512)>0 throughout n≥631, contradicting the assumed prime gap. This covers all n≥1 with no extrapolation from finite checking.

**Pass B: independent prime-chain recheck and separate uniform analytic inequality.** Independently check each listed small prime by trial division ≤sqrt(p), and partition the integers 1≤n≤630 by consecutive prime-chain endpoints. For every q in the chain and all n with q≤n<q', q'<2q≤2n, which is an exact *interval argument*, not sampling. The observed 1 and 630 endpoints are independently included. For the large range, reconstruct the bound on B from the *Chapter 1 base-p carry theorem* rather than trusting the printed Legendre valuation proof: if p²>2n, only one carrying column can exist, so v_p(B)≤1; if 2n/3<p≤n, n=p+t with 0≤2t<p and p≥5, so n+n has no base-p carry and v_p(B)=0. For each small prime p≤sqrt(2n), carry positions j occur only at p^j≤2n, giving p^{v_p(B)}≤2n. Thus the prime-factor upper bound follows independently from digital arithmetic and the already double-checked `thm:cheb`; the ratio-induction proof of the central-binomial lower bound from DEP-019 gives 4^n/(2n)≤B. This reproduces the contradiction with a different method of establishing the crucial prime-power facts.

For the continuous argument, **do not differentiate h** as the printed proof does. Instead let t=sqrt(2n)≥32 and rewrite algebraically:
h(n)/n=(2/3)ln2−4[(ln t)/t+(ln t)/t²].
On t≥32, the derivative of ln t/t is (1−ln t)/t²<0, and the derivative of ln t/t² is (1−2ln t)/t³<0. Consequently the bracket **decreases** with t; h(n)/n increases with n≥512. But h(512)/512=(34ln2)/(1536)>0. So h(n)>0 for every n≥512 independently of h' and of the printed numerical 0.22/0.07 estimates. This alternative transparent route has been added to the book in commit `e44b3e97473dc35b28cc202ebadace22ec1dd34a`; it uses only Volume 1's proved differentiation and monotonicity.

**Adversarial exact computations:** new `tools/test_volume2_bertrand_bounds.py` added at commit `03b94fa463136be4e67e34799302f94064fef551` checks each of the 11 chain entries prime, every consecutive ratio <2, every **630** small n's explicit next-chain-prime witness, the theorem's prime-gap conclusion independently for n=1…12000 by sieve, exact formula h(512)=(34/3)ln2, and sampled strict h(n)>0/h'(n)>0/independent h(n)/n positivity at n=512…20000. Checks n=512, 513, 630, 631, 632, 1000, 4096, 5000 and 10000 against the hypothetical-absence h(n)<0 contradiction. These probes are finite cross-checks; the symbolic monotonicity reasoning covers the infinite range. The source was edited at `37495951fb28e767a2673131d487934443b5faf0` to clarify all small-case coverage, equality at n=1 and why one numerical h(512) check is insufficient; at `e44b3e97473dc35b28cc202ebadace22ec1dd34a` to supply the independent analytic proof. Named-result register update `2ac13ded25a804e43c29cd613b7fe2b6fbbb4363` marks `thm:bertrand` VERIFIED_TWICE.

**Next task and boundaries:** The named Chapter 2 result inventory (five entries: Euclid primes, Euler harmonic divergence, binomial lemma, Chebyshev bounds and Bertrand's postulate) now has **5/5 internal two-proof mathematical checks**. The **full Chapter 2** is not signed off: its eleven keyed exercises, many unnamed arguments, plot data and figure render/pagination remain subject to the same strict standard. Overall **11/72 named results, 29/144 exercise solutions verified twice**, **61 named and 115 exercises unchecked**, plus every unnamed proof and every P00–P13, editorial/first-use, rendered/PDF/EPUB/print, external reviewer/KDP gate remains OPEN. On the next turn, check exact-head CI and inspect Chapter 2 exercise/hint/solution triplets in **printed order**, starting first UNREVIEWED `ex:mod4`, `ex:mersenne`, `ex:germain`, `ex:fermatnum` (read actual sources and register before acting). Continue genuine independent proofs and reader rewrite, not checklist-only edits; keep PR #103 DRAFT/unmerged.


## DEP-022 — independent solution audits for first four Chapter 2 exercises (3 November 2026)

**Scope and printed order:** `volume2-integers/chapters/ch02-primes.tex`, `ex:mod4`, `ex:mersenne`, `ex:germain` (all ★), `ex:fermatnum` (★★). Each question, hint and **full solution** was read; distinct general mathematical passes are provided below, with checking of n=0/1 boundary cases, prime vs composite factor claims, first-use order and separate finite arithmetic corroboration. All necessary tools were actually proved in Volume 1 or earlier printed Volume 2 Chapters 1–2: FTA, integer division, Euclid, elementary parity and finite products. The `\\pmod` notation is already used in Volume 1 `ch01-sums.tex` (Fermat exercise) and this Volume 2 Chapter 1 `ex:n2n` well before Chapter 2; its meaning is ordinary equality of remainders, no later CRT or cyclic-unit-group result required.

### `ex:mod4`: infinitely many primes congruent to 3 modulo 4

**Pass A (printed Euclid construction).** Assume a complete finite list p1,…,pk of primes with remainder 3 modulo 4. Let P=∏p_i (empty P=1) and N=4P−1. Then N≥3, N≡3 mod4, and for every listed p_i one has N≡−1 mod p_i so p_i∤N. By FTA, N has prime factors. None equals2 (N odd), so every factor is 1 or 3 modulo4. If all prime factors were1 mod4 their product would be1 mod4, contradicting N≡3; hence at least one q≡3 mod4 divides N. This q is not on the list, a contradiction. Exponents in prime factorisation may repeat; their exact count is irrelevant for existence, though a product with remainder3 has an *odd* number of 3-reminder factors counted with multiplicity. Works for k=0.

**Pass B (separate explicit construction).** Let P be the product of a hypothetical finite list of **all** 3-mod-4 primes, so P is odd, including P=1 for the empty list. Instead take N=2P+1, which is 3 modulo4 for EVERY odd P, and gcd(N,P)=gcd(2P+1,P)=1. Factor N into primes; since it is odd and 3 modulo4, some prime factor q is3 modulo4, but no listed p divides N. Thus the finite list was incomplete. This bypasses the exact 4P−1 construction and independently reconstructs the residue-factor obstruction, without importing Dirichlet's arithmetic-progression theorem. Finite exact arithmetic independently factored both constructions for the first 0…8 initial 3-mod-4 primes (18 cases); each produced a missing prime q≡3 mod4. The manuscript adds the small check 4·3·7·11−1=923=13·71, with 71≡3 mod4, from source edit `86c3508dcb243d5d0167c030079d7b56b6c0c29e`. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:mersenne`: necessary exponent shapes for 2^n−1 and 2^n+1

**Pass A (printed factorisation).** If 2^n−1 is prime and n=ab composite with a,b>1, then 2^n−1=(2^a−1)(1+2^a+⋯+2^{a(b−1)}). Both factors are >1, a contradiction. For 2^n+1, if n is not a power of2, write n=km with an odd m>1. The elementary polynomial identity X^m+1=(X+1)(X^{m−1}−X^{m−2}+⋯−X+1) for odd m shows 2^k+1 is a proper divisor of 2^n+1; contradiction. Thus n must have no odd factor, equivalently n=2^a with a≥0. The hypothesis on the plus sign includes n=1, where 2^1+1=3 prime and 1=2^0. For n=1 the minus expression is1, not prime, so its implication is vacuous. Both statements are one-way only.

**Pass B (separate modular divisibility).** For a composite n choose a proper factor a≥2 of n; modulo D=2^a−1, one has 2^a≡1, so 2^n=(2^a)^{n/a}≡1 and D divides 2^n−1. With 1<D<2^n−1, composite. Independently for n not a power of2, let d=2^{v_2(n)} (the largest power of2 dividing n), and n/d=m odd with m≥3. Modulo D=2^d+1, 2^d≡−1, so 2^n=(2^d)^m≡−1 and D divides 2^n+1. Since d<n, this is a proper divisor. This modular argument uses only integer division and does not presuppose polynomial cyclotomy or roots of unity. Independently tested 110 integer exponents with every composite/odd-part obstruction. Reader-facing counters to the **false converses** are printed: 2^11−1=2047=23·89 although11 prime, and 2^32+1=4294967297=641·6700417 although32 is a power of2; both products separately recomputed. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:germain`: Sophie Germain's factorisation for n>1

**Pass A (printed squares).** The identity
n^4+4=(n²+2)²−(2n)²=(n²−2n+2)(n²+2n+2)
holds by direct expansion. When n≥2, the first factor is (n−1)²+1≥2, the second is larger, so both are integers >1 and <n^4+4. Thus composite. The strict range matters: n=1 gives 5, prime.

**Pass B (independent modular certification of a nontrivial divisor).** Let d=n²−2n+2=(n−1)²+1. For n≥2, 1<d<n^4+4. Modulo d, n²≡2n−2, hence n^4≡(2n−2)²=4n²−8n+4≡4(2n−2)−8n+4≡−4. Thus d divides n^4+4 without first knowing its full factorisation or relying on a difference-of-squares identity. This provides a separate algebraic proof of non-primality. Exact integer checks cover all n=2,…,1000, verifying both the factor identity and the modular implication. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:fermatnum`: pairwise coprime Fermat numbers and new primes

**Pass A (inductive difference-of-squares).** For F_n=2^{2^n}+1, show ∏_{j=0}^{n−1}F_j=F_n−2=2^{2^n}−1 for **all n≥0**. At n=0 both sides equal1 (empty product), which the original question left unstated and the manuscript now makes literal; at n=1, F0=3=F1−2. If it holds at n, multiplying by F_n=(2^{2^n}+1) gives
(2^{2^n}−1)(2^{2^n}+1)=2^{2^{n+1}}−1=F_(n+1)−2.
For 0≤m<n, any common divisor d of F_m and F_n divides the product of earlier Fermat numbers F_n−2 and also F_n, hence d|2. But every F_k is **odd**, so d=1. Thus the Fermat numbers are pairwise coprime. Each F_k>1 has a prime divisor by FTA; pairwise coprimality forces infinitely many distinct such primes. No assumption that F_k themselves are prime is made.

**Pass B (independent polynomial telescoping and direct congruences).** For every x and n≥0,
(x−1)∏_{j=0}^{n−1}(x^{2^j}+1)=x^{2^n}−1
is a finite polynomial identity proved by repeated difference of squares; at x=2 the first factor x−1=1, giving the requested product equality without an induction on individual F_n. More directly, for n>m≥0, take modulus F_m=2^{2^m}+1: the quantity x=2^{2^m} is congruent to −1. Then
F_n=x^{2^{n−m}}+1≡(−1)^{2^{n−m}}+1=2 mod F_m,
because 2^{n−m} is even. Therefore gcd(F_m,F_n)=gcd(F_m,2)=1 since F_m is odd; this proves pairwise coprimality without using the preceding-product formula at all. Each contributes a prime absent from every other, yielding the required infinitude. Exact product identities were independently checked for all n=0,…,12 (up to 2^4096+1), with all 78 pairwise gcds and direct remainder F_n mod F_m=2. The n=0 empty case is now explicit in the question/solution at source commit `86c3508dcb243d5d0167c030079d7b56b6c0c29e`. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

**Tests, sources and incomplete gates:** Focused stdlib test `tools/test_volume2_ch02_four_exercises.py` committed at `65fc8fd49e0bc1bc1d73007e64feded2789419a8`; it structurally checks each question/hint/solution plus full Chapter 2 11/11/11 count, exact prime-prefix Euclid examples, Mersenne obstructions, Germain divisor and modular proof and Fermat recurrence/pairwise gcds. These samples are additional evidence; both general symbolic proofs above are required for each `VERIFIED_TWICE`. Exercise register update `15b403e409c11ca79e18df7e6e19c32f1aaaff5c` marks the four rows. **Overall 11/72 named results, 33/144 exercise solutions checked twice internally; 61 named, 111 exercises and all unnamed arguments remain unverified.** Printed Chapter 2 has 7 more unchecked exercises (`ex:gaps`, `ex:factorial`, `ex:erdosdiv`, `ex:furstenberg`, `ex:t1-factorial-new-prime`, `ex:t1-euclid-sequence`, `ex:t1-prod11`). Keep PR DRAFT/unmerged.


## DEP-023 — remaining seven printed Chapter 2 exercises, two independent checks each (3 November 2026)

**Scope:** `ex:gaps`, `ex:factorial`, `ex:erdosdiv`, `ex:furstenberg`, `ex:t1-factorial-new-prime`, `ex:t1-euclid-sequence`, and `ex:t1-prod11`, all eleven Chapter 2 exercises now independently audited twice. The full source questions/hints/solutions were examined, not just exercise labels. Inputs: Volume 1 finite combinatorics, factorials and prime divisors, prior Chapter 1 FTA/Legendre/Bézout/valuation proofs, and completed Chapter 2 Bertrand/Chebyshev where exercises explicitly call them *optional*. No Chapter 3 CRT or advanced topology is imported into the first-reading route.

**ex:gaps Pass A.** Given any length L≥1, set M=(L+1)!. For j=2,...,L+1, j|M, hence j|(M+j), while 1<j<M+j. Thus the L consecutive integers M+2,...,M+L+1 are all composite. Example L=3: M=24 yields 26,27,28 (inserted into reader-facing proof in commit `90b2d478676509a2a910672daf53ab325dec2c0c`). Bertrand gives a prime in (x,2x] (length x), *not* within any fixed number L of steps. Starting x=M+1, the L-number run ends at M+L+1<2(M+1), so a Bertrand prime can occur after the run; no contradiction. **Pass B, independent construction using earlier Bézout (fully spelled out, CRT not assumed):** choose L distinct primes q_1,...,q_L all bigger than L, e.g. by iterating earlier Euclid's theorem. Let Q=∏q_i, Q_i=Q/q_i. Since gcd(Q_i,q_i)=1, Bézout supplies an inverse u_i of Q_i mod q_i. The integer X_0=Σ_{i=1}^L (-i)u_i Q_i then satisfies X_0+i≡0 mod q_i for every i: mod q_j every term with i≠j vanishes; the remaining term is −j. Replace X_0 by X=X_0+tQ for a sufficiently large positive integer t so that X+i>q_i>1 for all i. The consecutive numbers X+1,...,X+L each have a proper divisor q_i, proving arbitrary composite runs **without** using factorials. This is a finite elementary construction from Chapter 1 Bézout, not an unproved appeal to the later printed CRT. Integer tests n=1…40 for the factorial construction plus sample first-use L=3. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**ex:factorial Pass A.** For integer n≥2 put m=floor(n/2)≥1. Earlier `thm:bertrand` gives a prime p with m<p≤2m≤n. Since p≥m+1>n/2 (also when n is odd), one has n<2p: among 1,...,n the **only** multiple of p is p. Thus v_p(n!)=floor(n/p)=1; all higher powers p²>n do not contribute. If n!=a^k with integer k≥2, FTA would force every prime valuation of n! divisible by k; 1 is not, contradiction. Edge n=2: m=1,p=2 works. **Pass B.** Rather than invoking Legendre, write n!=p·(∏_{1≤j≤n,j≠p}j). Because p>n/2 and no j other than p is a multiple of p, the second factor is **coprime to p**; hence n! is divisible by p but not p². If n!=a^k and p|a^k, FTA (or Gauss cancellation repeatedly) gives p|a and therefore p²|a^k for k≥2, impossible. This establishes the non-perfect-power claim by an independent direct divisibility route. Finite test checks n=2…500 by explicit factorials and prime witnesses. A reader example 5!=120 has p=3 once. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**ex:erdosdiv Pass A, all subparts and strict inequality.** Suppose Σ_p1/p converges; then choose k≥0 with tail Σ_{j>k}1/p_j<1/2. For any integer N≥1 and prime p, the multiples ≤N number floor(N/p)≤N/p; primes p>N contribute **zero**, making the union finite for every given N. Union bound gives **strictly fewer** than N/2 integers having any prime divisor outside p_1,...,p_k. Every remaining integer n admits the *unique* decomposition n=a b² from the parity of its prime exponents, a squarefree. If all prime factors belong to the first k, a is a product of some subset of them (including the empty subset a=1 for n=1), at most 2^k choices. Also b≤sqrt(N), giving at most 2^k floor(sqrt N)≤2^k sqrt N numbers. Therefore N<N/2+2^k sqrt N and hence sqrt N<2^{k+1}. But choosing N=2^{2k+2} forces equality sqrt N=2^{k+1}, contradiction. The exact suggested N is valid for every k≥0.

**Pass B independent smooth-integer growth counting.** Fix a hypothetical finite k and count n≤N with all prime factors in p_1,...,p_k directly from exponent vectors (e_1,...,e_k): for each i, 0≤e_i≤log_2N, since p_i≥2 and p_i^{e_i}≤N. By FTA, the count is bounded by (1+floor(log_2N))^k, even if distinct exponent vectors occasionally produce integers>N (that only overcounts). Elementary Volume 1 growth estimates give (1+log_2N)^k=o(N) for each *fixed* k. The union bound above, under a convergent reciprocal-prime sum, says at least N/2 of the integers are smooth, which is impossible for sufficiently large N. This proves divergence using a genuinely different count from squarefree decomposition. For the exercise's specific (b) bound, independently view every n as a subset S of its odd-exponent prime factors and an integer square root b; for each of 2^k subsets S and each b≤sqrt N there is at most one such n. A finite standard-library test independently enumerated smooth counts and squarefree decompositions for k=0…6 and N=16,100,333,1000,4000; finite probes do not prove divergence. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**ex:furstenberg Pass A, all topology axioms including b=1.** An open U is any union of progressions a+bZ with b≥1. Empty union gives ∅, and 0+1Z=Z gives the whole set. Arbitrary unions are open by definition. If x∈U∩V, there exist progressions x+bZ⊂U and x+cZ⊂V; then x+bcZ⊂U∩V. Taking union over all x in U∩V proves finite intersections are open (and the empty finite intersection is Z). Every nonempty open set contains one infinite progression x+bZ. The complement of a+bZ is precisely ∪_{j=1}^{b−1}(a+j+bZ), open; for b=1 this is the **empty union**, correctly handling that case. Thus every progression is closed. Every integer except ±1, including 0, belongs to pZ for some prime p: FTA for |n|≥2, and 0 is divisible by every prime. Therefore Z\{−1,1}=∪_p pZ. If only finitely many primes existed, the union would be **finite and closed**, making {−1,1} open, contradicting that nonempty open sets are infinite. This locally proves the basic topology axioms, without presuming an advanced course.

**Pass B, independent residue-base formulation.** Instead of using product bc, note x+bZ∩x+cZ=x+lcm(b,c)Z by the elementary divisibility rule (proved from gcd/FTA in Chapter 1), so the progressions themselves form a basis stable under intersection whenever the intersection is nonempty. Every union of them has the topology axioms, and none is finite. The finite complement of any progression splits into distinct residue classes, each a basis set, so progressions are clopen. If primes were finite, the set V=∪_p pZ would be clopen (finite union of clopen progressions), while its complementary nonempty {−1,1} would also have to contain a progression, impossible because every progression is infinite. The same global contradiction is reconstructed using basis/algebra of residue classes rather than the pointwise-intersection and closed-set arguments. Source now explicitly **defines topology before the reader is asked to prove its axioms** (commit `90b2d478676509a2a910672daf53ab325dec2c0c`). Exact residue/complement and lcm/product intersection checks across 18 moduli validate the elementary arithmetic boundaries. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**ex:t1-factorial-new-prime Pass A:** If p≤n prime divided n!+1, then it divides n!, their difference 1, impossible. Thus all prime factors of n!+1 are >n. The question does **not** imply n!+1 itself is prime: for n=5, 121=11², 11>5. **Pass B:** Modulo any prime p≤n, one of the factors 1,…,n equals p and n!≡0, so n!+1≡1 mod p; no such p can divide it. This is a direct uniform residue calculation, independent of a hypothetical common-divisor contradiction. The factorisation 121=11² is direct, no primality inference. Tested all p≤n for 2≤n≤150. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**ex:t1-euclid-sequence Pass A:** E_1=2, E_(r+1)=∏_{i=1}^r E_i+1. Directly E_2=3, E_3=7, E_4=43, E_5=1807=13·139, composite. If i<j, E_j≡1 mod E_i because E_i divides E_j−1. Thus gcd(E_i,E_j)=1, and FTA supplies a new prime from every E_j>1, proving infinitely many distinct primes. **Pass B:** Put P_r=∏_{i=1}^r E_i, so P_(r+1)=P_r(P_r+1), with gcd(P_r,P_r+1)=1 for all r≥1 (consecutive integers). Hence E_(r+1)=P_r+1 is coprime to the **entire product** of preceding E_i, an inductive global gcd statement; any prime from E_(r+1) is absent from all prior terms. This gives the same infinitude without separately reducing all pairs. Exact recursion and all pairwise gcds through E_8 checked; no false converse “all terms prime” claimed. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**ex:t1-prod11 Pass A:** The five primes p≤11 are exactly 2,3,5,7,11, product 2310, and 4^11=4,194,304. Thus 2310<4^11. By natural logarithm product law, Σ_{p≤11}ln p=ln2310≈7.7450028035, while 11ln4≈15.2492379723, so strict inequality holds. **Pass B:** Independently group (2·3·5)(7·11)=30·77=2310, and sum individual logs ln2+ln3+ln5+ln7+ln11. To compare without rounding, note 2310<4^6=4096<4^11, then apply strictly increasing ln. This checks exact arithmetic and strict comparisons without relying on approximate decimals. A finite code check confirms both exact and decimal values. This is expressly **a numerical exercise, not a new proof** of Chebyshev's universal estimate. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**Tests and scope:** `tools/test_volume2_ch02_remaining_exercises.py`, new at commit `98636d035cc55e5751ec360b49dfea756595bde5`, probes every above elementary boundary, source label/hint/solution triplet and exact Chapter 2 11/11/11 structural inventory; its arithmetic was independently recomputed in a private Python session, including factorial divisors through n=500 and smooth counts for k=0…6. Register updated at `cc92c47775419661181e49ddb8ca972e873cbe9e`. Exactly **all 11 printed Chapter 2 exercise solutions** now have two independent internal symbolic checks, but the chapter still has **unnamed argument and figure/format gates** open. Do not call the entire chapter certified.

## DEP-024 — four Chapter 3 named results: quotient ring, units, Euler, Wilson (3 November 2026)

**Scope:** `volume2-integers/chapters/ch03-congruences.tex`, first four named results in printed order: `prop:ringZn`, `prop:units`, `thm:eulerthm`, `thm:wilson`. Quantified n≥1 and special zero-ring n=1 explicitly confronted. The only earlier results imported are integer division, Bézout, Gauss cancellation/FTA and finite permutations from Chapters 1–2 and Volume 1; for Wilson's alternate route, Volume 1's `volume1-first-principles/chapters/ch03-polynomials.tex` `prop:rootsfactor` proves polynomial root-factor and root-counting over a field *before* Chapter 3. The printed Chapter 3 **defines rings, fields and units before use**, and source edits here now define Euler's φ(n) before its first named proposition.

**prop:ringZn Pass A, printed proof.** For integer n≥1, if a−a' and b−b' are multiples of n then (a+b)−(a'+b')=(a−a')+(b−b') is also, and ab−a'b'=a(b−b')+b'(a−a') likewise. Thus the classes of sum and product do not depend on representatives. Integer addition and multiplication laws descend, identities are classes of0 and1, additive inverses −a, and Euclidean division supplies exactly n residue classes 0,…,n−1. The n=1 ring has one element 0=1, allowed by the **ring** definition (but excluded from the definition of **field**). **Pass B, independent canonical-remainder construction:** Define r_n(a) as the unique integer 0≤r<n congruent to a (Euclidean division). On R={0,…,n−1}, define x⊕y=r_n(x+y), x⊗y=r_n(xy). The elementary property r_n(r_n(a)+b)=r_n(a+b) and its product analogue follow because r_n(a)−a is a multiple of n; these prove associativity and distributivity of ⊕,⊗ by lifting and re-reducing integer identities. The additive zero and multiplicative one are r_n(0) and r_n(1), with additive inverse r_n(−x). There are exactly n elements by construction, including n=1 with both identities zero. The bijection x↦x+nZ identifies these canonical operations with the stated quotient operation, establishing representative-independence from a separate path. Exhaustive representative and triple-law tests for n≤50. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**prop:units Pass A:** A class a has multiplicative inverse iff ∃u: au≡1 modn, equivalently integers u,v solve au+nv=1. By previously checked Bézout this occurs iff gcd(a,n)=1. The number of such residues is the now explicitly **prior-defined** Euler totient φ(n) (the classes 1,…,n with gcd=1). For n≥2, all nonzero classes are invertible iff n is prime: p prime gives gcd(a,p)=1 for 1≤a<p, whereas composite n has a nontrivial divisor d with gcd(d,n)>1, noninvertible. For n=1, 0=1, so this one-element ring is **not** a field (definition demands 0≠1); its sole element is simultaneously unit/identity, giving φ(1)=1. Printed proof now explicitly handles this edge (commit `2aaa846487ba3c91b6ee4443dac74c1e3e844968`).

**Pass B, independent permutation and gcd obstruction:** Suppose gcd(a,n)=1. If ax≡ay modn, then n|a(x−y), and Gauss' lemma from Chapter 1 yields n|(x−y). Thus multiplication by a is **injective** on the finite n-element residue set, hence surjective, so some x has ax≡1 modn. This establishes invertibility without using the Bézout inverse formula. Conversely if d=gcd(a,n)>1, then d|a and d|n; any ax congruent to1 modn would force d|1, absurd. For n=1 the finite set has one element and map is bijective. The field criterion and φ count follow by direct enumeration. Independent modular witnesses/field counts checked for all n≤80 and all representatives. The earlier clock caption was clarified: repeated addition by4 stays within {0,4,8} **only starting from0**, not from arbitrary residues (commit `2aaa846`). VERIFIED_TWICE.

**thm:eulerthm Pass A, printed unit-product proof.** Now with **explicit hypotheses n≥1, a∈Z, gcd(a,n)=1** and φ(n) already defined, multiplication by the unit a permutes all φ(n) units, since its inverse exists. Let U be their product in the commutative residue ring; U is itself a unit. On the one hand the product of the permuted list equals U, on the other it equals a^{φ(n)}U. Cancel the *invertible* U to obtain a^{φ(n)}≡1 modn. For prime p, φ(p)=p−1 and the same statement is Fermat's theorem. At n=1, φ(1)=1 and the unique residue0=1 makes the congruence automatic; at p=2, the exponent p−1=1, so the claim is consistent. The original theorem had an unqualified n in a chapter defining moduli n≥1; explicit range and edge explanation inserted in `5d130d095ab07fe860233dcdae25a0a5d99503f9`.

**Pass B, independent elementary orbit partition (Lagrange proved locally, not imported).** Let G be the φ(n)-element group of units and g=a modn. By finiteness and cancellation there is a least positive h with g^h=1 (powers must repeat; cancelling gives an identity return). For any unit x, the h elements x,gx,…,g^{h−1}x are distinct and form one cycle under multiplication by g: if g^ix=g^jx then g^{i−j}=1 and h divides i−j. Different cycles are disjoint; they partition G into blocks of size h. Consequently h divides |G|=φ(n). Thus g^{φ(n)}=(g^h)^{φ(n)/h}=1. No product of all units, and no unproved general group Lagrange theorem, is needed. For n=1, h=φ(1)=1 and the singleton cycle works. An exact test checked Euler congruence and order divisibility for every n≤400 and all a with gcd(a,n)=1. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**thm:wilson Pass A, printed inverse pairs.** For p=2, 1!=1≡−1 mod2. For odd prime p, all 1,…,p−1 are units. Inversion pairs each x with x^{-1}, product1; fixed points solve x=x^{-1}, i.e. x²=1. Since F_p is a field, (x−1)(x+1)=0 implies x=1 or −1, distinct for odd p. Every remaining class belongs to a two-element inverse pair. Product of all nonzero residues is therefore (1)(−1)=−1, proving (p−1)!≡−1 modp.

**Pass B, independent polynomial coefficient method.** Volume 1 `prop:rootsfactor` already proved: over any field, a degree-d polynomial has at most d roots, and if it has d distinct roots, the monic polynomial is the product of their linear factors. In F_p, the just-verified Euler/Fermat theorem gives a^{p−1}=1 for each nonzero a. Thus the monic degree-(p−1) polynomial X^{p−1}−1 has the p−1 distinct roots 1,…,p−1 and satisfies the exact polynomial identity
X^{p−1}−1=∏_{a=1}^{p−1}(X−a) over F_p.
Comparing constants yields −1=(−1)^{p−1}(p−1)!. For odd p, p−1 is even, so (p−1)!=−1. For p=2, direct 1!=1≡−1 mod2 covers the exceptional sign. This proof uses a polynomial *root-count theorem from earlier Volume 1* and Fermat from earlier Chapter 3, not inverse pairing. Exact p≤211 factorial tests and coefficientwise polynomial identity for all primes p≤31 corroborated independently. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**Reader and test evidence:** New `tools/test_volume2_ch03_first_four_results.py` at `2270de92a41eb328b2ea18534e25aab839678136` with bounded-witness runtime correction `a85f147cd262b55b18d7b6b66ed65fdf95d92424`. Book edits: totient defined before named proposition, zero ring n=1 and φ(1) made explicit, clock trajectory clarified (`2aaa846`); Euler theorem quantifier n≥1 and trivial residue case explicitly stated (`5d130d0`). The named register at `d60b9d29be550fac5d291a41c827834695484ae8` advances to 15/72. **No actual rendered figure/page check is implied.**

**Strict remainder after these two checkpoints:** **15/72 named results and 40/144 exercise solutions VERIFIED_TWICE internally**; **57 named results, 104 exercise solutions, all unnamed proofs/examples and all first-use/whole-book narrative, printed figures, PDF/EPUB/print/KDP/external independent reviewer and P00–P13 gates remain OPEN**. The full exact-head **Build books #1593 SUCCEEDED** on its then-current head `44b8dc3678e8f37d04de2e88c2450518e8d9e221` (list, deps, Volume 2 book build and site all success; publish skipped). ## DEP-025 — remaining four Chapter 3 named results, independently reconstructed (3 November 2026)

**Scope:** `thm:crt`, `prop:lincong`, `prop:phisum`, `thm:hensel` in printed Chapter 3 `volume2-integers/chapters/ch03-congruences.tex`, following `prop:ringZn`, `prop:units`, Euler and Wilson (DEP-024). Every theorem has two general mathematical passes and independent boundary checks. Consequently **all 8 named Chapter 3 results** are now VERIFIED_TWICE internally; **this does not certify Chapter 3's 21 exercises, its examples or its figures/page layout**. All inputs (Bézout, Gauss/FTA, finite permutations, integer binomial theorem and elementary polynomials) appear in Volume 1 or Chapters 1–3 **before** their first use; formal polynomial derivative is explicitly *defined and derived* in the paragraph before Hensel. No unproved general CRT from a later volume is imported.

**`thm:crt` Pass A (printed canonical-map argument).** For coprime integers m,n≥1, consider F:Z/mnZ→Z/mZ×Z/nZ sending x to its two residue classes. It is well-defined since mn|x−y implies m|x−y and n|x−y, and it respects addition, multiplication and the class1. If F(x)=F(y), then m|(x−y), n|(x−y), so by Gauss' lemma and gcd(m,n)=1, mn|(x−y); thus F is injective. Both finite sets have mn elements (including cases one modulus1), so it is bijective. Given mu+nv=1 by Bézout, the integer x=a·nv+b·mu has x≡a modm and x≡b modn. The signs are correct because nv≡1 modm, mu≡1 modn, and each other projection vanishes. This explicit formula describes the unique class modulo mn; the source now states explicitly that a modulus equal to1 makes its condition vacuous (commit `ff459e97804ec9fcd90e6426a125b0b490939927`).

**Pass B, independent affine constructive solution.** Given a,b, write x=a+mt. The condition x≡b modn becomes mt≡b−a modn. Since gcd(m,n)=1, Chapter 3's **previously proved unit criterion** gives an inverse w of m modulo n. Thus t≡w(b−a) modn is the **unique** solution for t modulo n. Replacing t by t+kn changes x by kmn, and conversely any x satisfying the first congruence is a+mt. Thus there is exactly one x class modulo mn solving both equations. This is a constructive proof of existence/uniqueness which does not invoke finite-set cardinality or the printed CRT injection. The map F is a ring morphism from direct congruence preservation, so bijectivity makes it an isomorphism. If n=1 the t-class has one element and x≡a modm; if m=1 the first condition is empty, x≡b modn; if m=n=1 all rings are singleton. Distinct moduli with gcd>1 cannot be claimed to have unique arbitrary solutions; e.g. x≡0 mod2, x≡1 mod4 is impossible. The new test checks all residue pairs for 1≤m,n≤34 with gcd=1 against both the explicit Bézout formula and direct inverse route (>20,000 pairs). VERIFIED_TWICE.

**`prop:lincong` Pass A (printed ideal-sum/Bezout argument).** For integers a,b and positive n, ax≡b modn exactly means ax−ny=b for some integers x,y, so b∈aZ+nZ=dZ where d=gcd(a,n). This proves necessity/sufficiency d|b by earlier Bézout. If d|b, write a=d a', n=d n', b=d b'; then gcd(a',n')=1, so a'x≡b' modn' has a unique solution class x0 modn'. Exactly d residues modulo n lift that class, represented by x0+j n', 0≤j≤d−1. They are distinct because (j−j')n' is divisible by n=d n' only when j≡j' mod d. The source previously said only “it gives d classes”; commit `ff459e9` now lists them and explains distinctness. If a=0, d=n: when n|b all n classes are solutions, otherwise none; if n=1, d=1 and its unique class is always a solution, consistent even for a=0 and negative b.

**Pass B, independent fiber-period argument.** Necessity is immediate because d|a and d|n imply d divides every ax−ny. For sufficiency use the *separate previously-proved unit permutation property*: gcd(a',n')=1 implies multiplication by a' permutes Z/n'Z, hence hits the residue b'; choose x0. The map x(modn)↦x(modn') has fibers of size d (explicitly x0+j n' for j=0…d−1), while the condition a'x≡b' modn' is constant on each such fiber. Thus its inverse image contains exactly d classes of the n-element set. This uses finite permutation/fiber cardinality rather than relying on the subgroup equality aZ+nZ=dZ. Tested negative a,b, n=1, a=0, all residue classes for >60,000 (n,a,b) tuples, with exact d-solutions counts and explicit periodic lifts. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**`prop:phisum` Pass A (printed reduced-fraction bijection).** For n≥1 consider n fractions k/n, k=1,…,n. Writing k/n=a/d in lowest terms gives d|n, 1≤a≤d, gcd(a,d)=1. Conversely for every divisor d|n and each such a, let k=a(n/d). Then 1≤k≤n and k/n=a/d; distinct reduced pairs (a,d) give distinct rational numbers k/n and hence distinct k. Thus the n fractions partition into disjoint classes indexed by d, with φ(d) choices each. Hence Σ_{d|n}φ(d)=n. The n=1 case gives φ(1)=1; concrete n=6 sum 1+1+2+2=6 now appears in source (commit `ff459e9`).

**Pass B, independent gcd-fiber partition.** Partition integers k∈{1,…,n} by g=gcd(k,n). Each g|n and k=g j with 1≤j≤n/g and gcd(j,n/g)=1 (because gcd(gj,gm)=g exactly when gcd(j,m)=1). Conversely each divisor g of n and each such j produces exactly one integer k with gcd(k,n)=g. Therefore the fiber for g has φ(n/g) members. Summing over g|n yields n=Σ_{g|n}φ(n/g)=Σ_{d|n}φ(d), by the bijection d=n/g among divisors. This count does not invoke reduced rational fractions; verified each gcd-fiber size and complete sum for all n≤1000. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**`thm:hensel` Pass A (printed elementary Taylor).** Let f∈Z[X], prime p, k≥1, a∈Z with f(a)=cp^k and f'(a) not divisible by p. The chapter *defines* f'(X)=Σ j c_j X^{j−1} and derives the **exact integer-polynomial** identity f(a+h)=f(a)+h f'(a)+h²g(a,h) by binomial expansion before naming the theorem. For h=tp^k and k≥1, h² is divisible by p^{2k}, and 2k≥k+1. Modulo p^{k+1}, the root requirement reduces to c+t f'(a)≡0 modp. Since f'(a) is invertible modp by earlier unit criterion, this has exactly one residue class t modp, represented by a unique digit t∈{0,…,p−1}. The existing root class a modp^k has exactly one lift in that congruence class to the next power, and induction can repeat since f'(a+tp^k)≡f'(a)≠0 modp.

**Pass B, independent monomial-and-difference uniqueness proof.** Expand each monomial in f directly:
(a+tp^k)^j=a^j+j a^{j−1} t p^k + Σ_{ℓ≥2}binom(j,ℓ) a^{j−ℓ} t^ℓ p^{kℓ}.
Every ℓ≥2 term contains p^{2k} and therefore vanishes modulo p^{k+1}. Summing gives f(a+tp^k)/p^k ≡ f(a)/p^k+t f'(a) modp; the quotient on the left is an integer because f(a) is divisible by p^k and the other terms all contain p^k. The map t↦c+t f'(a) is a bijection on F_p (slope a unit), so **existence** follows. To check **uniqueness independently**, suppose t,s both lift a: subtraction gives 0≡(t−s)p^k f'(a) mod p^{k+1}. Cancelling p^k as an integer divisibility statement yields p|(t−s)f'(a), and Gauss' lemma gives p|(t−s). Two digits 0≤t,s≤p−1 with difference divisible by p are equal. The p=2 case is included; derivative nonzero modp is indispensable. Explicit singular countercases printed in the book remain correct: f(X)=X² at a=0 mod5 has all five lifts 0,5,10,15,20 modulo25, whereas f(X)=X²−5 at a=0 mod5 has no lift modulo25. All six tested prime moduli 2,3,5,7,11,13, k=1…3 and four polynomial families independently generate unique lifts whenever root and derivative hypotheses hold. No p-adic completeness/new analytic machinery is assumed. VERIFIED_TWICE.

**Source and regression evidence:** Book source `ff459e97804ec9fcd90e6426a125b0b490939927` expands modular-one boundary, exact linear-congruence lift list (including a=0), and the totient example n=6. Focused standard-library tests `tools/test_volume2_ch03_crt_hensel.py` introduced at `1785eee7abfa56809d729c38904cda0c31f00966`; they check both CRT constructions, negative and zero coefficients, divisor-fiber counts and Hensel uniqueness/singular counterexamples plus 21/21/21 chapter exercise/hint/solution structure. The **entire named Chapter 3 result set, eight entries, is now independently verified twice internally**, not the chapter's unnamed claims or exercises. The current-head CI remains pending; do not transfer the green status of #1593 to later commits.

## DEP-026 — twelve printed Chapter 3 exercises independently checked (4 November 2026; reconciled from already-committed evidence)

**Evidence correction, not a second count.** The per-exercise register and tools/test_volume2_ch03_twelve_exercises.py already recorded 12 Chapter 3 exercises VERIFIED_TWICE, with explicit mathematical Pass A and Pass B, but this main ledger stopped at DEP-025 and incorrectly still said 40/144. The evidence has now been independently revisited against the current manuscript, including question, hint and full solution and the earlier Chapter 1–3 theorem prerequisites. The original 40 and 52 values describe different checkpoints; **before the new DEP-027 batch the honest total is 19/72 named results and 52/144 exercises**, not 40.

- **ex:last2:** A uses 7^4=1 mod 100 and exponent 2026=4·506+2; B computes separately mod 4 and mod 25, uses CRT to get 49. The power has no zero-exponent ambiguity.
- **ex:sunzi:** A solves x=8+15t with t=1 mod 7; B combines x=2 mod 21 then solves the remaining mod 5 condition; both give x=23 mod 105, and all three original residues are checked.
- **ex:digits:** A substitutes 10=1 mod 9 and 10=−1 mod 11 into the decimal polynomial; B proves divisibility of 10^j−1 by 9 and 10^j−(−1)^j by 11 and extends by additive inverses to signed integers. Zero and −12 are explicit edge cases.
- **ex:sqrt1:** A uses coprimality of x−1 and x+1 at odd p, prime-power cancellation and a CRT sign choice for each odd prime divisor; B uses v_p(x−1)+v_p(x+1)≥e with one summand zero, then counts independent signs. At modulus 1 the empty product has one root class.
- **ex:wilsonconv:** A splits composite n>4 into distinct factors a,b or, for a=b>2, into a and 2a below n; n=4 is separate and Wilson handles primes. B independently checks sufficient Legendre valuations for every prime power in n, with the n=4 exceptional valuation; no duplicate factorial factor is counted twice.
- **ex:hensel:** A lifts each of the two roots 2 and 3 mod 5 by the prior simple-root theorem, producing (7,18) mod 25 and (57,68) mod 125; B expands x=a+5t+25u by base-5 digits and verifies the two terminal congruences, with uniqueness at every simple-root lift.
- **ex:tower:** A reduces the top exponent 3^27 mod φ(100)=40, then uses order 20 of 3 mod 100, giving 87; B independently works mod 4 and 25, then CRT. All exponent reductions apply to units only.
- **ex:korselt:** A proves a^n=a modulo every p|n using Fermat for p∤a and zero otherwise, then uses squarefree CRT; B uses the fact that X^p−X divides X^n−X over F_p when p−1|n−1, by checking all p roots and using polynomial division. For 561=3·11·17, 560 is divisible by 2,10,16.
- **ex:lucas:** A raises (1+X) to p-power digits using the characteristic-p binomial identity and unique base-p coefficient selection; B recursively splits n=pq+r and k=ps+t, proving the one-digit congruence by induction. The k>n binomial coefficient is zero by convention.
- **ex:t1-toy-rsa:** A verifies exponent 3·27=81 modulo the primes 5 and 11, distinguishing zero from units, then combines by CRT; B shows X^81−X vanishes on both fields from the polynomial root factors and combines the resulting divisibilities. All 55 residues and 12→23→12 are checked.
- **ex:t1-two-power-roots:** A uses the exact 2-adic valuations of neighboring even factors x±1, yielding ±1 modulo 2^(k−1), then both lifts each; B expands x=1+2t and uses coprimality of t,t+1 to count four roots for k≥3. The k=1,2 exceptions are handled separately.
- **ex:t1-sunzi23:** A solves x=1+4t, t=5 mod 9, then x=21+36s and s=9 mod 11, giving 345 mod 396; B independently builds CRT idempotents 297,352,144 and computes 297+3·352+4·144=345 mod 396. The redesigned problem is distinct from ex:sunzi; its stable label and matching hint/solution are retained.

## DEP-027 — six advanced Chapter 3 exercises: complete symbolic Pass A and independent Pass B (4 November 2026)

**Scope and evidentiary limit.** Reconstructed the prompt, hint, complete solution, general proof steps, exceptional cases and earliest needed prerequisites for pb:primepoly, pb:fpfunctions, pb:aks, pb:wolsten, pb:jacobsthal and pb:kloosterman in the current Chapter 3 source. Pure-Python regression tools/test_volume2_ch03_advanced_exercises.py supplies independent finite corroboration, not a universal proof; additional small-field/valuation/convolution probes below also passed. This batch advances **six more, not the earlier twelve**. No other mathematical items are silently certified.

**pb:primepoly (Schur).** Pass A: if nonconstant integer P eventually took only prime absolute values, choose a with |P(a)|=p prime. Along a+kp the values are divisible by p; for infinitely many k they would be ±p, impossible because P−p and P+p are nonzero polynomials with finitely many roots. For infinitude of distinct prime divisors, suppose a finite list S accounts for all nonzero P(n), choose a≥0 with c=P(a)≠0, let M=|c| times the product of S, and factor P(a+Mt)=cH(t) with H integral, nonconstant and H=1 mod every prime in S. For large t, |H(t)|>1, and any prime factor of H(t) is outside S: contradiction. Pass B independently fixes the exact p-adic valuation of each P(a+Mt) for p in S, using M=product over p in S of p^(v_p(c)+1). Polynomial congruence preserves P(a+Mt)=c modulo each p^(v_p(c)+1), so each valuation remains v_p(c). Under the finite-prime hypothesis |P(a+Mt)|=|c| for every t with nonzero value, contradicting unboundedness of a nonconstant polynomial. The empty S and c=±1 cases are included; only Chapter 1 FTA, divisibility, and Volume 1 polynomial root facts are needed.

**pb:fpfunctions (all functions on F_p).** Pass A: δ_a(X)=1−(X−a)^(p−1) evaluates to the Kronecker point-indicator by Fermat. The sum P=Σ_a f(a)δ_a interpolates f with degree≤p−1; a difference of two such polynomials cannot have p distinct roots unless zero. Its top coefficient is −Σ_a f(a). For odd p, if f is bijective, Σ f(a)=0. Likewise for 1≤t≤p−2, S_t=Σx^t=0 because X^t−1 cannot vanish at all p−1 nonzero x; multiplication by a witness u with u^t≠1 permutes the field. Thus interpolation of f^t has degree≤p−2. Pass B: the evaluation map on polynomials of degree<p is an injective linear map between p-dimensional vector spaces over F_p, hence surjective; the leading coefficient follows independently from Σ_x P(x)=−c_(p−1) via Σx^j=0 for 0≤j≤p−2 and Σx^(p−1)=−1. A second power-sum proof lets H be the image of the t-th-power map on F_p×; H contains a nonidentity element by the polynomial root bound, so multiplying H by it shows ΣH=0, and constant-size preimages imply S_t=0. The p=2 range in part (c) is empty; f=x³ on F_5 has representatives X³, X², X for t=1,2,3. **CI repair:** a real missing closing math delimiter after the final p−2 was repaired in commit cc95eff2f3b03ba8a6c0e9ff38f26375954c6f44; source and mathematical arguments remain separate evidence.

**pb:aks (polynomial criterion).** Pass A: prime n gives divisibility of each interior binomial coefficient and a^n=a mod n by Fermat. For composite n choose q|n prime with q<n and q^e||n; the coefficient of X^q is binom(n,q)a^(n−q), with q-adic valuation e−1 because among the q consecutive numerator terms only n is divisible by q and v_q(q!)=1. Hence the polynomial congruence fails even when a is a unit modulo n. Pass B uses q·binom(n,q)=n·binom(n−1,q−1) and independently computes binom(n−1,q−1)=(-1)^(q−1) mod q, so the same exact valuation follows without the factorial string argument. Prime direction is independently Frobenius in characteristic n followed by Fermat for the constant term. Includes q=2, n=4, and gcd(a,n)=1 as required.

**pb:wolsten (all three Wolstenholme congruences).** Pass A: inversion of nonzero residues gives Σ1/k²=Σk²=0 mod p for p≥5; pairing k and p−k yields 2H_(p−1)=pΣ1/[k(p−k)]=0 mod p². Expanding ∏_(k=1)^(p−1)(1+p/k) through degree two gives 1+pH+p²e₂, with e₂=(H²−Σ1/k²)/2=0 mod p, so binom(2p−1,p−1)=1 mod p³ and binom(2p,p)=2 mod p³. Pass B pairs the PRODUCT factors indexed by k,p−k first: (1+p/k)(1+p/(p−k))=1+2p²/[k(p−k)] exactly. Thus the product is 1+2p²Σ_(k≤(p−1)/2)1/[k(p−k)] mod p³; the half inverse-square sum is zero mod p because the full sum is twice it. An independent reciprocal expansion 1/(p−k)=−1/k−p/k² mod p² yields the harmonic congruence. All denominators are p-units and the p≥5 restriction makes 2,6 invertible.

**pb:jacobsthal (binomial mod p²).** Pass A applies Vandermonde to binom(ap,bp) using ap=(a−1)p+p. The two boundary terms at i=bp and i=(b−1)p give binom((a−1)p,bp) and binom((a−1)p,(b−1)p); every interior term is a product of two multiples of p, by p|binom(p,r) for 0<r<p and i·binom((a−1)p,i)=(a−1)p·binom((a−1)p−1,i−1) for p∤i. Induction and Pascal finish, including b=0,a. Pass B factors the factorials into multiples and nonmultiples of p: (ap)!=p^a a! U(a), U(a)=product for i=0…a−1 and r=1…p−1 of (ip+r). For odd p each block B(i)=product_r(ip+r) is congruent to (p−1)! mod p² because Σr^(-1)=0 mod p; hence U(a)/[U(b)U(a−b)]=1 mod p² and the claim follows. For p=2, B(i)=2i+1 and U(a)=1+a(a−1) mod 4. The residual factor differs from 1 by 2b(a−b) mod 4; if b(a−b) is odd then a is even and b odd, hence binom(a,b) even by base-2 binomial coefficients, so its product with the residual difference is still zero mod 4. All a≥b≥0 and the p=2 exceptional calculation are essential, not assumed away.

**pb:kloosterman (four parts).** Pass A uses substitution x→−x to show K(a) is real, inversion to give K(0)=−1, and x=by for K(a,b)=K(ab) when b≠0. Additive-character orthogonality yields second moment p(p−1). For the fourth, expand Σ_(a,b)|K(a,b)|^4. Orthogonality requires two pairs with equal sums and equal reciprocal sums. If the common sum s≠0, equal product follows and the pairs are identical up to swapping: 2(p−1)(p−2)−(p−1) possibilities; for s=0 there are (p−1)². Hence N=3(p−1)(p−2), and separating b=0 contributes (p−1)^4+(p−1), giving M=2p³−3p²−3p. Then each real K(a)^4≤M<2p³ gives |K(a)|<2^(1/4)p^(3/4)<1.19p^(3/4), better than p−1 for p≥5 but not p=3. Pass B computes the fourth moment using a *one-variable convolution* instead: K(a)^2=Σ_s e(as/p)C_s, C_s=Σ_(x+y=s,x,y≠0)e((1/x+1/y)/p). Orthogonality yields M=pΣ_s|C_s|². Here C_0=p−1. For s≠0 put x=st,y=s(1−t) and u=t(1−t)≠0, with r_u the number of t≠0,1 mapping to u. The t↔1−t involution has exactly one fixed t=1/2, hence Σr_u=p−2 and Σr_u²=1+4(p−3)/2=2p−5. Orthogonality as s ranges through F_p× gives Σ_(s≠0)|C_s|²=pΣr_u²−(Σr_u)², so M=p[(p−1)²+p(2p−5)−(p−2)²]=2p³−3p²−3p independently. This establishes the moment via a different count, including p=3.
