## DEP-028 — Chapter 4 convolution, Möbius inversion and first two exercises (4 November 2026)

**Exact scope:** Printed Chapter 4 named results prop:conv and thm:mobius, and problems ex:360 and ex:muid, including their prompts, hint and complete solution. The tools assumed are Volume 1 finite sums, product factorisation, inclusion–exclusion, and Chapter 1 FTA/Chapter 3 totient and CRT, all proved earlier. Distinct Pass A and Pass B verify the universal statements; no proof relies on analytic Dirichlet series, a subsequent chapter or a finite test as a general argument. The existing manuscript mathematics did not need alteration; regression tests were added separately. Status VERIFIED_TWICE internally, external reviewer pending.

**prop:conv Pass A (printed ordered-factor proof).** The coefficient (f*g)(n)=Σ_(ab=n) f(a)g(b) is a finite sum. Exchanging a and b proves commutativity. Both ((f*g)*h)(n) and (f*(g*h))(n) expand as the same finite ordered-triple sum Σ_(abc=n)f(a)g(b)h(c), proving associativity without reordering any infinite sum. ε(n) is 1 only at n=1, so only the pair (n,1) survives in f*ε, and 1*f similarly. If gcd(m,n)=1, each divisor d|mn uniquely splits as d1|m times d2|n, and the complementary divisor splits likewise; because f,g are multiplicative, all summands split and the double sum factors as (f*g)(m)(f*g)(n). At 1, (f*g)(1)=f(1)g(1)=1.

**Pass B (independent prime-exponent coefficient algebra).** For each prime p and exponent e≥0, define the finite coefficient h_p(e)=Σ_(j=0)^e f(p^j)g(p^(e−j)). If f,g are multiplicative, FTA shows every positive ordered factorisation n=ab is uniquely specified by choosing j_p between 0 and e_p in n=∏p^(e_p); hence (f*g)(n)=∏_p h_p(e_p), with the empty product at n=1. This proves multiplicativity without the printed coprime-divisor bijection. Coefficient convolution for finite arrays is associative by the triple-index map i+j+k=e, commutative by j↔e−j, and has δ_(e,0) as a unit. These local identities and the ordered-factor identity together reconstruct all claims. No unintroduced infinite generating series is used. The test checks 120 values for five functions and pairwise coprime multiplicative cases through 39.

**thm:mobius Pass A (printed convolution inverse).** Both μ and the constant function 1 are multiplicative. At n=p^a (a≥1), Σ_(d|p^a) μ(d)=1−1=0 because only 1,p contribute; at n=1 the sum is 1. Therefore μ*1=ε at all n by the proved multiplicativity of convolution. For arbitrary arithmetic f,g, if g=f*1 then μ*g=(μ*1)*f=ε*f=f; conversely if f=μ*g then 1*f=(1*μ)*g=g. No assumption that f or g is multiplicative is made.

**Pass B (independent inclusion–exclusion and coefficient cancellation).** If n has r distinct prime factors, squarefree divisors correspond to subsets S of these primes, with μ(d)=(-1)^|S|. Thus Σ_(d|n)μ(d)=(1−1)^r=0 when n>1; n=1 gives 1 from the empty subset. Independently invert the full sum directly: Σ_(d|n)μ(d)g(n/d)=Σ_(d|n)μ(d)Σ_(e|n/d)f(e)=Σ_(e|n)f(e)Σ_(d|n/e)μ(d)=f(n), since the inner sum vanishes unless e=n. The reverse direction follows from the same finite identity. Tested on a deliberately **nonmultiplicative** f(n)=(n mod 7)^2−(n mod 5) for 1≤n≤300; this is corroboration only.

**ex:360 Pass A.** Factor 360=2^3·3^2·5; τ=4·3·2=24, σ=(1+2+4+8)(1+3+9)(1+5)=15·13·6=1170, φ=360(1−1/2)(1−1/3)(1−1/5)=96. All formulas were established earlier in this chapter, before the prompt and solution.

**Pass B.** The positive divisors are exactly the 24 distinct integers 2^a 3^b 5^c with 0≤a≤3, 0≤b≤2, 0≤c≤1; direct divisor enumeration sums to 1170 and separately counts 24. Independently compute φ(360) by elementary inclusion–exclusion of multiples of 2,3,5 in 1…360: 360−180−120−72+60+36+24−12=96. The direct count of k with gcd(k,360)=1 gives the same 96.

**ex:muid Pass A.** At every p^a with a≥1, only μ(1)=1 and μ(p)=−1 survive, so Σ_(d|p^a)|μ(d)|=2 and Σ_(d|p^a)μ(d)τ(d)=1−τ(p)=−1. The two divisor-sum functions are multiplicative because |μ| and μτ are multiplicative and convolution with 1 preserves multiplicativity. For ω(n)=r distinct primes this gives 2^r and (−1)^r. At n=1 both sums and right-hand sides are 1.

**Pass B.** Avoid the multiplicative-function lemma entirely: every nonzero μ(d) is a squarefree divisor, uniquely indexed by a subset S of the r distinct primes of n. Each such divisor has |μ(d)|=1 and μ(d)τ(d)=(−1)^|S|·2^|S|. Hence the first sum counts the 2^r subsets, and the second is the binomial sum Σ_(j=0)^r binom(r,j)(−2)^j=(1−2)^r=(−1)^r. This independently checks all exponents, including prime powers and n=1, and matches both hint and solution.

**Regression evidence:** New standard-library tools/test_volume2_ch04_convolution_first_exercises.py (file created 3753bec; corrected TeX label literal matching and removed unused helper in fdde9b5) checks finite convolution algebra, coprime multiplicativity, inversion of nonmultiplicative functions, independent 360 counts, both Möbius identities for all n≤500, and exact prior-result/exercise/hint/solution source order. Finite data do not prove the all-n statements. No manuscript source changes; external mathematical reader and visual page/figure checks pending.

## DEP-029 — Chapter 4 perfect numbers, real hyperbola estimate, periods and coprime density (4 November 2026)

**Baseline and scope.** Local checkout of PR #103 at `1718ff6524266b5404631801210d093e33cb81c6`; fetched main remains `e3b60ca365c601e681c36632a5cd1a2b1d8e5aea`. Read the current plan, full Chapter 4, incoming PR discussion, item registers and actual Volume 1 prerequisite proofs. Exact incoming-head Build books [#1626](https://github.com/OussamaSegh/Order-and-Chance/actions/runs/41789478707) is **completed/success**. This does not certify the source edits below. Scope: the two remaining Chapter 4 named results and its seven remaining unreviewed exercise keys, including question, hint, idea and complete writeup. No exercise was substituted or relocated, no mathematical conclusion weakened, no cover or publishing template changed. These are two **internal** reconstructions, not separate external reviewers.

### `thm:perfect` — Euclid–Euler, with explicit domains and cancellation

**Pass A.** Let integer p≥2 have q=2^p−1 prime. Then q is odd and coprime to 2^(p−1), so σ(2^(p−1)q)=(2^p−1)(q+1)=2n. Conversely any even positive n is uniquely 2^k m with k≥1 and odd m≥1. Perfection and multiplicativity give 2^(k+1)m=qσ(m), q=2^(k+1)−1≥3. Since gcd(q,2^(k+1))=1, earlier Chapter 1 Gauss cancellation gives q|m; write m=qs, s≥1. Substitution gives σ(m)=m+s. As s<m, the two distinct divisors s,m already exhaust this sum. If s>1, the additional divisor 1 gives a contradiction. Thus s=1 and σ(m)=m+1; as m≥3, this forces m=q prime. Put p=k+1. If p=uv with u,v>1, the geometric factorisation of 2^p−1 has the proper divisor 2^u−1, impossible, so p is prime too.

**Pass B, reciprocal-divisor audit.** Start independently from σ(m)/m=2^(k+1)/q=1+1/q. Dividing the divisor sum by m reverses its divisors: σ(m)/m=Σ_(d|m)1/d. The equation and coprime cancellation again show q|m. The divisors 1 and q already contribute 1+1/q; positivity forbids any other divisor, so m=q and q is prime. This recovers the crucial converse without relying on the printed s-inequality. The forward direction is separately the direct list of divisors 2^j and 2^j q for 0≤j≤p−1; their sum is (1+q)(2^p−1)=2n. Checked p=2→6, p=3→28, p=5→496 and the false converse p=11, where q=23·89 is composite. The source now states the positive/integer domains, supplies the previously implicit Gauss-cancellation step, and puts the third-divisor argument inside the proof. The `90` example now tests k=1 against the required odd part 3, replacing a misleading description of the power of 2. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.** Dependencies: Chapter 1 FTA/Gauss and the already-checked Chapter 4 multiplicative divisor formulas.

### `thm:hyperbola` — exact real-x count and every error term

**Pass A, two-strip derivation.** For every real x≥1 let m=floor(sqrt x)≥1. Every positive pair ab≤x has a≤m or b≤m, since otherwise ab≥(m+1)²>x. The overlap is exactly all m² points of the square a,b≤m, since m²≤x. Thus D(x)=Σ_(n≤x)τ(n)=2Σ_(a=1)^m floor(x/a)−m². Replacing the m floors costs less than 2m. Actual Volume 1 `ch05-sequences-series.tex`, `prop:gamma`, proves H_m−ln(m+1)≤γ≤H_m−ln m, hence 0≤H_m−ln m−γ≤1/m. Also m²=x+O(sqrt x), and 0≤ln(sqrt x/m)≤(sqrt x−m)/m=O(x^(−1/2)). Substituting all three bounds gives D(x)=x ln x+(2γ−1)x+O(sqrt x). Dividing by floor x, not silently by x, gives an average asymptotic to ln x.

**Pass B, disjoint smaller-coordinate shells and a uniform error bound.** Count pairs with min(a,b)=j. For j≤m there is the diagonal (j,j), plus 2(floor(x/j)−j) off-diagonal pairs. Summing these *disjoint* shells recovers the exact formula without two-strip inclusion–exclusion. Independently audit the analytic remainder: put y=sqrt x, δ=H_m−ln m−γ∈[0,1/m], F=Σ_(a≤m){x/a}∈[0,m), and G=2x ln(m/y)+x−m². Then R=D−x ln x−(2γ−1)x=G+2xδ−2F. Direct integration gives G=−2∫_m^y(x/t−t)dt. Since (x/t−t)=(y−t)(y+t)/t≤(y−t)·2y/m, we have −4<G≤0 (use y/m<2 and y−m<1). Also x/m≤y+2, so −2y−4<R≤2y+4 and |R|≤6sqrt x for **every** x≥1. This is an all-real-x argument, not a sampled asymptotic fit. The n=1 endpoint, exact squares and both sides of squares are included. Independent integer divisor-sieve counts verify 27 at x=10 and 111 at x=30 and thousands of rational x values. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.** Source now defines γ before its theorem, explains the integer-to-real logarithm step, and replaces the erroneous phrase “diagonal contribution” by the **whole square**; the old explanation would subtract m rather than m². No change to the theorem's mathematical conclusion.

### `ex:sigmaodd` — square or twice a square

**Pass A.** Prime-power multiplicativity makes σ(n) odd iff every local σ(p^a) is odd. For p=2 it is always odd; for odd p its a+1 terms are all odd, so a must be even. Therefore n=2^b m² with odd m; b even gives a square, b odd twice a square. Conversely either shape has all the required parities. **Pass B, independent divisor pairing.** Modulo 2 only odd divisors contribute. Write n=2^b u with u odd; odd divisors of n are exactly divisors of u. Pair d with u/d. All pairs cancel modulo 2 except a fixed divisor d=sqrt u, which exists exactly when u is square. This proves the same condition without multiplicativity. n=1,2 give odd sums 1,3; n=18=2·3² gives39, while n=12 gives28. The source now teaches these checks. Prompt/hint/solution agree; only already-taught FTA/divisors are used. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:coprimesum` — involution, fixed points and inversion

**Pass A.** For n≥2, the φ(n) permitted k lie in 1,…,n−1. The map k→n−k permutes them since gcd(n−k,n)=gcd(k,n). If S is their sum, S=nφ(n)−S, so S=nφ(n)/2. A fixed point causes no difficulty in the permutation sum: n=2 has the single k=1. For n≥3 any fixed k=n/2 has gcd(k,n)=n/2>1 unless n=2. **Pass B, independent Möbius summation.** Insert the already-proved indicator Σ_(d|gcd(k,n))μ(d), interchange finite sums and sum the multiples of d. This yields S=Σ_(d|n)μ(d)d·(n/d)(n/d+1)/2=(n²/2)Σ_(d|n)μ(d)/d+(n/2)Σ_(d|n)μ(d). Earlier Chapter 4 gives the first divisor sum φ(n)/n and the second ε(n)=0 for n>1, giving the same result. At n=1 the second term survives and S=1, whereas the proposed nφ(n)/2 alone is 1/2: the domain restriction is necessary and now illustrated. Direct gcd counts through n=500 support both derivations. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:even` — last digit of an even perfect number

**Pass A.** By the just-checked classification use n=2^(p−1)(2^p−1), with p prime. p=2 gives6. For odd p, if p=1 mod4 then p≥5, 2^(p−1)=6 mod10 and 2^p−1=1 mod10; if p=3 mod4 the factors are4 and7 mod10, including p=3. These give6 and8 respectively. The printed use of 16^k=6 mod10 applies only for k≥1, but the displayed multiplied factors at k=0 were separately checked and are correct. **Pass B, independent modulo-five reconstruction.** For odd p, expand n=2^(2p−1)−2^(p−1). As 2p−1=1 mod4, the first term is2 mod5. The second is1 mod5 if p=1 mod4 and4 mod5 if p=3 mod4. Therefore n=1 or3 mod5; evenness singles out digits6 or8. p=2 is again handled separately. This does not assume a Mersenne number is prime merely because p is prime, and does not claim a converse for decimal endings. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:oddperfect` — at least three distinct prime factors

**Pass A.** σ(1)=1 excludes1. For any prime power p^a, a≥1, the finite positive geometric sum σ(p^a)/p^a=Σ_(j=0)^a p^(−j) is strictly below p/(p−1). If an odd n>1 has one prime, the ratio is <3/2; if it has two distinct primes p<q, p≥3, q≥5, multiplicativity gives σ(n)/n<3/2·5/4=15/8<2, contradicting perfection. **Pass B, direct reciprocal-divisor rectangle.** For n=p^a q^b list the divisors p^i q^j and write σ(n)/n=Σ_(i=0)^a Σ_(j=0)^b p^(−i)q^(−j). The finite rectangle is strictly smaller than the positive infinite rectangle whose elementary geometric sum is p/(p−1)·q/(q−1)≤15/8. The one-prime case is its single row. This avoids assuming the ratio itself is multiplicative and checks the strict finite-tail inequality and maximal choice p=3,q=5. Exact rational finite sums for odd primes≤31 and exponents1…4 support the argument. The question asserts no existence or nonexistence of odd perfect numbers. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:primitive` — no unproved cyclic-subgroup classification

**Pass A.** Extend each length-n word periodically to letters a_i for all integer i. An integer h is a period iff a_(i+h)=a_i for every i. Periods are closed under sums, negatives and differences; n is one. Let g be the least positive period. Divide any period h by g; its remainder is also a period, and minimality forces remainder zero. Thus g|h, in particular g|n. The first g letters form a primitive root, since any shorter repeating block would give a smaller positive period. If another primitive block has length d|n, d is a period, g|d, and that block is d/g copies of the first g letters, so d=g. Therefore q^n=Σ_(d|n)P(d). Earlier Möbius inversion gives P(n)=Σ_(d|n)μ(d)q^(n/d).

**Pass B, Bézout/common-root argument and direct cancellation.** Existence also follows by taking a shortest divisor-length block whose power is the word. If primitive blocks of lengths d,e both work, shifts by d,e preserve the two-sided repeated word. Integer Bézout expresses gcd(d,e) as an integer combination of d,e, so it too is a period. As gcd(d,e) divides each length, each block repeats the same initial gcd-length block; primitivity forces d=e=gcd(d,e). For the inversion formula expand q^(n/d)=Σ_(e|n/d)P(e) and switch finite sums: the coefficient of P(e) is Σ_(d|n/e)μ(d)=ε(n/e). Only e=n survives. Neither route imports Lagrange's theorem or a classification of subgroups of Z/nZ. The original solution jumped from a subgroup assertion to g|n without explaining that classification; the source now supplies the elementary integer-remainder proof. q=1 yields P(1)=1 and P(n)=0 for n>1; n=1 gives q. Words retain their starting letter (not necklaces), so ab and ba count separately, and P(4)=16−4=12 for q=2. Exhaustive words for q=1,n≤10; q=2,n≤10; q=3,n≤8 confirm counts, primitive-root uniqueness and every cyclic period. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally.**

### `ex:coprimeprob` — every finite sum, infinite rearrangement and final constant

**Pass A (all parts).** (a) The checked identity μ*1=ε gives the gcd indicator; finite interchange yields C(N)=Σ_(d≤N)μ(d)floor(N/d)², including N=1. (b) The telescoping bound Σ_(n>K)n^(−2)≤Σ_(n>K)1/[n(n−1)]=1/K proves Z=Σ n^(−2) finite, and |μ|≤1 gives absolute convergence of M=Σμ(n)/n². The double product is absolutely summable: outside a K×K square its absolute mass is at most2Z/K. For T≥K² the grouped region dm≤T contains that square. Square partial products tend to MZ, while every grouped partial sum is exactly Σ_(k≤T)k^(−2)Σ_(d|k)μ(d)=1. Thus MZ=1, fully justifying the grouping rather than citing a future Dirichlet-series theorem. The source now includes this local tail proof and defines ζ(2)=Z. (c) Since 0≤t²−floor(t)²<2t for t≥1, |C(N)/N²−Σ_(d≤N)μ(d)/d²|≤2H_N/N. The omitted μ-series tail is ≤1/N. Therefore |C(N)/N²−1/Z|≤(2H_N+1)/N→0. Actual Volume 1 `ch03-polynomials.tex`, `ex:basel` **full solution** proves Z=π²/6 by finite cotangent polynomial, Viète and squeeze; this is available before Volume 2 and not an imported infinite-product formula.

**Pass B, finite-prime CRT exclusion.** Fix integer B≥2 and let S be primes≤B, Q=∏_(p∈S)p. In one full square of residues modQ, CRT gives exactly ∏_(p∈S)(p²−1) pairs that are not simultaneously zero mod any p. Hence their limiting proportion in 1…N squares is A_B=∏_(p≤B)(1−1/p²). Every genuinely coprime pair qualifies; any false positive has a common prime p>B. Union bound gives a proportion at most Σ_(B<p≤N)floor(N/p)²/N²≤Σ_(r>B)1/r²≤1/B. Thus limsup and liminf of C(N)/N² differ from A_B by at most1/B. To identify A_B independently, expand the **finite number of** geometric factors: A_B^(−1)=Σ_(n with primes≤B)1/n² by FTA and positive geometric sums. Every n≤B is included, so 0≤Z−A_B^(−1)≤1/B; hence A_B→1/Z. Likewise expanding ∏_(p≤B)(1−1/p²) gives the μ sum over squarefree S-supported n, differing from M by at most1/B. Thus this route separately confirms both MZ=1 and the coprime limit without the printed double-series grouping. No Chapter 11 theorem is assumed. Exact finite gcd/floor counts through N=250, rational floor-error checks, grouped product cancellation and full CRT residue squares Q=2,6,30,210 corroborate the proof; the orchard count is91/144 at N=12. **VERIFIED_TWICE internally**, external review and EPUB/print visual validation still open.

For12 list divisors1,2,3,4,6,12 with μ values1,−1,−1,0,1,0. For30 the eight squarefree-divisor values are1,−1,−1,−1,1,1,1,−1, summing0. ## DEP-030 — Chapter 5 orders and primitive roots (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Checked the statements, proofs, hypotheses, prior results, hints and solutions for the four named results and first four exercises below. The source now explicitly partitions the excluded moduli in the cyclicity proof. Finite checks support, but do not replace, these general derivations.

**`prop:order`.** Pass A: integer exponents mapping to one form an additive subgroup containing Euler's positive exponent. Integer division by its least positive member `k` proves that subgroup is `kZ`. Writing `d=gcd(k,j)` and cancelling coprime `k/d,j/d` yields `ord(a^j)=k/d`. Pass B: divide any signed return exponent by `k`; the remainder is another return exponent, so minimality makes it zero. For the power formula, Bézout gives integers `u,v` with `u(k/d)+v(j/d)=1`; if `k/d` divides `(j/d)m`, multiply this identity by `m` to show it divides `m`. Conversely `m=k/d` works. Covers `n=1`, `j=0`, negative exponents and existence from Euler.

**`thm:cyclic`.** Pass A: if exact order `d` occurs, one element's `d` powers exhaust the degree-`d` roots of `X^d-1`, so exactly `phi(d)` elements have order `d`. The order-class count and `sum_(d|p-1)phi(d)=p-1` force every class to occur. Pass B: let `L` be the lcm of unit orders. For each `q^e||L`, extract a `q^e`-order element from an element whose order contains that factor. Their product has order `L`, because their orders are coprime and the field group is abelian. Every one of the `p-1` units satisfies `X^L=1`; the field root bound implies `p-1<=L`. As `L|p-1`, equality holds, and the `phi(p-1)` coprime powers of this element are precisely the primitive roots. The local text proves the field root bound; `p=2` gives the trivial group.

**`lem:lift`.** Pass A: in `(1+p^k t)^p`, the first nonconstant term is `p^(k+1)t`; for `2<=j<p`, `p|binom(p,j)` and `jk+1>=k+2`; the last term has `pk>=k+2` for odd `p`. Hence the valuation increases exactly once. Pass B: factor `x^p-1=(x-1)(1+x+...+x^(p-1))`. For `x=1+p^k t`, the second factor is `p mod p^2`: its linear correction has factor `p^k * p(p-1)/2`, and all higher terms have factor `p^(2k)`; at `p=3,k=1` the latter are still divisible by `p^2`. Thus its valuation is one. Includes odd `p=3` and `k=1`.

**`thm:cyclicn`.** Pass A: if a generator `g mod p` fails at `p^2`, `g+p` succeeds by the binomial linear term `(p-1)pg^(p-2)`. The lemma forces its order at `p^k` to be `(p-1)p^(k-1)`; CRT handles `2p^k`. Every excluded modulus has four roots of `x^2=1`: either four on its factor `2^a,a>=3`, or the four independent signs on coprime factors `a,b>2`. A cyclic group of even order has exactly two. Pass B: independently partition `n` by the exponent of two and its number of distinct odd prime factors. Exponent at least three gives the first obstruction; exponent at most two with two odd prime factors, or exponent two with one, gives the second. The leftovers are exactly `1,2,4,p^k,2p^k`. For odd `p`, the lemma says `g^(p-1)` has order `p^(k-1)` modulo `p^k`; together with `ord_p(g)=p-1`, this forces `ord_(p^k)(g)=phi(p^k)`. Check `n=1,2,4` directly.

**`ex:findroots`.** Pass A: maximal-proper-divisor order tests give `ord_7(3)=6`, `ord_11(2)=10`, `ord_13(2)=12`; the coprime exponents `1,5,7,11` yield `2,6,11,7 mod 13`. Pass B: independently enumerate powers until first return, then the twelve unit orders modulo thirteen; only `2,6,7,11` have order twelve. The hint's prime-divisor test is sufficient because each proper divisor of `p-1` divides `(p-1)/q` for some prime `q` dividing `p-1`.

**`ex:period`.** Pass A: long division iterates the nonzero remainder `r -> 10r mod p`, starting from one; its least return is `ord_p(10)`. Pass B: a repeated block of length `k`, allowing leading zeroes, represents `A/(10^k-1)`; equality with `1/p` is equivalent to `p|10^k-1`. Both give periods six for `1/7` and sixteen for `1/17`, using `10^8=-1 mod 17`. The excluded primes two and five terminate.

**`ex:fermatfactor`.** Pass A: `2^(2^n)=-1 mod p` makes the order divide `2^(n+1)` but not `2^n`, so it is exactly `2^(n+1)|p-1`. Pass B: the difference-of-squares factorization gives a return at `2^(n+1)`; any earlier power-of-two return would persist under squaring and contradict the displayed minus one. The same exact order follows. Includes `n=0,p=3`; no Fermat-number primality is assumed.

**`ex:powersum`.** Pass A: a primitive-root geometric sum is `p-1=-1 mod p` when `p-1|k`, and otherwise zero by field cancellation. Pass B: reduce `k` modulo `p-1`. If its residue `r` is between one and `p-2`, the root bound supplies a unit `u` with `u^r!=1`; scaling the sum by `u` permutes the units, so `S=u^k S` and `S=0`. The divisible case follows termwise from Fermat. Includes `k=0,p=2`; cancellation is never applied to zero.

**Verification boundary.** `tools/test_volume2_ch05_orders.py` checks order formulae through modulus 80, cyclic classification through 120 and the finite exercise examples. **27/72 named results and 71/144 solutions** now have two internal passes; **45 named and 73 solutions** remain. Other Chapter 5 items, unnamed arguments, all-page visual, external and publisher gates stay open. Next: `thm:twopower`, `cor:mod8squares`, `ex:kth`, `ex:gausswilson`. PR #103 remains draft.

## DEP-031 — finish the Chapter 5 named results and next two exercises (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Checked the remaining named Chapter 5 theorem and corollary, plus `ex:kth` and `ex:gausswilson`, against the printed statements, proofs, preceding tools, hints, ideas, and full solutions. The duplicate modulo-16 example was removed. The Gauss–Wilson solution now uses the four-element cosets taught locally, eliminating its unnecessary Volume 1 vector-space basis dependency.

**`thm:twopower`.** Pass A: for `m=0`, `v_2(5−1)=2`. Squaring `1+2^(m+2)t` with odd `t` makes the first nonconstant term have valuation `m+3`, while the quadratic term has valuation at least `m+4`. Thus `ord_(2^k)(5)=2^(k−2)`, since the immediately preceding power is not one. Its powers are all `1 mod 4`, so multiplying by `−1` gives disjoint sets; their combined `2^(k−1)` elements exhaust the odd units. Pass B: factor `5^(2^m)−1=4*product_(i=0)^(m−1)(5^(2^i)+1)`. Each factor in the product is `2 mod 4`, giving valuation `m+2` directly without induction. The reduction map from odd classes modulo `2^k` onto `{1,3} mod 4` has a kernel of size `2^(k−2)`; the powers of `5` lie in it and already have that exact order, so they fill it. The other fiber is their negatives. The boundary `k=3` has four units and order two for `5`.

**`cor:mod8squares`.** Pass A: the unique representation `(-1)^a5^b` squares to `5^(2b)`. Conversely every even power occurs; reduction mod eight distinguishes the positive even-exponent forms (`1`) from positive odd (`5`) and negative forms (`3` or `7`). Pass B: every odd square is `1 mod 8`. Conversely start with a root of `u=1 mod 8` at modulus eight. If odd `r` is a root mod `2^j`, then `r+2^(j−1)` has square differing by `2^j r mod 2^(j+1)`, which toggles the next bit because `r` is odd. One of the two candidates is a root mod `2^(j+1)`. Induct through arbitrary `k>=3`. Thus the criterion is independent of the representation theorem; for `k=3` the only square class is one.

**`ex:kth`.** Pass A: write `a=g^alpha`, `x=g^xi` in the cyclic field-unit group of order `M=p−1`. The linear congruence `k xi=alpha mod M` has `d=gcd(k,M)` solutions exactly when `d|alpha`; this is equivalent to `a^(M/d)=1`. Pass B: the power map `x->x^k` is a group homomorphism. The kernel has `d` members by the order formula and coprime-generator exponent count, so its image has `M/d` members. Every image element satisfies `y^(M/d)=1`; the field polynomial `X^(M/d)−1` has at most `M/d` roots, so its root set equals the image. Every successful fiber has exactly `d` members. This independently supplies the iff condition and count, including `p=2`, `d=1`, `d=M`, and the stated nonzero `a`.

**`ex:gausswilson`.** Pass A: inverse pairing reduces the unit product to the product of `S={u:u²=1}`. In the cyclic case `S={1,−1}` for `n>2`. Otherwise the classification gives at least four roots. Choose distinct nonidentity `u,v` in `S`; `H={1,u,v,uv}` has four elements, and each coset `sH` has product `s⁴u²v²=1`. Cosets partition `S`, so the whole product is one. Pass B: fix any nonidentity `u in S`; multiplication by `u` pairs `S` into `{s,us}`, each with product `u`. When `S` has more than two elements, a second nonidentity `v!=u` produces four-element cosets and proves `4` divides `|S|`, so `|S|/2` is even and the paired product `u^(|S|/2)=1`. The cyclic case has one pair and product `u=−1`. This avoids importing vector-space basis theory; the source now teaches Pass A directly.

**Finite corroboration and limit.** The expanded `tools/test_volume2_ch05_orders.py` checks unique forms and square sets for `3<=k<=10`, power-equation counts for small primes and exponents, and every unit product through `n=120`. These are regression samples, not proofs. **29/72 named results and 73/144 exercise solutions** have two internal passes; **43 named and 71 solutions** remain. All six named results in Chapter 5 are internally checked, but the chapter's remaining exercises, unnamed arguments and all other gates remain open. Next: `ex:cyclotomic` and the Chapter 5 short exercises, then its Journey problems. PR #103 stays draft.

## DEP-032 — cyclotomic and short Chapter 5 exercises; preface contract (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Reconstructed each prompt, hint, idea and full solution below. The preface's former invitation to use ideas from “subsequent volumes” contradicted the present independent-solution contract; it now asks readers to combine methods developed here. The Gauss–Wilson hint was aligned with its new elementary coset proof. These narrative repairs do not certify the remaining preface or whole-book voice gate.

**`ex:cyclotomic` (all three parts).** Pass A: induct on `n`. The proper-divisor product is monic and integral, so monic polynomial long division of `X^n−1` stays integral. Its complex root product proves zero remainder and identifies the quotient as `Phi_n`. Comparing constant terms gives `Phi_n(0)=±1` for `n>=2`. If `p∤n` divides `Phi_n(a)`, then `a^n=1 mod p`, so `p∤a`. A smaller order `d|n` makes `a` a root of a proper factor from `X^d−1` as well as `Phi_n`, hence a double root of `X^n−1`; its derivative `nX^(n−1)` does not vanish at `a`, contradiction. Thus the order is `n`, which divides `p−1`. For infinitude, suppose all eligible primes are `p_1,...,p_r`; with `m=n*product p_i`, choose `t` so `|Phi_n(mt)|>1`. This value is `±1 mod m`, hence has a prime divisor outside both `n` and the list, which part (b) makes eligible. For `n=1`, use Euclid's theorem separately. Pass B rebuilds the quotient by grouping the distinct complex `n`-th roots by their exact orders: each group is the primitive-root product, so division is exact over `C[X]`; because both dividend and monic divisor have integer coefficients, every step of the unique long division stays integral. In characteristic `p∤n`, the gcd of `X^n−1` and its derivative is one since zero is not a root of `X^n−1`; thus *no* two divisor factors can share a root. This independently rules out a lower order, including `n=2` and `a=0`. The constant-term congruence excludes every factor of `m` for every integer `t`, including an empty alleged prime list. Finite cyclotomic polynomials through `n=24` and all primes below 98 support the derivative/order implication, not the infinitude claim.

**`ex:t1-units16`.** Pass A: the four powers of five are `1,5,9,13`; their negatives are `15,11,7,3`, and squaring these forms yields exactly `1,9`. Pass B directly lists the eight odd residues and their squares: `1,7,9,15` square to `1`, while `3,5,11,13` square to `9`. The two sets of four agree with the unique representations and the modulo-eight square test. The exercise repeats a worked example intentionally as elementary practice; the genuinely duplicate second example in the prose was removed at DEP-031.

**`ex:t1-square-cosets`.** Pass A: the square criterion says the square subgroup is the odd units `1 mod 8`, with `2^(k-3)` members among `2^(k-1)` units. Index four; multiply by representatives `1,3,5,7` to obtain the four residue fibers. Pass B: reduction of units mod `2^k` onto the four odd unit classes mod eight is a surjective group homomorphism. Its kernel is precisely the square subgroup by the corollary; two elements lie in the same coset iff their quotient is in the kernel iff they have the same remainder mod eight. This proves both index and exact cosets independently. At `k=3` each coset is a singleton.

**`ex:t1-gen7`.** Pass A: powers of two first return at three, while powers of three are `3,2,6,4,5,1` and first return at six. Coprime exponents `1,5` produce generators `3,5`. Pass B: direct first-return table for all six units gives orders `1,3,6,3,6,2` for residues `1,2,3,4,5,6` respectively, confirming that exactly `3,5` are primitive and `2` is not. This also matches `phi(6)=2`.

**Verification boundary.** The expanded Chapter 5 regression has six passing finite tests. Strict internal totals: **29/72 named results and 77/144 solutions** checked twice; **43 named and 67 solutions** remain. Chapter 5 still has four unreviewed advanced keyed problems (`pb:smith`, `pb:lambert`, `pb:ramsum`, `pb:phipm1`) and unnamed passages. Next reconstruct those four, then start Chapter 6. No chapter or phase is signed off; PR stays draft.

## DEP-033 — four advanced Chapter 5 problems (4 November 2026)

**Scope and source repairs.** Reviewed every part, hint, idea and complete solution of `pb:smith`, `pb:lambert`, `pb:ramsum`, `pb:phipm1`. The latter used `Λ` before defining it, asserted positivity before taking logarithms and skipped the order step behind a cyclotomic index identity. The prompt now defines `Λ`; the solution proves `sum_(d|n)Λ(d)=log n` by counting prime powers, proves positivity by conjugate root pairs, supplies the order step and gives an explicit polynomial inverse of `1−ζ`. These are genuine first-use repairs, not a claim of whole-book prerequisite signoff.

**`pb:smith` (both parts).** Pass A: let `A_(i,d)=1_(d|i)` and `D=diag(f(1),...,f(n))`. The `(i,j)` entry of `A D A^T` is `sum_(d|gcd(i,j))f(d)=g(gcd(i,j))`. `A` is lower triangular with diagonal one, hence the determinant is `product f(d)`, including zero-valued weights. Gauss's `sum_(d|m)phi(d)=m` gives part (a). Möbius inversion of `m²` gives `J_2(m)=sum_(d|m)μ(d)(m/d)²=m² product_(p|m)(1−p^(−2))`; this is the requested squared-gcd determinant. Pass B performs the inverse operation directly: the lower triangular matrix `B_(i,d)=μ(i/d)1_(d|i)` has determinant one. In `B M B^T`, expand `g(gcd(i,j))` and switch finite sums. Each coefficient of `f(d)` factors as `sum_(i:d|i|r)μ(r/i)` times `sum_(j:d|j|s)μ(s/j)`, which is one only when `d=r=s` and zero otherwise by `μ*1=ε`. Thus `B M B^T=D` without assuming the factorization. The prime-power calculation of `J_2` separately checks its local factor; for `n=4` the product `1·3·8·12=288`. Actual Volume 1 triangular determinants are the prerequisite.

**`pb:lambert` (all three parts).** Pass A: for `r=|x|<1`, `sum_(n,k>=1)|a_n x^(kn)| <= C/(1−r) sum_n n^K r^n < infinity` by the polynomial bound and the ratio test. Absolute interchange groups terms by `m=kn`, giving coefficient `sum_(d|m)a_d`. The established identities `sum_(d|m)phi(d)=m` and `sum_(d|m)μ(d)=1_(m=1)` yield `x/(1−x)²` and `x`. For `λ`, local prime-power divisor sums alternate `1−1+...+(-1)^a`, equal to one exactly at even exponents, hence the square indicator and `sum_(j>=1)x^(j²)`. At `x=1/2` the first two values are `2` and `1/2`. Pass B starts with finite truncations in `n`; for each fixed coefficient `x^m`, all terms have stabilized once `n>=m`. The remaining analytic tail is bounded uniformly by `C/(1−r)sum_(n>J)n^K r^n ->0`. The resulting coefficients have at most polynomial growth, so their power series also converges absolutely for `r<1`; coefficientwise equality therefore gives the same analytic identity without an unjustified formal-series swap. Independently, the product over prime exponents of `sum_(d|m)λ(d)` is one exactly for perfect squares, and differentiating the finite geometric identity then taking its absolutely convergent limit gives `sum m x^m=x/(1−x)²`. Complex `x`, arbitrary fixed real growth exponent `K`, and the endpoint exclusion `|x|=1` are respected.

**`pb:ramsum` (all three parts).** Pass A: the complete root sum `η_q(n)=q 1_(q|n)` groups by the reduced denominator of `a/q` into `sum_(d|q)c_d(n)`. Möbius inversion gives `c_q(n)=sum_(d|gcd(q,n))d μ(q/d)`, an integer. Multiplicativity reduces Hölder's formula to `q=p^a`, where `b=min(a,v_p(n))` gives respectively `phi(p^a), −p^(a−1), 0` as `b=a,a−1,<=a−2`. Fourier orthogonality of distinct reduced fractions over a common period `N` gives the requested delta inner product. Pass B inserts `1_((a,q)=1)=sum_(h|gcd(a,q))μ(h)` directly into the original complex sum; summing all multiples of `h` is a geometric root sum and yields the same divisor formula without reduced-denominator grouping. In the average of two integer formulas, `N^(−1)sum_(n<=N)1_(d|n)1_(e|n)=1/lcm(d,e)` since `d,e|N`. Thus the inner product is `sum_(d|q,e|r) μ(q/d) μ(r/e) gcd(d,e)`. It factors over primes. For local exponents `a=b>=1`, the four surviving terms yield `p^a−p^(a−1)=phi(p^a)`; for `a!=b`, they cancel in pairs, including when one exponent is zero. This proves orthogonality by integer divisors independently of Fourier cancellation. `q=1`, `n=0` and negative `n` satisfy the same identities; the source defines `e(t)` before using it.

**`pb:phipm1` (all three parts).** Pass A: divide `X^n−1` by `X−1`, set `X=1`, and take logs of `n=product_(d|n,d>1)Phi_d(1)`. Each `Phi_d(1)>0` because nonreal primitive roots pair with conjugates and contribute `|1−ζ|²>0`; `d=2` contributes two. By FTA, `sum_(d|n)Λ(d)=log n`, so Möbius inversion gives `log Phi_n(1)=Λ(n)`. For (b), negating primitive odd-order roots gives `Phi_(2m)(X)=Phi_m(−X)` for odd `m>1`. If `p|m`, the order formula shows `ord(z^p)=m` iff `ord(z)=pm`; hence `Phi_(pm)(X)=Phi_m(X^p)`. These identities give `Phi_n(−1)=1` for odd `n>=3`, `Phi_m(1)` for `n=2m` with odd `m>=3`, and `Phi_(n/2)(1)` for `4|n`. Therefore the only values greater than one are the corresponding prime for `n=2p^a` (odd `p`) and two for `n=2^a,a>=2`. For (c), non-prime-power `n>=2` has `Phi_n(1)=1`; the product of all `1−ζ^a` is one in `Z[ζ]`, so the other factors invert `1−ζ`. Pass B starts instead from the finite Möbius product `Phi_n(X)=product_(d|n)(X^d−1)^{μ(n/d)}`. Let `X->1` through values greater than one; `sum_(d|n)μ(n/d)=0`, so the factors `X−1` cancel and `Phi_n(1)=product_(d|n)d^{μ(n/d)}`. In its prime factorization the exponent of `p` is one exactly when `n` is a power of `p`, zero otherwise, by pairing the squarefree divisors containing and excluding any second prime. This independently confirms (a) and positivity. The two primitive-root maps in (b) can be checked by applying the order formula to every complex root, with no sign change because all displayed polynomials are monic and have the same degree. For (c), the alternative integer-polynomial identity `Phi_n(X)−1=(X−1)Q(X)` yields `(1−ζ)Q(ζ)=1` explicitly, without a product over conjugates. The prompt's `n>=3` excludes the exceptional `Phi_2(−1)=0`; `n=1` is also outside (a) and (c).

**Finite corroboration and limit.** `tools/test_volume2_ch05_orders.py` now has ten passing tests, including exact small Smith determinants, Lambert coefficients, complex/direct Ramanujan sums and orthogonality, cyclotomic values and polynomial-inverse identities. Finite tests are only samples. **All six named Chapter 5 results and all sixteen keyed Chapter 5 solutions now have two documented internal mathematical passes**, including two Journey problems reviewed earlier. ## DEP-034 — Chapter 6 Euler criterion and first supplementary law (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Reconstructed three named statements and proofs, their immediate explanatory passages, all hypotheses and Chapter 5 dependencies. The new `tools/test_volume2_ch06_first_criteria.py` compares direct square sets with Euler powers, Gauss signs and the modulo-eight law over small primes. It is finite corroboration only. No Chapter 6 exercise is certified in this batch.

**`thm:eulercrit`.** Pass A: an odd-prime primitive root `g` exists by Chapter 5. The class `g^i` is a square iff `i` is even, giving exactly `(p−1)/2` square and nonsquare classes. `g^((p−1)/2)` has order two, so it is `−1` in the field. Consequently `a^((p−1)/2)=(-1)^i`, exactly the Legendre value. For `p|a`, both sides are zero. Products of these congruences give equality of integer symbols because `−1,0,1` are distinct modulo odd `p`; evaluating at `a=−1` gives the mod-four law. Pass B avoids primitive roots: the map `x->x²` on the nonzero field has exactly two preimages `x,−x` for each square, giving `h=(p−1)/2` distinct squares. Fermat says each is a root of `X^h−1`; the degree-`h` root bound makes these *all* its roots. Every nonzero `a` has `(a^h)²=1`, hence `a^h=±1`; it is `1` exactly on squares, `−1` otherwise. The same product argument proves complete multiplicativity, and `(-1)^h` gives the supplementary law. This includes `p=3`, `a=0` and arbitrary signed integers `a`.

**`thm:gausslemma`.** Pass A: for each `1<=k<=h`, reduce `ak` into the unique symmetric representative `r_k` with `|r_k|<=h`. None is zero because `a` is a unit. If `|r_k|=|r_l|`, then `ak=±al mod p`, so `k=l` or `k+l=p`; the latter is impossible since `k+l<=p−1`. Therefore absolute values permute `1,...,h`. Multiply: `a^h h! = product r_k = (-1)^μ h! mod p`. The factorial is a unit, so cancellation and Euler's criterion give the result. Pass B treats `{±1,...,±h}` as the `h` pairs of nonzero residues modulo `p`: multiplication by `a` permutes these pairs, while choosing the positive representative of each pair attaches a sign. This directly constructs a signed permutation `ak=ε_k σ(k)` with no duplicate or missing pair. Its product is `a^h product k=(product ε_k)product σ(k)`, and bijectivity gives `product σ(k)=product k`. Cancelling only nonzero factors shows the Legendre symbol is the product of the signs. The second reconstruction checks that the signed interval has no endpoint ambiguity (odd `p`) and that the negative count is defined even for `p=3`.

**`cor:two`.** Pass A: for `a=2`, all `2k` lie between `2` and `p−1`. A signed representative is negative exactly when `2k>p/2`, that is `k>p/4`. Thus `μ=h−floor(p/4)`. For `p=8m+1,3,5,7`, this is `2m,2m+1,2m+1,2m+2` respectively, matching the parity of `(p²−1)/8`. Pass B independently counts integers in the interval `p/4<k<=h` as `floor((p+1)/4)` and checks the four odd residue classes: signs `+,-,-,+` for `1,3,5,7 mod 8`. Expanding `(8m+r)²−1` over eight gives even, odd, odd, even for those same four residues. Hence the exponent formula and the square iff `p≡±1 mod 8` hold, including `p=3`.

## DEP-035 — Eisenstein, quadratic reciprocity and its independent Gauss-sum bridge (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Checked the complete named lemma and theorem, the four-part optional Gauss-sum exercise, both full solutions and printed first-use sites. Actual Volume 1 `prop:orth`, `ex:eisen` full solution and `ex:gauss` full solution were read and entered in the prerequisite crosswalk. Volume 1 proves prime-cyclotomic irreducibility and root-sum orthogonality; its Gauss exercise proves only the *absolute* square `|G|²=p`. The signed square and reciprocity comparison are established here. The new finite Chapter 6 regression checks Eisenstein floors, reciprocity and complex Gauss sums for small primes, not the universal statements.

**`lem:eisenstein`.** Pass A: write `ka=p floor(ka/p)+s_k`, with `0<s_k<p` because `p∤a`. Let `A` sum the `s_k<p/2` and `B` sum those above `p/2`, whose number is `μ`. Gauss' lemma makes the signed absolute values a permutation of `1,...,h`, where `h=(p−1)/2` and `S=sum_(k<=h)k`. Therefore `aS=pT+A+B` and `S=A+μp−B`. Subtracting gives `(a−1)S=p(T−μ)+2B`; since `a,p` are odd, `T=μ mod 2`, proving the character formula. Pass B writes each ordinary remainder as `s_k=ε_k b_k+pδ_k`, with `b_k` the distinct positive signed representatives, `ε_k=±1`, and `δ_k=1` precisely when `ε_k=-1`. Sum over `k`: `aS−pT=sum ε_k b_k+pμ`. Modulo two, `sum ε_k b_k=sum b_k=S`, while odd `a,p` reduce to one. Hence `(a−1)S=T+μ mod 2`, so again `T=μ mod 2` without the two auxiliary sums `A,B`. This applies to negative odd `a` using Euclidean floors. For even `a` it need not hold: `a=2,p=3` has floor sum zero but Legendre value `−1`.

**`thm:qr`.** Pass A: for distinct odd primes, the rectangle `1<=x<=(p−1)/2`, `1<=y<=(q−1)/2` contains `((p−1)/2)((q−1)/2)` integer points. No point lies on `qx=py`, since `p|qx` implies `p|x`, impossible in the rectangle. Below the line, `qx/p` is nonintegral and less than `q/2`, so exactly `floor(qx/p)` allowed positive `y` occur in column `x`; their sum is `T(q,p)`. Above it, exchange roles for `T(p,q)`. Their sum is the rectangle count, and Eisenstein turns the two floor sums into the product of Legendre signs. Pass B is the fully independent quadratic Gauss-sum argument audited with `ex:gausssum` below: `g²=p*` and Frobenius modulo the distinct odd prime `q` give `(p*/q)=(q/p)`. Substitute `p*=(-1)^((p−1)/2)p` and Euler's law for `-1` to obtain exactly the displayed reciprocity sign. The arithmetic descent from `Z[ζ]` to integers is proved before cancelling `p*`; no invalid cancellation of `g` occurs in the quotient ring. Includes both primes `3 mod 4` and the `p=3,q=5` boundary.

**`ex:gausssum`, parts (a)–(d).** Pass A: expand `g²`, set `b=ac`, and use character multiplicativity plus finite root orthogonality. The inner nonzero `a` sum is `p−1` only at `c=-1` and `−1` elsewhere; `sum_(c!=0)χ(c)=0` gives `g²=χ(-1)p=p*`. In `Z[ζ]/q`, Frobenius and odd `q` give `g^q=Σχ(a)ζ^(aq)=χ(q)g`. Prime-cyclotomic irreducibility makes `1,ζ,...,ζ^(p−2)` a rationally independent integer spanning set; hence `qZ[ζ]∩Z=qZ`. Multiply the Frobenius congruence by `g`, use `g²=p*`, descend the resulting *integer* congruence and cancel the unit `p* mod q`; Euler's criterion gives `(p*/q)=χ(q)`. The root count `#{x:x²=a}=1+χ(a)` including `a=0` gives `G=Σ_x ζ^(x²)=g`, hence `G²=p*`. Pass B starts with this `G`. Its norm is `G conj(G)=Σ_(x,y)ζ^(x²−y²)`. The invertible change `(u,v)=(x−y,x+y)` over `F_p` turns the exponent into `uv`; summing in `v` gives `p` at `u=0` and zero otherwise. Also `conj(g)=χ(-1)g` by substituting `a->-a`; so `g²=χ(-1)p` without the printed `b=ac` expansion. For the integer descent, independently write any relation `n=qF(ζ)` as `qF(X)−n=Φ_p(X)H(X)`: irreducibility makes `Φ_p` the minimal polynomial, monic integer division makes `H` integral, and reducing modulo `q` gives the polynomial equality `−n=bar(Φ_p)bar(H)`. A nonzero constant cannot have the positive-degree monic factor `bar(Φ_p)`; hence `q|n`. Repeating Frobenius now gives the same Legendre comparison. This checks all four prompt parts and the exact Volume 1 prerequisites; the sign choice of `G` itself is **not** claimed or needed.

## DEP-036 — Jacobi proposition and first three Chapter 6 exercises (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Checked the Jacobi statement, proof and immediate algorithm against the actual earlier Legendre laws and reciprocity, plus the first three prompts, hints, ideas and complete solutions. The `ex:minus3` solution now teaches a second route through an order-three element. Finite tests check composite Jacobi reciprocity through 101 and small prime/residue cases; the general checks below, not the tests, establish the claims.

**`prop:jacobi`.** Pass A: for odd `a,b`, the difference `u(ab)−u(a)−u(b)=(a−1)(b−1)/2` is even, since both factors are even. Likewise `v(ab)−v(a)−v(b)=(a²−1)(b²−1)/8` is even because each numerator factor is divisible by eight. Thus the parity exponents `u(n)=(n−1)/2` and `v(n)=(n²−1)/8` multiply correctly over the prime factors of any positive odd `n`. The prime supplementary laws prove the first two Jacobi formulas. With coprime factorizations `m=product q_i`, `n=product p_j`, including multiplicities, expand both Jacobi symbols; each prime pair contributes the quadratic-reciprocity sign `(-1)^(u(q_i)u(p_j))`. Adding all exponents mod two gives `u(m)u(n)`. Pass B checks the sign maps directly on the units modulo four and eight: `(-1)^u` is `+` on `1 mod4`, `−` on `3 mod4`; `(-1)^v` is `+` on `1,7 mod8`, `−` on `3,5 mod8`. Each table is multiplicative under the four odd residue classes, independently confirming the parity identities. The cross-product of prime-factor signs is then the parity of the number of pairs `(q_i,p_j)` both `3 mod4`; that equals the product of the two counts modulo two, i.e. `u(m)u(n)`. At `m=1` or `n=1`, the empty product is one and both exponents are even/zero. The algorithm paragraph's zero rule also correctly handles non-coprime inputs, although the proposition itself assumes coprimality.

**`ex:minus3`.** Pass A: for `p>3`, multiplicativity and the `-1` law give `(-3/p)=(-1)^((p−1)/2)(3/p)`. Reciprocity with three gives `(3/p)=(-1)^((p−1)/2)(p/3)`, so the signs cancel; modulo three the only nonzero square is one. Pass B uses no reciprocity: a root `r²=-3` yields `t=(r−1)/2` with `t²+t+1=0`, and conversely such a `t` gives `(2t+1)²=-3`. For `p>3`, `t!=1`; `(t−1)(t²+t+1)=t³−1` makes its order exactly three. The cyclic unit group has such an element iff `3|p−1`. The extra argument is now taught in the source. Excluding `p=3` is essential because there `-3=0` and `t=1` can satisfy the quadratic.

**`ex:onemod4`.** Pass A: if an odd prime `p|n²+1`, `-1` is a square modulo `p`, so Euler's criterion forces `p=1 mod4`. If finitely many such primes were `p_i`, let `m=2 product p_i` and `N=m²+1`. Then `N>1` is odd, every prime factor has the desired class, and `N=1 mod p_i` rules out all listed primes. Pass B derives the restriction directly from Chapter 5: `n²=-1 mod p` implies `n⁴=1` but `n²!=1`, so `ord_p(n)=4` and `4|p−1`. The same Euclid construction works even for an empty alleged list (`m=2`), and the Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic supplies at least one odd prime factor of `N`. This does not assert that every `1 mod4` prime occurs as a factor for a fixed `n`.

**`ex:minus2`.** Pass A: `(-2/p)=(-1)^((p−1)/2+(p²−1)/8)` by the two supplementary laws, with sign `+` only for `p=1,3 mod8`. For `p=x²+2y²` odd, if `p|y`, then `p|x`, making `p²|p`, impossible. Thus `y` is invertible modulo `p` and `(xy^(−1))²=-2`, forcing the listed classes. Pass B checks the four odd prime classes modulo eight independently: the signs of `-1` and `2` are `(+, +),(-,-),(+,-),(-,+)` at `1,3,5,7`, giving the same positive classes. For the representation conclusion, direct mod-eight arithmetic gives `x` odd when `p` is odd; if `y` is even, `x²+2y²=1 mod8`, and if `y` is odd it is `3 mod8`. This route does not divide by `y` and independently proves the necessary class condition. The prompt treats `p=2` separately and claims no converse representation theorem.

## DEP-037 — next three Chapter 6 exercises (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Independently checked the three prompts, hints, ideas, full derivations and boundary cases below. The Pépin solution now explicitly proves that `3` is a unit for every stated `F_n` before invoking its order, and includes a separate contradiction from any proper prime factor. The expanded finite Chapter 6 regression covers the relevant small primes and Fermat numbers; it does not replace either general argument.

**`ex:mersfactor`.** Pass A: if `p=3 mod4` and `q=2p+1` is prime, then `q=7 mod8`. The supplementary law gives `(2/q)=1`; Euler's criterion then says `2^((q−1)/2)=2^p=1 mod q`. For `p=11,23,251`, the candidate factors `q=23,47,503` are prime (trial division through square root suffices) and are strictly smaller than the corresponding Mersenne values, so the three latter are composite. Pass B uses the supplementary law as a *square-existence* statement: take `s²=2 mod q`, then Fermat gives `2^p=s^(q−1)=1 mod q`. This avoids interpreting the Legendre sign via Euler's power. The `p=3,q=7` boundary yields equality `2³−1=q`, so the example's compositeness claim correctly starts at the listed larger exponents.

**`ex:consecutive`.** Pass A: for nonzero `a`, `χ(a(a+1))=χ(1+a^(−1))`; inversion and translation permute the nonzero residues onto all field elements except `1`. The complete character sum is zero, so the displayed sum is `−1`. For `1<=a<=p−2`, both `a,a+1` are nonzero, so their square-pair indicator is `(1+χ(a))(1+χ(a+1))/4`. Summing gives `(p−2−χ(−1)−1−1)/4=(p−4−χ(−1))/4`. Pass B uses no inversion: set `t=2a+1` and note `χ(a(a+1))=χ(t²−1)`, as four is a square. The equation `y²=t²−1` is equivalent to `(t−y)(t+y)=1`. Every nonzero `u=t−y` uniquely determines `v=u^(−1)=t+y`, hence a unique pair `(t,y)` because two is invertible. There are `p−1` pairs, while counting by `t` gives `p+sum_t χ(t²−1)`, so the character sum is `−1`. This includes the zero terms at `a=0,−1` automatically. Re-expanding the square-pair indicators with those endpoints removed gives the same exact count; for `p=3` it is zero.

**`ex:pepin`.** Pass A: for `n>=1`, `F_n=2^(2^n)+1` is `1 mod4` and `2 mod3`. If it is prime, reciprocity gives `(3/F_n)=(F_n/3)=(2/3)=−1`, and Euler's criterion gives the required congruence. Conversely the congruence implies `3` is a unit (`F_n=2 mod3`), `3^(F_n−1)=1`, and `3^((F_n−1)/2)!=1`. Since `F_n−1` is a power of two, the exact order is `F_n−1`; it is at most `φ(F_n)<=F_n−1`, so every smaller positive integer is coprime to `F_n`, proving primality. Pass B checks the prime-direction residues as `F_n=5 mod12` and uses the prime supplementary/reciprocity rules directly. For the converse, take any prime divisor `q|F_n`; reducing the proposed congruence modulo `q` gives order exactly `F_n−1` in `F_q^×`, so `F_n−1|q−1`, forcing `q>=F_n`. Thus `F_n` has no proper prime factor, without using the totient equality. `n=1` gives `F_1=5`; `F_5=4294967297` is composite and fails the criterion, consistent with the theorem.

## DEP-038 — residue chance, small symbols and primitive-root sums (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Reconstructed the three complete prompts, hints, solutions and earlier theorem/exercise dependencies. The focused regression independently enumerates small Legendre distributions and pairs, the requested symbols, primitive-root power sums and Ramanujan values. No new source assertion was needed in this batch.

**`ex:t1-residue-chance`.** Pass A: exactly one input has Legendre value zero; by Euler's criterion, each nonzero sign occurs `(p−1)/2` times. Translation `U->U+1` preserves the uniform law, so `X,Y` each have probabilities `1/p,(p−1)/(2p),(p−1)/(2p)` at `0,+1,−1` respectively. Their means are zero and variances `E[X²]=E[Y²]=(p−1)/p`. Multiplicativity gives `XY=χ(U(U+1))`; `ex:consecutive` gives the complete sum `−1`, so `Cov(X,Y)=−1/p`. Since independent variables with zero means have zero covariance, they are not independent. Pass B counts the joint nonzero sign pairs directly from the indicator products `(1±χ(a))(1±χ(a+1))/4` for `1<=a<=p−2`, keeping the two zero endpoints separate. Using the independently verified hyperbola sum from DEP-037, the `(++)` count is `(p−4−χ(−1))/4`, the `(+-)` count `(p−χ(−1))/4`, and each of `(-+)` and `(--)` has `(p−2+χ(−1))/4`. These four counts sum to `p−2`; multiplying sign pairs gives total `−1` after the zero endpoints are added. This independently checks the covariance and shows dependence for every odd `p`, including `p=3`. The finite uniform probability, expectation and variance definitions are actual Volume 1 content recorded in the crosswalk.

**`ex:t1-res11`.** Pass A: squaring `0,1,...,5` gives exactly `{0,1,3,4,5,9}` modulo eleven. The `2` law gives `(2/13)=−1`, `(2/17)=+1`; reciprocity gives `(3/13)=(13/3)=+1` and `(3/17)=(17/3)=−1`. Complete multiplicativity makes both `(6/13)` and `(6/17)` equal `−1`. Pass B independently lists all square classes modulo eleven and thirteen (six is absent modulo thirteen), then checks Euler powers: `6²=10 mod13`, `6⁴=9 mod13`, `6⁶=−1 mod13`; `6²=2 mod17`, `6⁴=4 mod17`, `6⁸=−1 mod17`. These checks confirm both symbols without relying on the reciprocity-sign arithmetic. Zero is included in the residue list as the prompt requires.

**`pb:primsum` (all three parts).** Pass A: let `m=p−1` and choose primitive root `γ`. Generators are exactly `γ^a` with `gcd(a,m)=1`. For each `d|m`, the `d`-order subgroup generated by `γ^(m/d)` partitions into exact-order `e|d` classes. Its complete `k`th-power sum is `d` if `d|k` and zero otherwise, by the field geometric sum. Möbius inversion yields `C_m(k)=sum_(d|gcd(m,k))d μ(m/d)`, the previously checked Ramanujan sum. At `k=1`, this is `μ(m)`. For `p>3`, generators have order `m>2` and pair with distinct inverses, so their product is one. Pass B inserts the independent coprimality indicator `1_(gcd(a,m)=1)=sum_(d|gcd(a,m))μ(d)` into the primitive-root sum. Summing first over multiples `a=db` gives `m/d` if `(m/d)|k`, zero otherwise, and reindexing yields the same Ramanujan divisor formula without the subgroup-order partition. For the product, pair exponent classes `a` and `m−a`; when `m>2` and `gcd(a,m)=1` they are distinct, and each pair sums to `m`, so the total generator product is `γ` to a multiple of `m`, hence one. At `p=3`, the sole generator is `2=−1`, so the product restriction is necessary. `k=0` is also included in part (c), giving `φ(m)` on both sides.

## DEP-039 — revalidate the already checked finite-field exercise after a teaching simplification (4 November 2026)

**No increase in totals.** `pb:phimodp` already has a two-pass record at DEP-008. This continuation rechecked all three parts and shortened the rational irreducibility example: `(X+1)^4+1=X^4+4X^3+6X²+4X+2` satisfies Volume 1's Eisenstein criterion at two, and translation is reversible. The prior rational-quadratic comparison also remains mathematically valid. The existing `tools/test_volume2_cyclotomic_local.py` supplies the relevant finite regressions; no duplicate test file is added.

The general degree proof was reconstructed from the local quotient-field construction: `alpha` has exact order `n`, the nonzero-field product gives `ord_n(p)|d`, and Frobenius plus polynomial root count gives `d<=ord_n(p)`. Independently, Frobenius is injective and hence bijective on the finite field. The orbit of `alpha` has length `f=ord_n(p)`; the orbit product polynomial has coefficients fixed by Frobenius. Those coefficients lie in `F_p`, since `T^p−T` already has its maximum `p` roots there. Thus its degree gives `d<=f`, while the orbit's distinct roots of the minimal polynomial give `d>=f`. Squarefreeness follows from the derivative of `X^n−1` for `p∤n`.

The always-reducible example also has an independent explicit check: for odd primes, at least one of `−1,2,−2` is a square. The respective factorizations are `(X²+i)(X²−i)` for `i²=−1`, `(X²+sX+1)(X²−sX+1)` for `s²=2`, and `(X²+tX−1)(X²−tX−1)` for `t²=−2`. At two, use `(X+1)^4`. The index-divisible-by-characteristic identity follows either by the printed successive root-order identities or directly from `Phi_(p^a m)(X) Phi_m(X^(p^(a−1)))=Phi_m(X^(p^a))`, reducing by Frobenius and cancelling in `F_p[X]`. Both include `m=1`, `p=2`, and `a=1`.

## DEP-040 — Fermat-number factors and pairwise coprimality (4 November 2026)

**`pb:fermatnum`, all parts, Pass A.** If prime `p|F_n`, then `2^(2^n)=−1 mod p` and the exact order of two is `2^(n+1)`. For `n>=2`, this first forces `p=1 mod8`, so two is a square by the supplementary law. Euler's criterion then makes the order divide `(p−1)/2`, giving `p=1 mod 2^(n+2)`. Euler's two expressions for 641 give `5*2^7=−1` and `2^4=−5^4` modulo 641; raising the first to the fourth power and multiplying yields `2^32=−1`, without dividing `F_5`. The finite product `F_0...F_(n−1)=F_n−2` follows by difference of squares and induction. Hence common factors of distinct Fermat numbers divide two; since they are odd, they are pairwise coprime, giving infinitely many distinct prime divisors.

**Independent Pass B.** In the cyclic field-unit group, the square subgroup has size `(p−1)/2`; since two is a square and has exact order `2^(n+1)`, its order divides that subgroup size, yielding the extra factor two independently of the printed Euler-power step. Repeated squaring modulo 641 separately gives `2^16=154`, whose square is `−1` since `154²+1=37*641`, checking the numeric factor. For pairwise coprimality, if `m<n`, then `2^(2^m)=−1 mod F_m`; raising to the even exponent `2^(n−m)` gives `F_n=2 mod F_m` directly, independently of the product induction. Both include `F_0=3,F_1=5` in the coprimality statement; `n>=2` is needed only for the stronger factor progression. The source now writes `641=5*2^7+1` so its modulo-128 conclusion is immediate. The expanded finite Chapter 6 regression checks the product/coprimality identities and known small factor arithmetic; historical asides remain for the separate full history audit.

## DEP-041 — Carmichael numbers and Pratt certificates (4 November 2026)

**Scope.** Reconstructed all three parts of each prompt, hint, idea and solution. The Chernick prompt now specifies positive integer `k` and asks for the smallest example *in this family*. The previous wording could conflate its answer 1729 with the smallest Carmichael number 561. Three finite regressions pass; their samples supplement the general arguments below.

**`pb:carmichael`, Pass A.** For composite Carmichael `n`, the base `−1` excludes even `n>2`. If odd `p^v||n` with `v>=2`, choose a primitive root mod `p²` and a CRT base equal to it mod `p²` and one mod `n/p^v`; it remains a unit mod `n`. Its order `p(p−1)` would divide `n−1`, contradicting `p|n`. Thus `n` is squarefree. CRT with a primitive root mod each `p` then forces `p−1|n−1`. If `n=pq`, `p<q`, reducing `pq−1` mod `q−1` gives `p−1`, a positive number smaller than `q−1`, contradiction. Chernick's three distinct primes give a squarefree composite product and the expansion `n−1=36k(36k²+11k+1)`, divisible by each of `6k,12k,18k`; the previously checked Korselt sufficiency applies. At `k=1` all three factors are prime and give1729, and each factor strictly increases for positive integer `k`, proving minimality in this family.

**Independent Pass B.** Exclude repeated prime factors without prime-power primitive roots: if `p^v||n`, `v>=2`, choose by CRT a unit equal to `1+p^(v−1)` mod `p^v` and one mod the coprime remaining factor. Its `(n−1)`st power is `1+(n−1)p^(v−1) mod p^v`, since higher binomial terms vanish; this cannot be one because `p∤n−1`. The prime-field generator and CRT again force the necessary local divisibilities. Two prime factors are impossible by the independent size observation that the larger prime's `q−1` would divide the smaller positive `p−1`. For Chernick's family, directly reduce the product modulo `6k`, `12k`, `18k`: it becomes respectively `1`, `(6k+1)²=1 mod12k`, and `(6k+1)(12k+1)=1 mod18k`. Squarefree CRT and Fermat for each prime then prove the Carmichael congruence for every coprime base without invoking the criterion as a black box. Composite, odd, squarefree and positive-`k` hypotheses are all used.

**`pb:pratt`, Pass A.** A prime has a primitive-root witness. Conversely `a^(n−1)=1` makes `a` a unit and its order `e` divide `n−1`. If `e<n−1`, some prime divisor `q` of `(n−1)/e` gives `e|(n−1)/q`, contradicting the witness condition. Hence `e=n−1<=phi(n)<=n−1`, implying primality. For the tree bound, separate the mandatory child two. If there are `r>=1` odd children, their product is at most `(p−1)/2`; induction gives `f(p)<=2+2log2((p−1)/2)−r<=2log2(p)−1`. With no odd children `f(p)=2`, which satisfies the bound for `p>=3`. At most `2k` tree nodes have at most `k` binary digits. Each node's claimed factorization is multiplied back and its distinct prime children recursively verified; at most `k+1` modular powers, each using `O(k)` multiplications, yield `O(k³)` arithmetic operations overall.

**Independent Pass B.** The witness conditions force full order by the same maximal-proper-divisor test, but primality follows directly by counting distinct residues: `1,a,...,a^(n−2)` are `n−1` distinct units, so every nonzero residue is a unit and no proper prime factor can exist. For the tree bound, induct using *all* distinct children `q`, including two, where `f(2)=2log2(2)−1` exactly. With `r>=2` children, `f(p)+1<=2+sum_q(2log2(q)−1)=2log2(product q)+2−r<=2log2(p−1)<2log2(p)`. With one child, it must be two and `f(p)=2`, handled directly. This also counts repeated appearances in different branches, as the prompt's tree requires. The verification-cost argument checks complete factorization and recursively certified primality rather than assuming either; finding the factorization is outside the checking-cost claim. The stated complexity counts arithmetic operations on bounded-size integers, not unit-cost bit operations.

**Verification boundary.** `tools/test_volume2_ch06_carmichael_pratt.py` checks Carmichael definitions against the local criterion through 700 plus 1105/1729, Chernick families through `k=1000`, exhaustive Pratt witness existence through250 and tree sizes for primes through10000. These do not replace the general proofs. Totals **35/72 named results and 94/144 solutions**, leaving **37 named and 50 solutions** unchecked internally. ## DEP-042 — strong-liar count and squares modulo prime powers (4 November 2026)

**`pb:rabin`, Pass A.** In a cyclic group of even order `M`, the exponent congruence for `x^t=1` has `gcd(t,M)` solutions. The roots of `x^(2t)=1` not satisfying `x^t=1` map to the unique involution, so `x^t=−1` has `gcd(2t,M)−gcd(t,M)` solutions. In each odd prime-power unit group the odd part contributes `g_i=gcd(d,m_i)`; the factor `p_i^(e_i−1)` contributes none because `p_i∤n−1`. The minus-one equation at squaring stage `j` has `g_i*2^j` solutions exactly when `j<nu_i`. CRT multiplies component counts, and the stages are disjoint. Also `nu=min nu_i<=s` since every `p_i=1 mod2^nu`. Thus the count is `product g_i * (1+sum_(j<nu)2^(jr))`, exactly Monier's formula. For the bound divide by `phi(n)`: the odd-factor ratios are at most one, the excess 2-adic factor is at most one, and the normalized geometric factor is at most `2^(1−r)`. For `r>=3` this is at most1/4. For one prime power the ratio is at most `p^(1−e)`, at most1/5 except `n=9`. For two prime factors, unequal `nu_i` add a factor at most1/2; a repeated prime adds at most1/3. In the remaining squarefree equal-`nu` case, both odd ratios cannot equal one: otherwise each `p_i−1|n−1`, forcing the distinct primes equal by reducing `p_1p_2−1`. At least one proper odd-divisor ratio is at most1/3, giving at most1/6. The exception9 has2/6 liars;15 has2/8.

**Independent Pass B.** Split the exponent coordinate in each cyclic component into its odd-order part and its `2^nu_i` part using integer CRT. A strong liar's odd coordinate must be killed by odd `d`, giving `g_i` choices; its 2-coordinate is either identity in every component or has the same exact order `2^(j+1)` in all components. There are `2^j` such coordinates per component for `j<nu`, reproducing the formula by exact-order counting rather than subtracting two root kernels. Normalizing these independent coordinates gives the same three bound regimes. Independently reconstructing the two-prime exceptional case shows that if both odd parts divide `d`, then `p_2−1|p_1−1` and vice versa, impossible for distinct primes. All possible numbers of prime factors and repeated exponents are covered; the exclusion of9 is necessary. Exhaustive base counts through odd composite1000 corroborate the formula and bound.

**`pb:squaresmodpk`, Pass A.** A nonzero square modulo `p^k` has even valuation `2j<k` and a square unit part modulo `p^(k−2j)`, uniquely determined by the residue. Odd-prime unit groups have exactly half their units square, yielding the stated sum plus the zero class and limit `p/(2(p+1))`. Dyadic square units number `2^(m−3)` for `m>=3`, with one unit square for `m=1,2`; separating the boundary contributions in the finite valuation sum gives limit1/6. CRT gives exact multiplicativity of `S`. On products of distinct odd primes the square proportion is at most `(2/3)^r`, tending to zero by Euclid's infinitude. For an integer square modulo every modulus, any odd prime valuation in its absolute value is contradicted by the modulus `p^(v_p(|a|)+1)`. Thus its absolute value is `b²`. If it were negative, the modulus `4b²` forces a root divisible by `b`, then a square equal to−1 modulo4, impossible. Zero is handled separately.

**Independent Pass B and source improvement.** Split square residues into unit squares and those divisible by `p²`. Multiplication by `p²` bijects squares mod `p^(k−2)` onto the latter set, so `S(p^k)=U(p^k)+S(p^(k−2))`. For odd `p`, `U=phi(p^k)/2` gives the same finite formula and a contraction with limit `p/(2(p+1))`. For two and `k>=3`, `U=2^(k−3)` gives `u_k=1/8+u_(k−2)/4`, hence `u_k−1/6=(u_(k−2)−1/6)/4`. The bases `S(2)=S(4)=2` show convergence on both parity subsequences. The source now teaches this exact recurrence in place of the abbreviated error estimate. CRT square roots can be combined directly, independently confirming multiplicativity. The local–global conclusion uses only divisibility and FTA; the shrinking density alone would not imply it. Tests enumerate square sets for small prime powers and coprime products and check the recurrence exactly.

## DEP-043 — opening two-square results and first two exercises (4 November 2026)

**`lem:thue`.** Pass A: with `m=floor(sqrt(p))`, `(m+1)²>p` grid pairs give a collision of `u−av mod p`. Their differences satisfy `ax=y` and `|x|,|y|<=m<sqrt(p)`. If either difference vanished, invertibility of `a` and the size bound would force both to vanish, contradicting distinct pairs. Pass B: arrange the distinct residues `0,a,...,ma mod p` on a circle of circumference `p`. Their `m+1` positive gaps sum to `p`, so one is at most `p/(m+1)<sqrt(p)`. Its endpoints have distinct indices with difference `x` between `−m` and `m`; the circular gap is a nonzero integer `y` congruent to `ax`, including the wrapping gap. This independently gives both strict bounds, including `p=2`.

**`thm:twosq`.** Pass A: modulo four excludes an odd prime3mod4. For `p=1 mod4`, Euler's criterion supplies `a²=−1 mod p`; Thue gives nonzero small `x,y` with `y=ax`, so `x²+y²` is a positive multiple of `p` below `2p`, hence equals `p`. Pass B: take an integer representative `a` of the modular root with `|a|<p/2`. Then `a²+1=mp` with `0<m<p`; choose the least positive multiplier `m` represented as `mp=x²+y²`. If `m>1`, reduce `x,y` to balanced residues `r,s mod m`, each of absolute value at most `m/2`. Their norm is `mt` with `0<=t<=m/2<m`. It is nonzero: if both residues vanished, `m²|mp` would force `m|p`, impossible for `1<m<p`. Both `xr+ys` and `xs−yr` are divisible by `m`; the two-square identity gives `pt=((xr+ys)/m)²+((xs−yr)/m)²`, contradicting minimality. Thus `m=1`. This descent uses only earlier integer divisibility and norm identities, without Gaussian factorization.

**`thm:twosqn`.** Pass A: the product identity combines representations of2, primes1mod4, and square factors. Conversely a prime `q=3 mod4` dividing a represented number must divide both coordinates, since otherwise their quotient would square to−1 mod q; removing `q²` repeatedly proves even valuation. Pass B: for a nonzero represented integer set `t=min(v_q(x),v_q(y))`, interpreting a zero coordinate's valuation as infinite. After removing `q^(2t)`, at least one coordinate is a unit, so the remaining norm cannot vanish mod q by the −1 criterion. Hence the valuation is exactly `2t`, independently establishing necessity. For sufficiency, explicitly multiply the Gaussian-coordinate pairs for the allowed prime factors using the already expanded two-square identity; `n=1` uses `(1,0)` and zero coordinates cause no exception.

**`ex:three`.** Pass A: squares modulo eight are0,1,4, and no triple sums to7. If the represented number is divisible by four, all three coordinates must be even because a sum of at most three ones cannot equal zero mod4 unless it has none. Repeatedly divide by four to reach the forbidden7mod8. Pass B separately enumerate the27 residue triples and the eight parity triples; the latter has only `(0,0,0)` giving zero mod4. Therefore every presumed representation of `4^a(8b+7)` would have all coordinates divisible by `2^a`, contradicting the first table after division. Includes `a=0,b=0`; no converse three-square theorem is assumed.

**`ex:2026`.** Pass A: `1013=22²+23²`, and the product identity gives `2026=1²+45²`. The source's six displayed factorizations for2021,2022,2023,2024,2028,2030 each have an odd exponent at a prime3mod4. Also2027=3mod4. The three successes are `2025=45²+0²`, `2026=45²+1²`, `2029=45²+2²`. Pass B independently enumerate all possible square coordinates up to `floor(sqrt(2030))=45`, obtaining precisely those three successes, and directly multiply every excluded factorization. The primality assertion for1013 is verified by trial division through31; no primality claim about2027 is needed.

## DEP-044 — primitive and weighted two-square exercises; triangular-number dependency repair (4 November 2026)

**`ex:primitive2`.** Pass A: if four divides the norm, both coordinates are even by square residues mod4, contradicting gcd1. A prime3mod4 dividing the norm must divide both coordinates by the −1 nonsquare argument, again impossible. Pass B: the exact minimum-coordinate valuation proof in DEP-043 gives `v_q(x²+y²)=2 min(v_q(x),v_q(y))` for such primes. Primitive coordinates have minimum zero, so none divides the norm. For two, primitive coordinates are either both odd (norm2mod4) or opposite parity (odd norm), independently excluding4. Zero coordinates cause no exception because gcd1 then forces the other to be±1.

**`ex:x2y2`.** Pass A: the −2 criterion supplies a unit modular root for the stated odd primes. Thue produces `0<y²+2x²<3p` divisible by `p`, hence `p` or `2p`. In the latter case `y=2z` and division gives `p=x²+2z²`. Pass B: use the identity `(x²+2y²)(r²+2s²)=(xr+2ys)²+2(xs−yr)²`. A balanced root `a²=−2 mod p` yields a represented multiple `mp=a²+2` with `0<m<p`. Choose the least positive represented multiplier. If `m>1`, balanced remainders `r,s` of its coordinates modulo `m` give `r²+2s²=mt` with `0<t<=3m/4<m`; zero would imply `m|p`, impossible. The displayed identity, with both new coordinates divisible by `m`, then represents `pt`, contradiction. Thus `m=1`. Includes `p=3` and needs no unique factorization in a new ring.

**`ex:x3y2`.** Pass A: for prime1mod3 the earlier −3 exercise supplies the root; Thue gives a positive norm below4p, so its multiple is1,2 or3. The first succeeds; in the third, `3|y` and division transforms `3p=y²+3x²` to `p=x²+3(y/3)²`. The second is impossible because an even norm forces same coordinate parity, in either case giving zero mod4 rather than `2p`. Pass B independently reconstructs the allowable multiplier list and modular reductions: odd `p>3`, `y²+3x²` even forces `x=y mod2`, while squares mod4 give the exclusion. For multiplier3, squares mod3 force exactly the coordinate division used, with no division of a residue class mistaken for integer divisibility. Exhaustive small-prime weighted-square searches corroborate both weighted exercises but do not replace their general proofs.

**`ex:eureka` redesign and both checks.** Old obligation: admit the unproved full three-square theorem, then conclude every nonnegative integer is three triangular numbers. That violates plan requirement3: replace/relocate a problem whose required theorem is not proved on its route. New obligation under the same stable key: prove the exact equivalence between `n=T_r+T_s+T_t` and `8n+3` being three integer squares, prove the latter coordinates must be odd, and exhibit all n0…10. The universal existence claim has been removed, not certified. Pass A: expand `8T_k+1=(2k+1)²` for the forward direction. A square triple totaling3mod8 can only have residues1,1,1, so positive absolute coordinates are `2r+1,2s+1,2t+1`, giving the reverse direction after subtracting3 and dividing8. Pass B uses the inverse map `r=(|a|−1)/2` for each odd coordinate, an integer≥0, and checks that its triangular value is `(a²−1)/8`; the three values sum exactly to n. The examples use triangular values0,1,3,6,10: explicit triples cover each n0…10, with `9=T_3+T_2+T_0` yielding `75=7²+5²+1²`. The prompt, idea and full solution now agree; the existing hint still applies. This is an equivalence exercise, and its solution explicitly distinguishes equivalence from universal existence.

## DEP-045 — four-square existence, disc count and final Chapter 7 exercises (4 November 2026)

**`thm:foursq`, Pass A.** Expand Euler's displayed four bilinear coordinates; the norm product holds. Hence FTA reduces positive integers to primes, with2 represented directly. For odd prime `p`, the two residue sets of squares and negatives of one-plus-squares each have `(p+1)/2` members, so intersect. This gives `mp=x²+y²+1` with `0<m<p`. Choose the least represented positive multiplier. If even, pair same-parity coordinates and use integer half-sums/half-differences to represent `(m/2)p`, contradiction. For odd `m>1`, balanced residues `y_i` have norm `mr` with `0<=r< m`. Zero would force `m|p`, impossible for `1<m<p`. Euler's bilinear coordinates of `(x_i),(y_i)` all vanish mod m: the first is the old norm and the other three cancel in pairs. Dividing each coordinate by m yields a representation of `rp` with `0<r<m`, contradiction. Thus m1 and every prime, then every positive integer, is represented.

**Independent Pass B.** Treat the four displayed forms as the matrix with rows `(a1,a2,a3,a4)`, `(-a2,a1,-a4,a3)`, `(-a3,a4,a1,-a2)`, `(-a4,-a3,a2,a1)`. Its rows are pairwise orthogonal with common squared norm `sum a_i²`; hence its transpose-product gives the identity for arbitrary real, in particular integer, coordinates. The new exact formal-monomial regression independently expands all terms and confirms all mixed coefficients cancel. Reconstruct the descent's exceptional cases separately: an even represented norm has0,2 or4 odd coordinates, so same-parity pairing is always possible; an odd multiplier has strict residue bounds `(m−1)/2`, so `r< m`, not merely `r<=m`. If all residues vanished, divisibility by m² contradicts m<p. The norm divides by m² while each coordinate divides by m; these factors are checked independently. The seed bound `2((p−1)/2)²+1<p²` holds also at p3. n1 is represented and n0 has the separate trivial zero representation. No quaternion theorem is required.

**`thm:circle`.** Pass A: the half-open unit squares with lower-left corners at counted lattice points are disjoint and have total area N(R). They fit in the radius `R+sqrt2` disc and cover the radius `max(0,R−sqrt2)` disc by flooring coordinates. Expanding these areas gives the source bound for `R>=sqrt2`; for `1<=R<sqrt2` its proposed lower bound is negative and automatic. Division by `pi R²` proves the asymptotic. Pass B uses the half-open squares *centered* at lattice points. Every point is at most `delta=sqrt2/2` from its square center, so for all R>=1 their union contains the radius `R−delta` disc and lies within `R+delta`. This independently gives the stronger error bound `sqrt2*pi*R+pi/2`, implying the printed bound. It also checks no integer-radius restriction is needed. Finite quarter-step radii through100 satisfy the stronger bound; direct count N(10)=317.

**`ex:zagier`, all parts.** Pass A: positivity bounds x²<p and yz<p/4, so S is finite. The boundaries x=y−z and x=2y would make p a square or a multiple of4, impossible. Each branch has positive image coordinates and preserves x²+4yz. The first and third branches exchange, and the second stays in the middle. Fixed points can only be middle, where x=y and `p=x(x+4z)` forces `x=y=1,z=(p−1)/4`, a valid unique point. Pairing other points makes |S| odd. Swapping y,z is another involution and therefore has a fixed point, representing `p=x²+(2y)²`. Pass B explicitly checks branch images before composing: first gives x'>2y'; third gives x'<y'−z' because z>0; middle gives y'−z'<x'<2y' because z,x>0. Composition first→third returns `(x,y,z)` by direct substitution, as does third→first; middle squared has x''=x,z''=z. Independent norm expansions cancel all mixed terms. The proposed fixed point lies strictly in the middle since z>0,p>=5. These verifications establish closure, inverse and fixed-point assertions without assuming them from a finite sample. Exhaustive S through primes1mod4 below1000 corroborates every branch and unique fixed point.

**`ex:t1-which20`.** Pass A:15 has a forbidden factor3 of odd exponent;18,45,50 have only even exponents at primes3mod4. The source's equalities `18=3²+3²`, `45=3²+6²`, `50=5²+5²=7²+1²` check directly. Pass B enumerate coordinates through7 and obtain no pair for15 and the listed pairs for the other three. No converse primitive-representation claim is imported.
