## DEP-046 — Pythagorean parametrization, fourth-power descent and three opening exercises (4 November 2026)

**`thm:pyth`.** Pass A: expand the formulas; coprimality and opposite parity make all coordinates positive and primitive. Conversely primitive positive triples have exactly one even leg. With y even, `(y/2)²=((z−x)/2)((z+x)/2)` has positive coprime integer factors, so FTA makes both squares. Their roots m>n≥1 are coprime, and odd z forces opposite parity. Pass B uses the rational slope `t=y/(z+x)=n/m` in lowest terms, with0<t<1. The circle equation yields `x/z=(m²−n²)/(m²+n²)` and `y/z=2mn/(m²+n²)`. The integer numerator triple has gcd1 for opposite parity and gcd2 if both parameters are odd (no odd prime can divide all coordinates). In the latter case its reduced even-leg candidate is `mn`, odd, contradicting y even in the primitive original. Thus parameters have opposite parity and the normalized primitive triple is exactly the printed one. Zero legs are outside the stated positive domain.

**`thm:fermat4`.** Pass A: minimize positive z; a common prime of x,y would divide z twice and permit a smaller solution, so the square-leg triple is primitive. Parametrization gives `x²=m²−n²,y²=2mn,z=m²+n²`. With x odd, the second primitive triple `(x,n,m)` forces n even and gives `n=2ab,m=a²+b²`, with gcd(a,b)=1. Now `(y/2)²=mab`; these factors are pairwise coprime since a,b divide n and gcd(m,n)=1. All are positive squares, say `a=u²,b=v²,m=w²`, giving `u⁴+v⁴=w²` with0<w<z, contradiction. Pass B splits factors earlier: n even,m odd and `y²/4=m(n/2)` first give `m=u²,n=2v²`. The primitive triple `(x,2v²,u²)` has parameters a,b with `v²=ab`. Coprimality makes `a=r²,b=s²`, so `u²=r⁴+s⁴`. This is again a positive original-shape solution with u<z, independently reconstructing the integer divisions and decreasing measure. The claimed absence of positive fourth-power solutions follows by replacing the right-hand square by a fourth power; no assertion about zero coordinates is made.

**`ex:areaper`.** Pass A: substitute `c=ab/2−a−b` into Pythagoras; dividing by positive ab yields `(a−4)(b−4)=8`. Negative factor pairs force one nonpositive leg; positive pairs1,8 and2,4 give5,12,13 and6,8,10, both checking area=perimeter directly. Pass B write a scaled primitive triple with parameters m,n and scale k. Area divided by semiperimeter gives inradius `kn(m−n)`; area=perimeter forces inradius2. Factor `kn(m−n)=2` over positive integers: n1,m−n1,k2 gives6,8,10; n2,m−n1,k1 gives5,12,13; n1,m−n2,k1 has both parameters odd and is excluded. This independently exhausts the possibilities.

**`ex:x2y23z2`.** Pass A: a nonzero integer solution can be made primitive. Mod3 forces x,y divisible by3; substituting back forces z divisible by3, contradiction. Nonzero rational points clear a common nonzero denominator to such a solution. Pass B: z0 forces x=y0 immediately. Otherwise the q3mod4 norm argument makes `v₃(x²+y²)` even, while `v₃(3z²)` is odd, contradiction independent of primitive descent. Signed coordinates and a zero individual leg are covered.

**`ex:catalan23`.** Pass A: y0 gives x1. For y≥1, mod3 forces x=2m≥2. The positive factors `2^m−1,2^m+1` of3^y differ by2 and cannot both be divisible by3, so must be1,3. Thus(x,y)=(2,1). Pass B: if x≥3, reduction mod8 makes `2^x−3^y` either5 or7, never1. Check x0,1,2 directly and obtain precisely(1,0),(2,1), including the zero-exponent boundary. Three finite regressions corroborate primitive-triple completeness through hypotenuse500, small fourth-power searches, the triangle and exponent examples. They do not prove the infinite assertions.

## DEP-047 — remaining Diophantine results and exercises (4 November 2026)

**Source repairs.** Resolved DEP-046's queued example after the fixed-source build completed: `(8,6,10)=2*(4,3,5)`, with the same-parity parameters explicitly outside the primitive theorem. The chord explanation now handles tangent and missing second affine intersections; the circle caption excludes the known base point and counts coincident Markov children once. The no-rational-point example now checks both parities of the denominator. Mordell prose no longer invents an odd-valuation requirement for a prime dividing `y²+1`: it would have to divide1. The120-degree triangle solution now identifies its tangent parameter and proves triangle inequalities before invoking the cosine rule.

**`prop:chord`.** Pass A: restriction to `x=x0+s,y=y0+ts` gives `s(alpha s+beta)` over Q. It cannot vanish identically, since division by the rational line polynomial would make that line a factor of Q, excluded. A distinct second finite root, if present, is `−beta/alpha` rational. Every other rational point determines a rational slope or the vertical line. Pass B choose affine rational coordinates with that line as one coordinate axis; polynomial division separates its line factor from a remainder in the line parameter. A zero remainder would be exactly an excluded component. Otherwise the degree-at-most-two nonzero restriction has the known rational root, and division leaves degree at most one over Q. This independently handles degree drop, double tangent root, vertical lines and even singular conics; a distinct second point is not guaranteed. The source proposition was correct, but the former teaching paragraph overstated existence and is now conditional.

**`thm:mordell7`.** Pass A: even x gives y²=7mod8. For odd x, `y²+1=(x+2)((x−1)²+3)`; the second factor is positive3mod4, hence has a prime3mod4 divisor. Such a prime cannot divide `y²+1`, since it would make−1 square. Pass B use the earlier result that every odd prime dividing an integer norm `y²+1` is1mod4. Therefore each positive odd divisor of that norm is1mod4; the displayed quadratic factor is instead3mod4. This gives the contradiction without any valuation or coprimality assertion about the two factors. All signed x are covered by congruences and the strictly positive quadratic factor.

**`thm:markov`.** Pass A: the Vieta mate `x'=3yz−x=(y²+z²)/x` is a positive integer, preserves the equation and is an involution. Sort x≥y≥z. The quadratic at y is `2y²+z²−3y²z<=z²−y²<=0`. If negative, y lies strictly between its two roots, so0<x'<y<=x. Equality forces y=z=1 and x2 unless the root triple1,1,1; its mate is1. Thus the positive coordinate sum decreases until the root and reversibility classifies all solutions. Pass B split the sign check: if z≥2, the quadratic at y is at most `z²−4y²<0`; if z1 and y≥2 it is `1−y²<0`; if y=z1 the equation is `(x−1)(x−2)=0`. This independently checks all boundary cases and the unique terminal triple. Integer positivity follows directly from the root product, and well ordering supplies finite termination.

**`ex:mordell5`.** Pass A: even x and x3mod4 are impossible mod4, leaving x1mod4. Factor `y²+4=(x−1)(x²+x+1)`; the quadratic factor is positive3mod4, so has a prime3mod4 divisor q. Such a q dividing the norm must divide y and2, impossible. Pass B observe y is even in the surviving class and `(y/2)²+1` has only odd prime divisors1mod4. Multiplying by4 introduces no new odd prime divisor, so the odd positive divisor `x²+x+1` cannot be3mod4. This independently checks signed inputs and avoids relying on an exact valuation of q.

**`ex:eisentri`.** Pass A: the line through(−1,0) factors the equation and gives `x=(1−t²)/D,y=(2t+t²)/D,D=1+t+t²`. D>0 for all rational t. The vertical line adds(−1,1), and the known point occurs at t−2. Positive x,y are exactly0<t<1, so reduced t=n/m with0<n<m gives the displayed integer triple, normalized by its gcd and then scaled by positive integers. Pass B recover t directly as y/(x+1) for x≠−1 and substitute the conic equation to check both formulae; at x−1 the equation gives exactly y0 or1. This establishes completeness including exceptions independently of factoring the substituted quadratic. A rational scale of a primitive integer triple is integral only if its reduced denominator divides all coordinates, hence is1. For positive legs the norm equation gives `max(a,b)<c<a+b`, so a triangle exists and its cosine is−1/2. Finite rational-point enumeration checks the inverse formula at small denominators.

**`ex:markovcoprime`.** Pass A: common gcd is preserved under each reversible move and permutations; the root has gcd1. A prime dividing two coordinates divides the third by the equation, so pairwise gcds are1. The strictly descending largest-coordinate parent makes bounded tree enumeration complete. Pass B each pairwise gcd itself is invariant: `gcd(3yz−x,y)=gcd(x,y)` and similarly for z, while the untouched pair is unchanged. Starting at the root proves pairwise coprimality directly. Independently solve the quadratic for every ordered pair up to200 and compare with all bounded tree moves; both give exactly the nine printed triples, with no unexplored bounded branch.

**`ex:imo88`.** Pass A: fix a nonsquare positive integer k and minimize a+b with a≥b. The other root `a'=kb−a` is integral. The quadratic satisfies f(−1)>0 and f(b)<0 for k≥2, so its smaller root lies in(−1,b), hence is an integer between0 and b−1. Zero would force k=b²; otherwise it is positive and gives a smaller original-shape solution, contradiction. Pass B exclude a negative mate algebraically: if a'=−h with h≥1, the root sum gives a=kb+h and the product gives `b²+h²=k(1−bh)`, impossible because the left is positive and right nonpositive. Again zero forces k=b². For a positive mate, `a'=(b²−k)/a<b²/a<=b`, giving strict descent without the printed root-interval argument or any unproved bound on k. The denominator of the new ratio is positive, so it has the same integer k.

**`ex:t1-triple`.** Pass A gives(3,4,5),(15,8,17),(5,12,13), with direct norm checks. Pass B invert each using `(z+x)/2=m²,(z−x)/2=n²`, recovering4/1,16/1,9/4 respectively and the stated positive opposite-parity coprime parameters. Every prompt and hint agrees with its full solution.

## DEP-048 — continued-fraction foundations and first exercise (4 November 2026)

**`prop:cfconv`.** Pass A: induct by combining the final entries `a+1/t`, giving the tail fraction formula, with n1 checked directly. The determinant changes sign under the two recurrences, starting D0=−1; integer coefficients therefore give coprime convergents and the adjacent-difference formula. Positive integer digits give `q_n>=q_(n−1)+q_(n−2)` and Fibonacci growth, with q0=q1=1 allowed. Pass B multiply matrices `M(a)=[[a,1],[1,0]]`: the product through a_n is `[[p_n,p_(n−1)],[q_n,q_(n−1)]]`. Its determinant is the product of n+1 factors−1, and composition of the associated fractional-linear functions gives the general tail formula independently of the printed induction. The determinant argument works for real digits; gcd claims require integer digits, exactly as stated. All denominators are positive under the stated tail hypotheses.

**`thm:cfapprox`.** Pass A: substitute the complete quotient x_(n+1) into the tail formula and subtract p_n/q_n. The numerator is the opposite adjacent determinant, giving sign(−1)^n and denominator `q_n(x_(n+1)q_n+q_(n−1))`. Since the irrational complete quotient lies strictly between a_(n+1) and a_(n+1)+1, its second factor lies strictly between q_(n+1) and q_(n+1)+q_n. This proves both strict bounds, alternation and convergence. Pass B compare the fractional-linear map `F_n(t)=(tp_n+p_(n−1))/(tq_n+q_(n−1))` at its two integer endpoints and its limit at infinity. Cross-multiplication using determinant±1 fixes its orientation and the exact distance to p_n/q_n; inserting the actual tail strictly between the endpoints yields the same two bounds without a rounded numerical estimate. Includes n0 with q_(−1)=0, negative a0, and the possible equality q0=q1.

**`lem:cfexistence`.** Pass A: the two-step adjacent difference has sign(−1)^n, so even convergents strictly increase and odd ones strictly decrease; their positive gap tends to zero by denominator growth. Each tail has a similar finite limit. Passing through reciprocals gives `x_k=a_k+1/x_(k+1)`; every following tail is strictly greater than1, so floors recover all digits. The algorithm is infinite, and a rational would terminate by Euclid, so the limit is irrational. Uniqueness follows from the common convergents. Pass B take the closed prefix interval with endpoints `p_n/q_n` and `(p_n+p_(n−1))/(q_n+q_(n−1))`, corresponding to next real tail from1 to infinity. Matrix composition makes these intervals nested, of exact length `1/(q_n(q_n+q_(n−1)))`, tending to zero. Their unique common point is not any convergent: the next interval strictly excludes that endpoint. If it were A/B rational, its distance to every different p_n/q_n would be at least `1/(Bq_n)`, eventually larger than the prefix interval length, contradiction. Applying the same interval construction to each tail and the continuous reciprocal identity recovers floors exactly. This gives a separate irrationality proof instead of simply invoking nontermination. Arbitrary infinite digits, including all ones, are covered.

**`ex:cf415`.** Pass A: Euclid gives digits4,2,6,7 and convergents4/1,9/2,58/13,415/93. The last determinant is `415*13−93*58=1`; every solution is `(13+93k,58+415k)`, k integer. Pass B independently back-substitute `1=43−6*7=13*43−6*93=13*415−58*93`, evaluate the nested fraction directly and check that subtraction of any two solutions gives `415Δx=93Δy`; coprimality forces precisely the stated family.

**Queued source repair, not an exercise verification.** `ex:cfunique` remains unreviewed: its part(a) says that because the following tail is greater than1, “the floor of that tail is exactly a_k.” The intended statement concerns the current tail: `x_k=a_k+1/x_(k+1)` implies floor(x_k)=a_k. Rewrite this explicitly, including irrationality, when the current fixed-source build completes. The named existence lemma itself already gives the correct argument. Do not count the exercise yet.

**Boundary.** Three exact finite regressions check matrix/tail arithmetic, strict sqrt2 bounds and nested intervals, plus the Bézout family. ## DEP-049 — best approximation, periodicity and Pell (4 November 2026)

**`lem:best`.** Pass A: consecutive convergent columns n,n+1 have determinant±1, hence form an integer basis. Express(p,q) with coefficients u,v. A zero u contradicts0<q<q_(n+1); zero v gives the bound directly. Otherwise u,v must have opposite signs under the denominator restriction, while the two convergent errors also have opposite signs. The two contributions to qx−p therefore reinforce, so its absolute value is at least the nth error. Pass B use the n,n−1 integer basis: `(p,q)=r(p_n,q_n)+s(p_(n−1),q_(n−1))`. The complete-quotient identity gives `epsilon_(n−1)=−x_(n+1)epsilon_n`, so a strictly smaller error would require `|r−s*x_(n+1)|<1`. If s0, r0 and q0, impossible. If s≥1, integrality and x_(n+1)>a_(n+1) force r≥s*a_(n+1), hence q≥q_(n+1). If s<=−1 the same reasoning forces q<0. This independently proves the bound including n0.

**`thm:legendre`.** Pass A choose the largest n with q_n<=q<q_(n+1), allowed even when q0=q1. The best-approximation lemma bounds the nth scaled error by the proposed fraction's error. If the fractions differ, their determinant is a nonzero integer, and the triangle inequality implies `2<q_n/q+1<=2`, contradiction. Pass B work directly with that determinant: `|p q_n−p_n q|<=q_n|p−qx|+q|p_n−q_nx|< (q_n+q)/(2q)<=1`. A nonzero integer cannot satisfy this. No reduced-fraction hypothesis is needed; identical values already give the conclusion.

**`thm:periodic`, Pass A.** A repeated complete quotient y is fixed by the period's integer fractional-linear map, yielding a nonzero quadratic with positive leading denominator coefficient. The manuscript proves Q(y) is a two-dimensional field, and the finite prefix puts irrational x in that field, hence in a rational quadratic relation. Conversely transform an integer quadratic f by the unimodular convergent matrix. The transformed integer coefficients A_n,C_n are values `q²f(p/q)` at consecutive convergents and have uniform bounds from the strict error estimate. The discriminant stays equal to the original positive nonsquare D under determinant±1. Thus B_n is bounded too; only finitely many coefficient triples and roots occur, so complete quotients repeat. The deterministic floor/reciprocal map then repeats forever.

**Independent Pass B.** For the forward direction invert the prefix map to write y as a fractional-linear function of x and substitute it into y's fixed-point polynomial, clearing its nonzero denominator. The resulting nonzero rational polynomial has degree at most2 (invertibility prevents it from being identically zero); irrationality rules out degrees0/1. This avoids the field-dimension argument. For the converse write `f=A(X−x)(X−x')`. The approximation bound gives `|q²f(p/q)|<|A|(|x−x'|+1)`, directly, independently of the source's delta expansion. For the binary quadratic matrix `S=[[A,B/2],[B/2,C]]`, a convergent substitution T gives `TᵀST` and unchanged determinant because det(T)=±1. Therefore the discriminant is unchanged, bounding B once A,C are bounded. Nonzero transformed A follows from f having no rational root. This separately establishes the finite-state argument for either real irrational root, with arbitrary finite preperiod.

**`thm:pell`, Pass A.** Infinitely many convergents to sqrt(d) give positive p,q with `|p−q sqrt(d)|<1/q` and bounded nonzero integer norms `p²−dq²`. One norm k occurs infinitely often; two distinct pairs with identical coordinates modulo |k| yield an integer quotient of their algebraic numbers, of norm1. Its sqrt(d) coefficient cannot be zero, since that would make the quotient±1 and the pairs equal up to sign, excluded by positivity. Sign and inversion produce a norm1 solution u>1 with positive integer coordinates. Choose the least positive y; monotonicity gives the least epsilon>1. Dividing any other positive solution by the appropriate integer power places it in[1,epsilon), where minimality forces1. Integer closure and the inverse x−y sqrt(d) then classify all solutions as ±epsilon^k, including k0.

**Independent Pass B, matrix existence.** By the now-checked periodicity theorem, the continued fraction of sqrt(d) has a finite prefix matrix P and a repeating period matrix M, both integral unimodular. If y is the repeated complete quotient, M fixes y with eigenvalue `mu=Qy+Q'>1`. Conjugating gives the integral unimodular matrix `T=P M P^(-1)` fixing sqrt(d), with the same eigenvalue. Write T as `[[a,b],[c,e]]`; its fixed-point equation implies `a=e,b=dc`, since1 and sqrt(d) are rationally independent. Thus `a²−dc²=det(T)=±1`, and `a+c sqrt(d)=mu>1`. The coefficient c cannot vanish, since that would force mu=±1. If the norm is−1, square the expression; the result is an integer norm1 unit>1, providing existence without bounded norms or pigeonhole on residue pairs. The positivity formulas `(u+u^(-1))/2` and `(u−u^(-1))/(2sqrt(d))` then independently check the positive coordinates. Reduction into[1,epsilon) proves exhaustiveness as above; all signs, reciprocal powers and y0 solutions are covered without importing a later unit-group theorem.

## DEP-050 — all remaining continued-fraction exercises (4 November 2026)

**Source repairs.** `ex:cfunique` now defines the current tail x_k and following tail x_(k+1), proves `x_k=a_k+1/x_(k+1)` and floor(x_k)=a_k, explicitly deduces irrationality from nontermination and uniqueness from a common convergent limit. This resolves the queued wrong-tail sentence and the former uniqueness shortcut. The golden-ratio solution cites the preceding Fibonacci-ratio result, expands the sqrt5 identity and handles unreduced fractions. Liouville's solution now exhibits the geometric tail bound and justifies choosing an irreducible least-degree polynomial. These changes reopen format validation after the successful df6df9bf build.

**`ex:cfunique`.** Pass A: strict even/odd nesting and shrinking adjacent gaps give a common limit for the expansion and every tail; reciprocal continuity and following tails>1 recover each floor. The resulting infinite algorithm cannot be rational by Euclid; common convergents give uniqueness. For all-one digits the positive fixed point satisfies x²−x−1=0, giving phi, and the integer recurrence gives Fibonacci numerators/denominators. Rationalization yields the two sqrt3 complete quotients `(sqrt3+1)/2` and `sqrt3+1`. Pass B apply the independently proved nested-prefix/rational-separation argument from DEP-048 for irrationality and uniqueness. Period matrices give the fixed-point polynomials directly: phi²−phi−1=0, and the tail `[1;2,1,2,...]` satisfies `2y²−2y−1=0`, whose admissible root gives outer value sqrt3. Initial values and the Fibonacci recurrence independently verify every convergent index.

**`ex:calendar`.** Pass A: exact target24219/100000 has CF `[0;4,7,1,3,24,6,2,2]`, whose first nonzero convergents are1/4,7/29,8/33,31/128,752/3105. The printed cycle interpretations and errors agree. Pass B independently divide100000 by24219, then successive remainders3124,2351,773,32,5,2,1, confirming every digit. Cross-products give exact annual errors `781/100000`, `773/3300000`, `31/100000`, `−1/400000` for Julian, idealized33-year, Gregorian and31/128 cycles. Hence the fixed-target one-day figures128.04 and400000 years follow without floating assumptions. The source correctly distinguishes the fixed decimal model from real changing year lengths and historical calendar adoption.

**`ex:n21`.** Pass A: for n≥1 the square root lies strictly between n and n+1. Its reciprocal remainder is sqrt(n²+1)+n, of integer part2n and the same fractional part, giving the stated period. The unit n+sqrt(n²+1) has norm−1; squaring gives coefficients2n²+1 and2n. Pass B solve the repeated-tail fixed-point equation `y=2n+1/y`; the positive root is n+sqrt(n²+1), and the outer value is the claimed square root by the verified infinite-existence lemma. Direct expansion proves the Pell identity for all n, independently of norm language. The problem asks for a solution, not a newly unproved fundamental-minimality claim.

**`ex:sqtri`.** Pass A: `m(m+1)/2=k²` is equivalent to `(2m+1)²−2(2k)²=1`. The full Pell classification gives every positive solution as a power of3+2sqrt2; recurrence preserves odd x/even y, and the inverse m=(x−1)/2,k=y/2 is integral. Add the trivial solution to include0. Pass B derive parity directly from the Pell equation modulo4: odd y would make x²3mod4, impossible; hence y even,x odd. Thus *every* positive Pell solution enters the inverse map, with no parity subfamily omitted. The first indices1,8,49,288 give square values1,36,1225,41616 by direct multiplication. Finite enumeration through index100000 agrees with all generated powers.

**`ex:golden`.** Pass A: the exact scaled error is `1/(phi+F_n/F_(n+1))`, tending to1/sqrt5 because previous convergents give the reciprocal Fibonacci ratio. For c>sqrt5, the stricter bound implies Legendre's condition, so reduced candidates are convergents; sufficiently large ones have scaled error>1/c. Reducing a fraction preserves the inequality, and each of the finitely many rational values admits only finitely many denominator multiples because its distance from phi is positive. Pass B derive Binet locally: phi and psi=−1/phi satisfy the Fibonacci characteristic equation, so `(phi^m−psi^m)/sqrt5` has initial values0,1 and the Fibonacci recurrence. Therefore the scaled error equals `(1−(−phi^(−2))^(n+1))/sqrt5`, independently proving the limit. For finiteness without Legendre, the nonzero integer `p²−pq−q²` gives `|p/q−phi|*|p/q−psi|>=1/q²`. In a sufficiently small neighborhood of phi the second factor is<c, excluding the proposed stronger bound. Outside that neighborhood, the bound forces q bounded, hence finitely many p. This also covers unreduced inputs directly.

**`ex:liouville`, both parts.** Pass A: an irreducible rational polynomial of degree≥2 has no rational root, so `q^dP(p/q)` is a nonzero integer. In distance≤1 of x, MVT gives `|P(p/q)|<=M|x−p/q|` with finite positive M; outside, choose c<=1. Thus c=min(1,1/M) works globally. For the factorial series, the tail is positive and bounded above by the geometric sum over *all* integer exponents starting at(n+1)!, namely `(10/9)10^(−(n+1)!)<2/q_n^(n+1)`. A rational L=A/B would be separated from each distinct truncation by at least1/(Bq_n), contradiction. If L were algebraic, a least-degree integer polynomial is irreducible over Q by factoring and clearing denominators; irrationality forces degree≥2. Comparing its Liouville bound with the tail would bound a fixed positive constant by a quantity tending to zero, contradiction. Pass B obtain the same global algebraic bound without calculus: factor `P(r)−P(x)=(r−x)sum_j a_j sum_(i<j)r^i x^(j−1−i)`. For |r−x|<=1 the second factor is bounded by `sum_j j|a_j|(|x|+1)^(j−1)`, a finite positive explicit constant. Independently, rational long division has finitely many remainders, hence an eventually periodic decimal. L's digits0/1 have unbounded zero gaps and infinitely many ones at factorial positions, so are not eventually periodic (nor an eventual9 alternate representation). This again excludes degree1 before the algebraic-bound argument. q need not be a reduced denominator.

**`ex:t1-pell3`.** Pass A rationalizes the two-period expansion and checks the first two convergents:1/1 has norm−2,2/1 has norm1. Pass B y1 is the smallest positive denominator possible and already gives x2, independently confirming fundamental minimality; its predecessor and period are also recovered by the fixed-point calculation above.

## DEP-051 — quadratic-ring units, division, factorization and first exercises (4 November 2026)

**`prop:quadunits`.** Pass A: a ring inverse forces two nonnegative integer norms to multiply to1; conversely conjugation supplies the inverse when norm1. Solve a²+b²=1, a²+2b²=1 and `(2a−b)²+3b²=4` to obtain respectively4,2,6 units. A prime norm cannot split into two nonunit integer norms, proving irreducibility. Pass B independently bound the possible coefficients of a norm-one element and multiply each listed element by its conjugate to verify inverse and completeness. Norm multiplicativity then checks that no unlisted unit with norm>1 is possible. The proposition does not assert that every irreducible has ordinary-prime norm.

**`prop:eucl`.** Pass A: a/b has rational coordinates in1,theta because it equals a*conjugate(b)/N(b). Round both coefficients to integers, leaving sigma,tau in[−1/2,1/2]. The three squared norms are bounded by1/2,3/4,3/4, all strictly below1, so r=a−bq has N(r)<N(b). Pass B write the two exact numerator coefficients over the positive integer denominator N(b), and use integer division to choose nearest integers, including ties. Maximize the three quadratic forms on that coefficient square: the Gaussian and rectangular forms have corner maxima1/2 and3/4; the Eisenstein cross term has maximum3/4 at opposite-sign corners. Complex norm scaling independently transfers these bounds to the remainder. Zero a and zero remainder are allowed; b0 is excluded. No assertion that coefficient rounding is the nearest Eisenstein point is needed.

**`thm:ufd`, all parts.** Pass A: choose a nonzero least-norm element d among combinations au+bv. Dividing any such combination by d leaves a smaller-norm combination, hence zero, proving aR+bR=dR, gcd divisibility and Bézout. If an irreducible pi does not divide a, its gcd with a is a unit; scale the Bézout relation to1 and multiply by b to prove pi prime. Norm multiplicativity and unit characterization give factorization by strong norm induction. Prime divisibility matches an irreducible in any second factorization, and domain cancellation proves uniqueness inductively; no leftover nonunit product can equal a unit because norms multiply. Pass B run the actual Euclidean chain: norms strictly decrease until the last nonzero remainder, common divisors propagate both ways, and back-substitution gives its explicit linear combination. This independently proves the gcd/Bézout assertions, including one zero input. Modulo an irreducible pi, each nonzero residue class has an inverse by this gcd relation; consequently a product zero has a zero factor, independently expressing primality. Match prime exponents in two irreducible lists by repeated cancellation to establish uniqueness. All stated general-domain hypotheses are used. The source now defines aR and aR+bR before the theorem, rather than leaving the first ideal-style notation implicit.

**`thm:six`.** Pass A: in Z[sqrt(−5)] the norm a²+5b² never equals2 or3. A proper factorization of norms4,9,6 would require precisely one of those norms, so2,3,1±sqrt(−5) are irreducible. Cross-side norms4/9 versus6 exclude associates. Pass B independently list all multiplicative partitions of4,9,6 into integers≥2 and rule them out by the coefficient bound (b≠0 gives norm≥5; b0 gives a square). Units have norm1 and are exactly±1; coefficient comparison also distinguishes the conjugates. Thus the two irreducible multisets for6 cannot be made equal by units or reordering. No later ideal factorization theorem is used in this proof.

**`ex:gaussfactor`.** Pass A: the displayed four factors for65 and the unit−i times four factors for2026 multiply exactly to those integers; each norm is an ordinary prime, so the factors are irreducible and hence Gaussian prime. Euclidean remainders for the gcd are2−4i,−1+2i,0 with descending norms20,5,0. Pass B multiply both factorizations independently in integer coordinate arithmetic. The gcd candidate g=−1+2i divides the inputs with quotients−1−5i and3−2i. It also satisfies the certificate `g=(1−i)(11+3i)+(1+2i)(1+8i)`, so every common divisor divides g; this proves the gcd without trusting rounding. The factor1013 was checked prime in the earlier two-square exercise. Units and associate ambiguity are retained explicitly.

**`ex:zsqrt5`.** Pass A: the norm partition and unit arguments above show all four irreducibles are pairwise nonassociate, including the two conjugates (neither is the other's negative). The two factorizations therefore contradict uniqueness. The element2 divides their product6 but neither factor, so is not prime. Pass B divisibility by2 in this ring requires both integer coefficients even; the candidates1±sqrt(−5) fail this directly. Independent norm partitions give irreducibility, while the complete unit list±1 gives the pairwise distinction. This also shows why a failed rounding estimate alone would not suffice to prove non-Euclidean behavior; the demonstrated UFD failure supplies the actual obstruction.

## DEP-052 — prime classification, r2 and Fermat's cube descent (4 November 2026)

**`thm:gaussprimes`.** Pass A: norm2 and split norm p give irreducibles; a prime3mod4 cannot split because a proper factor would have norm q, an impossible two-square representation. Split conjugates cannot be associates: a common factor of norm p would divide2a and2bi, forcing p|a,b and p²|p. Every Gaussian irreducible, being prime, divides an ordinary prime factor of its own norm; factoring that prime by the three cases exhausts the list. Pass B for q3mod4 construct `F_q[X]/(X²+1)`, a field by earlier polynomial Bézout because−1 is nonsquare. Thus q is prime directly. For split p, choose a²=−1 and a Gaussian gcd of p,a+i; Bézout excludes a unit gcd, coefficient divisibility excludes an associate of p, so its norm is p. This independently constructs the split factor. The explicit2 identity and UFD complete the classification. Finite trial division by all possible small-norm factors checks both Gaussian and Eisenstein classifications independently of their congruence criteria.

**`thm:r2`.** Pass A: unique factorization gives four unit choices; the exponent of1+i is fixed, the two conjugate exponents above each split p must sum to e (e+1 choices), and inert q has norm q² so requires even exponent. The divisor sum has the same local factors1,e+1 and alternating0/1 because χ4 is multiplicative. The source now proves that multiplicativity from the residue table before invoking convolution; no later character theory is required. Pass B derive local bijections: division by1+i bijects norm2n with norm n because equal coordinate parity is automatic; an inert q dividing a norm forces both coordinates divisible by q, so removal of q² preserves counts and odd exponents give zero. For split p, partition norm p^e*m (p∤m) by the valuation a at pi and e−a at its conjugate. Dividing those factors bijects each of the e+1 disjoint classes with norm-m elements. Starting from r2(1)=4 gives exactly the claimed local recurrence formula. Axis coordinates are covered and unit multiplication is free for nonzero z. Exact lattice enumeration through10000 agrees with the divisor-sum sieve, including r2(45)=8,r2(65)=16,r2(325)=24.

**`thm:eisprimes`.** Pass A: a primitive cube root u mod p1mod3 yields `(2u+1)²=−3`. In R this factors the ordinary prime divisor into two factors neither divisible by p; therefore p is not prime and, by the proved UFD implication, not irreducible. A proper factor has norm p. Conjugate associates would force p|3b² and p|(2a−b)², hence p|a,b (p≠2,3), contradiction. For inert q2mod3, norm q is impossible since `4N=(2a−b)²+3b²` is0/1mod3. The explicit3=unit*lambda² and prime-divisibility of norms exhaust the list. Mod lambda, omega=1 and3=0, giving every element a residue0,±1; they are distinct because lambda divides neither1 nor2 by norms. Pass B construct gcd(p,u−omega) instead, since N(u−omega)=u²+u+1 is divisible by p. Bézout and the nonintegral omega coefficients of its conjugate quotient exclude gcd unit/p, forcing norm p. The direct formula `N(a+b omega)=(a+b)² mod3` independently rules out inert norm q. The same norm divisibility/completeness argument then applies.

**`lem:cube`.** Pass A: choose a sign with ±theta=1+beta*lambda. Factoring its cube minus1 gives `lambda³*beta*(beta+1)*(beta−omega²)`; modulo lambda these are three successive residues in F3, so one contributes an extra lambda. Pass B expand binomially and use3=−omega²*lambda²: modulo lambda⁴ the difference is `lambda³*(beta³−omega² beta)`. Its parenthesis vanishes modulo lambda because beta³=beta in the three explicitly enumerated classes and omega²=1. Also lambda⁴=9omega², so its ideal equals(9); an exhaustive test of all81 integer-coordinate classes is a complete finite verification of this congruence, not merely a sample of complex elements. All54 classes not divisible by lambda have cube±1 mod9.

**`thm:flt3`, Pass A.** Primitive nonzero integer solutions are pairwise coprime, hence coprime also in R by integer Bézout. A lambda dividing an integer forces3 to divide that integer, so at most one coordinate has that factor. If none does, the cube lemma makes x³+y³−z³ equal to one of±1,±3 modlambda⁴, none divisible by lambda⁴ (v_lambda(3)=2). Otherwise remove the exact lambda power and permit an arbitrary unit on the right, yielding a family `alpha³+beta³=epsilon*lambda^(3n)*gamma³` with all three elements nonzero and lambda-free, gcd(alpha,beta)=1, n≥1. The cube congruence first forces n≥2. The three factors have pairwise differences lambda*beta times units; all are divisible by lambda, at most one by lambda². Their valuations must therefore be3n−2,1,1. Rotate beta by omega to put the high valuation in alpha+beta. Away from lambda any common prime would divide alpha,beta, so after removing the exact lambda powers the three kappas are pairwise coprime and lambda-free. UFD makes each a unit times a cube. The exact relation F0+omega*F1+omega²*F2=0 then gives `theta2³+eta theta3³=eta' lambda^(3(n−1))*theta1³`. Cube congruences modulo lambda² force eta=±1 among the six units, so its sign can be absorbed into the cube. The new pair is coprime, all elements nonzero/lambda-free and the positive exponent has decreased, contradicting minimal n.

**Independent Pass B audit.** Rebuild the local cube statement by the binomial method above rather than the factor argument. Independently check all four sign choices with each of the six units modulo lambda²=(3): divisibility of ±1±eta occurs only for eta=±1. Prime by prime, any rho not associated to lambda shared by two cubic factors divides beta by their difference and then alpha by one factor, excluded. Thus every other prime exponent belongs to exactly one kappa and must be a multiple of3. Each pairwise lambda difference has *exactly* valuation1, giving at most one high valuation and, from their exact sum3n, the distribution3n−2,1,1. Substituting these normalized factors into the independently expanded linear relation gives exponent `3n−3`, not `3n−2`; the sign-unit test makes the resulting equation a member of the same enlarged family. This separately checks closure, integrality, nonzero terms, coprimality and the bottom stair n1. The exact finite residue and linear-relation regressions verify the universal local identities; the general descent is the symbolic argument just reconstructed. The teaching paragraph now correctly says the positive integer exponent decreases, rather than calling unordered Eisenstein elements a “positive solution.”

**Boundary.** All nine named Chapter 10 results now have internal two-pass records. Four new regressions check factor searches, norm counts and complete finite cubic residue/unit identities; they do not substitute for UFD/descent. Totals **61/72 named results and 122/144 solutions**, leaving **11 named and 22 solutions**. Next: the six remaining Chapter 10 exercises, then Chapter 11 characters and series. ## DEP-053 — all remaining quadratic-ring exercises (4 November 2026)

**`ex:gaussgcd`.** Pass A: p divides `(a+i)(a−i)` but neither factor (its imaginary coefficient is±1). If gcd(p,a+i) were a unit, multiplying its Bézout relation by a−i would force p to divide that factor; if it were an associate of p, it would divide a+i. Both are impossible. Thus its norm is a proper nontrivial divisor of p², hence p. The numerical divisions are `1013=22(45+i)+(23−22i)` and `45+i=(1+i)(23−22i)`. Pass B use the checked split classification p=pi*conjugate(pi): a+i must contain at least one of these primes because its norm is divisible by p, but cannot contain both because p does not divide its imaginary coefficient. Its gcd with p is exactly one split prime of norm p. Independently,23−22i divides1013 with quotient23+22i and45+i with quotient1+i, while its first remainder expression certifies every common divisor divides it. Ties in rounding are harmless; associates are allowed.

**`ex:euler1000009`.** Pass A: any norm-p Gaussian factor is irreducible, and UFD identifies it up to unit with one of the two conjugate factors in a known prime representation. Units/conjugation give precisely coordinate signs and order, proving part(a). The two norm representations of1000009 have different absolute-coordinate sets, hence it is composite. Every displayed Gaussian division in part(b) checks, with remainder norms decreasing to3413 and zero;293*3413=1000009. Pass B use prime divisibility directly: if alpha and beta have the same ordinary-prime norm, alpha divides beta*conjugate(beta), so divides one factor; the quotient has norm1 and is a unit. This proves uniqueness without matching whole factorizations. Independently, the cross-product imaginary magnitude is232084; ordinary Euclid gives gcd(1000009,232084)=3413, confirming the Gaussian factor's norm and an explicit proper integer factor. The argument does not need either293 or3413 to be certified prime.

**`ex:x3y21`.** Pass A: odd y would give x³2mod4, impossible, so y is even and x³=y²+1 positive odd. Common Gaussian primes of y+i and y−i divide2i, hence must be1+i; odd norms exclude that prime, so the factors are coprime. UFD makes each a cube up to a unit; cubing permutes the four units, as now explained in source. Comparing the imaginary coefficient gives `b(3a²−b²)=1`, so b±1; b1 requires3a²2, while b−1 gives a0. Therefore y0 and x1. Pass B independently derive the generic quadratic cube identity `(a+b theta)³=(a³−3Dab²)+(3a²b−Db³)theta` for theta²=−D. For D1, the forced divisor b|1 gives exactly the same two complete cases; no bounded search substitutes for that divisibility. Recheck coprimality by norm divisibility at the sole prime above2 and the unit-cube table directly. All signed integer inputs are included.

**`ex:xxyy`.** Pass A: the form is the norm N(x−y omega). The ramified prime3 has representation1,1; split primes1mod3 have a norm-p element; inert primes2mod3 cannot be such norms. The source now distinguishes ramification at3 from nonassociate splitting. Pass B necessity follows immediately from `x²+xy+y²=(x−y)² mod3`, so a prime is3 or1mod3. For sufficiency, the earlier verified weighted-square exercise gives p=a²+3b² for every prime1mod3. Set x=a−b,y=2b and expand to obtain exactly that norm form. This independent construction uses only earlier Chapter7 tools; signs are allowed, so no extra positivity restriction is needed.

**`ex:y2x3m2`.** Pass A: even y would force even x with x³2or6mod8, impossible; hence y is odd. The conjugate factors in Z[sqrt(−2)] have odd norm and any common irreducible divides `2sqrt(−2)=−sqrt(−2)³`. Since sqrt(−2) has prime norm2, no such divisor can occur in an odd norm. Coprime factorization makes y+sqrt(−2) a unit times a cube; both units±1 are cubes. The coefficient equation `b(3a²−2b²)=1` forces b±1. b−1 would give3a²1; b1 gives a±1, hence y∓5 and x3. Both solutions check directly. Pass B independently reconstruct the common-prime argument using the checked UFD and prime-norm criterion, then apply the generic D2 cube expansion above. Exhausting the divisors of coefficient1 is a complete universal case analysis: b−1 is impossible, and b1 forces exactly a±1. The cited Euclidean-division result is now accompanied by the UFD theorem, so no later-ring theorem is silently assumed. x is automatically positive since its cube is y²+2.

**`ex:t1-split13`.** Pass A:13 is1mod4 and1mod3, so both classifications predict splitting. The norms2²+3² and3²−3(−1)+(−1)² both equal13. Pass B multiply the coordinate pairs directly: `(2+3i)(2−3i)=13`, and `(3−omega)(4+omega)=13` using omega²=−1−omega; the second factor is exactly the conjugate. Prime norms independently certify the displayed factors as irreducible and hence prime.

**Boundary.** All nine named and eight keyed Chapter 10 obligations now have two internal records. Three new exact finite tests check Gaussian certificates, coefficient/gcd cases and norm representations, but general completeness rests on the reconstructed proofs. ## DEP-054 — character sums, count, orthogonality and first exercises (4 November 2026)

**Source improvements.** Before using conjugates or analytic bounds, the text now proves `chi(u)^phi(m)=1` from Euler and chi(1)=1, hence |chi(u)|=1 on units and0 elsewhere. The empty product/modulus1 case is explicit. The character-table exercise notes its zero n5 term. The chi8 exercise now supplies the requested prime-by-prime table through19, which was previously omitted despite a correct general proof.

**`prop:t1-charsum`.** Pass A: nonunits contribute zero. The principal row sums to phi(m). A nonprincipal character has a unit b with chi(b)≠1; multiplication by b permutes units, so S=chi(b)S and S0. Pass B directly use earlier CRT/prime-power coordinates, independently parametrizing every character by generator-root images. The sum over unit exponent tuples factors into finite geometric sums, one per cyclic coordinate. If any image is nontrivial its sum is zero; otherwise the product is the unit count. This explicit construction derives the needed parametrization rather than assuming a later duality theorem. At m1 the sole character is constant1.

**`prop:t1-unitproduct`.** Pass A: iterated CRT identifies the residue ring with its prime-power factors and therefore identifies units with unit tuples; earlier odd-prime primitive roots and the sign/5 structure for powers of two give the stated cyclic product. Pass B choose CRT idempotents e_i for the pairwise coprime prime-power moduli. Reduction and the explicit inverse `tuple(u_i)->sum e_i u_i` preserve addition/multiplication; a tuple is invertible exactly when all u_i are, with inverse `sum e_i u_i^(-1)`. For2^k,k>=3, the map `(a,b)->(-1)^a5^b` is a homomorphism from C2×C_(2^(k−2)) and bijective by Chapter5's uniqueness. The cases1,2,4 are trivial/cyclic separately. No classification of arbitrary finite abelian groups is imported.

**`thm:t1-charcount`.** Pass A: a generator of a cyclic group of order N may map to any one of the N distinct complex Nth roots of unity, and that choice uniquely defines the homomorphism. Independent factors multiply their choices, giving exactly phi(m). A nontrivial unit component g^k with N∤k is separated by mapping g to exp(2pi*i/N), other factors trivially. Pass B construct every function explicitly: for each local exponent tuple k_i and parameter tuple j_i, set its unit value to `exp(2pi*i*sum j_i*k_i/N_i)` and extend by zero. Coordinate uniqueness proves well-definition, multiplication and distinctness; any character's generator images force exactly this formula, proving completeness independently. Trivial N1 factors and the empty tuple cause no exception.

**`thm:t1-ortho`.** Pass A: root-of-unity values give conjugate=unit inverse, so the row product is the character chi*psi^(-1); its sum is principal exactly when chi=psi. For the column sum, a separating character permutes all characters by multiplication and forces cancellation unless a is the identity. For the residue indicator, nonunit a or n gives zero, while unit a replaces its conjugate by evaluation at a^(-1), reducing to the column sum. Pass B expand the explicit coordinate formula above. Row inner products factor into `sum_(k_i) exp(2pi*i*(j_i−l_i)k_i/N_i)`; all factors survive exactly when j=l. Column products instead sum over j_i and survive exactly when the two unit exponent tuples agree. If either input is a nonunit every summand vanishes. These two independent geometric-sum computations prove every part without an abstract dual group theorem and include m1 and arbitrary signed integer inputs via periodicity.

**`ex:t1-char5`.** Pass A: powers of2 are1,2,4,3, and the four fourth-root choices give the printed rows on1,2,3,4. Squares1,4 identify the real nonprincipal row as Legendre. The sample inner products are0 and n5 contributes0. Pass B independently enumerate all assignments of fourth roots to nonidentity units and retain only multiplication-preserving assignments; exactly the four rows remain. Direct conjugated sums check all distinct rows, including the signs of i and−i.

**`ex:t1-chi8check`.** Pass A: the four odd residue classes give exponent parities even,odd,odd,even: classes1,7 have sign+,3,5 sign−. This is an exhaustive proof for all odd primes, not a bounded sample. Pass B expand `(8r+s)²−1` over8 for s1,3,5,7 and independently check the parity. Exact Euler powers for primes3,5,7,11,13,17,19 give signs−,−,+,−,−,+,−, matching the newly supplied table and chi8's definition.

**Boundary.** Four finite tests compare small character constructions against all possible homomorphisms, check counts/separation/multiplication and both orthogonality directions including noncyclic groups and nonunits. ## DEP-055 — series, products, sieve and boundary results: named-result register complete internally (4 November 2026)

**`lem:t1-pseries`.** Pass A: for decreasing t^(−s), each term n>=2 is bounded by the integral over[n−1,n]. Summing the tail after floor(X)>=1 and taking the limit gives exactly `floor(X)^(1−s)/(s−1)`. Pass B group terms into dyadic blocks: a block with at most2^k terms each at most2^(−ks) gives a convergent geometric majorant with ratio2^(1−s)<1. Independently apply the finite cutoff inequality through M and its elementary antiderivative before taking M to infinity, confirming the stated sharp endpoint choice floor(X). X1 never requires an integral from0; s1 is excluded.

**`lem:t1-dseries`.** Pass A: the absolute double mass is the product of two finite absolute series. Grouping pairs by their positive integer product gives the Dirichlet convolution and absolute convergence of the grouped series. Pass B for N>=K², the region nm<=N contains the entire K×K rectangle. The absolute mass outside that rectangle is at most `A_tail(K)*B_abs+A_abs*B_tail(K)`, tending to zero. Thus hyperbolic partial sums converge to the product of the two rectangular-limit sums. Triangle inequalities give absolute convergence of the convolution series as well. This finite-region proof independently justifies the rearrangement for arbitrary complex coefficients; no multiplicativity or positivity of coefficients is assumed.

**`thm:t1-eulerprod`.** Pass A: each finite set of prime factors has an absolutely convergent product of geometric series, identifying the finite product with the sum over supported integers by FTA. Every omitted integer exceeds X, so the p-series tail forces convergence to the full series. Each denominator's modulus is at most1+p^(−s), and the positive product of these is bounded by exp(sum p^(−s)); therefore the partial products have a uniform positive modulus lower bound and the limit cannot vanish. Pass B put g(n)=mu(n)f(n). Complete multiplicativity and f(1)=1 give `(g*f)(N)=f(N)*sum_(d|N)mu(d)=epsilon(N)`. Both series converge absolutely by |f|,|g|<=1, so the checked convolution lemma makes their product1. Expand only the finite *inverse* Euler product into its finite squarefree-subset sum; it approaches the g-series by the integer tail bound. That limit is nonzero by the product1 identity, so reciprocating gives the f-series and a nonzero Euler-product limit independently of the smooth-number/geometric expansion. Zero prime values and complex phases are allowed. The source also explains the vanishing s1 example with finite positive products containing harmonic partial sums, avoiding an unsupported boundary identity.

**`cor:t1-mu-zeta`.** Pass A: |mu|<=1 gives both absolute hypotheses. Convolution with1 yields the epsilon series1, and the previously proved nonzero zeta product can be reciprocated continuously. Pass B expand `product_(p<=X)(1−p^(−s))` over the finite squarefree subsets. Its terms are exactly mu(d)d^(−s) on that support, with all missing nonzero terms at d>X. The p-series tail identifies its limit with the full mu-series independently of the double-product grouping. No false complete-multiplicativity assumption about mu is made; the source's absolute-convergence and reciprocal-limit steps are now explicit.

**`thm:t1-sieve`.** Pass A: insert the finite gcd indicator `sum_(d|gcd(n,P(z)))mu(d)` and exchange two finite sums. Counting positive multiples yields floor(x/d), valid also for0<=x<1. If x>=1,z>=sqrt(x), any composite survivor would have a prime factor at mostsqrt(n)<=z, contradiction, so survivors are1 and primes in(z,x]. This is exactly `1+pi(x)−pi(min(x,z))`, including z>=x. Pass B independently apply inclusion–exclusion to the finite sets of multiples of primes<=z; each subset intersects in the multiples of its squarefree product, with sign(−1)^size. This gives the same formula without the Möbius gcd identity. The sqrt condition is sufficient, not necessary: there can be no residual composite even below that threshold. Exact fractional x and noninteger z tests corroborate the endpoint handling.

**`prop:t1-chi4series`, repaired proof.** Pass A: complete pairs give positive terms `2/((4k+1)(4k+3))`. Their sums increase. The old proof only established each sum<1, which would permit limit1; it now retains the fixed negative pair1/3−1/5 to prove the uniform upper bound13/15<1. The second positive group gives a strict lower bound above2/3. Every ungrouped partial sum differs by at most one remaining nonzero term tending to zero, so the original series has the same limit. Pass B integrate the finite geometric identity: the N-group sum is `integral_0^1 (1−t^(4N))/(1+t²) dt`. The omitted integral is at most1/(4N+1), hence tends to zero without interchanging an unproved infinite sum/integral. The limit integral is strictly greater than the integral of1−t² (=2/3), since their difference is positive except at0, and strictly less than1 (for example bound the integrand by1 on[0,1/2] and4/5 on[1/2,1], giving9/10). This independently proves convergence and both strict inequalities using Volume1's finite polynomial integration tools.

**`prop:t1-charbound`.** Pass A: divide floor(x) into complete periods and a remainder. Every full nonprincipal period sums to0, and at most phi(m) remainder terms are nonzero, each of modulus1. Pass B let u be the number of unit residues in the remainder. Its sum is the negative of the complementary residues' sum in that complete period, so its modulus is at most both u and phi(m)−u, hence at most phi(m)/2, which implies the printed weaker bound. This independently uses the exact full-period cancellation and handles x0. At modulus1 there is no nonprincipal character, so that case is vacuous.

**Boundary.** All **72/72 named results** now have two documented internal checks. ## DEP-056 — remaining elementary sieve and character-series exercises (4 November 2026)

**`ex:t1-sieve30`.** Pass A opens the eight squarefree divisor terms: Phi(30,5)=30−15−10−6+5+3+2−1=8 and Phi(50,5)=50−25−16−10+8+5+3−1=14. A surviving composite must have two prime factors at least7, hence be at least49. This explains agreement at30 and the single extra survivor49 at50. Pass B independently lists the residue classes prime to30:1,7,11,13,17,19,23,29; their representatives up to50 give14 survivors, and direct trial primality gives pi(30)=10,pi(50)=15,pi(5)=3. The theorem gives a sufficient, not necessary, square-root threshold. Added the second eight-term calculation and explicit least-composite reasoning to the solution. Hint and idea agree.

**`ex:t1-phi-ratio`.** Pass A uses the earlier proved Gauss divisor identity phi*1=id and mu*1=epsilon: convolving gives phi=mu*id. Division by n and multiplicativity give the local factor1−1/p. Pass B independently counts integers1..n coprime to n by finite inclusion–exclusion on its distinct prime divisors. Every subset product d divides n, so its multiples number exactly n/d; division by n gives both the divisor sum and product. n=1 has empty product1. All tools precede this exercise; hint and solution align.

**`ex:t1-mu-floor`.** Pass A replaces each floor by its finite multiple count and regroups by m=dk; the coefficient is sum_{d|m}mu(d)=epsilon(m), leaving exactly m=1 for real x>=1. Pass B defines F(N) for integer N; F(N)−F(N−1)=sum_{d|N}mu(d), so F(1)=1 and every subsequent increment is zero. For real x, floor(x/d)=floor(floor(x)/d), extending the integer result. At10 the nonzero terms are10−5−3−2+1−1+1=1; the required lower bound x>=1 is essential. Hint, idea and writeup satisfy it.

**`ex:t1-squarefree`.** Pass A splits the detector multiplicatively: at p^a its value is1 for a=0,1 and1−1=0 for a>=2. Summing and reversing finite sums counts multiples of d² by floor(x/d²). Pass B performs independent inclusion–exclusion on the set of prime squares dividing n: (1−1)^r vanishes exactly when r>0. Applying the same exclusion to multiples up to x produces the counting formula directly. n=1 and0<=x<1 are covered by empty products/sums. Clarified positive n and nonnegative real x in the prompt; this avoids an undefined square-root/zero-divisibility endpoint. Hint and solution remain aligned.

**`ex:t1-char-series`, all three parts.** Pass A: complete nonprincipal character periods cancel; an incomplete period contains at most phi(m) nonzero terms of modulus1, establishing the exact requested bound (now written explicitly). Set A(0)=0 and chi(n)=A(n)−A(n−1); subtract the two finite sums to derive the displayed identity for arbitrary complex b_n. For b_n=1/n this gives A(N)/N plus sum A(n)/(n(n+1)); the first tends to0 and the latter converges absolutely by bounded A and the telescoping positive majorant. No absolute convergence of the original series is claimed. Pass B independently uses the complementary-period bound min(u,phi−u)<=phi/2. Group the original series in complete blocks r=1..m and subtract the constant reciprocal1/(km+m), whose coefficient is the zero period sum. For k>=1 each block has modulus at most phi(m)/(m*k²), so the block series converges by p-series2. The incomplete block tends to0, bounded by m/(km+1). This proves convergence of ordinary partial sums without summation by parts. The finite identity can also be verified by collecting each coefficient b_j separately: A(j)−A(j−1)=chi(j). At modulus1 no nonprincipal character exists. Earlier orthogonality, root moduli and convergence tools are explicit; hint and all parts align.

**`ex:t1-group3`.** Pass A computes2/3+2/35+2/99=2578/3465, with positive difference from2/3 and1−S=887/3465>0. Pass B directly sums the six alternating reciprocals with common denominator3465; alternatively the finite-integral representation from DEP-055 gives a positive integral strictly between the first group and1. ## DEP-057 — pseudoprimes, partial certificates and Fermat quotients (4 November 2026)

**`pb:pseudo`, all three parts.** Pass A:341=11*31 and2^10=1 mod11,2^5=1 mod31; both exponents divide340, so CRT gives the Fermat congruence. For composite odd n, write n=ab with1<a<n:2^a−1 is a proper factor of m=2^n−1. The pseudoprime congruence gives n|(m−1), while2^n=1 mod m, so2^(m−1)=1 mod m. Also m>n and odd, allowing unbounded iteration from341. For prime p>=5, N=(4^p−1)/3=(2^p−1)(2^p+1)/3 has two integer factors>1. Fermat and inversion of3 mod p give p|(N−1), and N−1 is even, so2p|(N−1). As2^(2p)=1 mod N, N passes the test. Pass B: compute2^10=1 mod341 directly (1024=3*341+1), hence2^340=1. In the n-to-m construction the order of2 mod m divides n; independently the exact identity m−1=2(2^(n−1)−1) makes it a multiple of n. Composite factorization and growth remain separately necessary, and hold. In (c), multiply N−1 by3:3(N−1)=4(4^(p−1)−1), divisible by p and even; p!=3 and odd force2p|N−1. This verifies the quotient/division step without cancellation modulo a composite. Hints, ideas and tools are all earlier; finite checks include341,561,645 and primes through47.

**`pb:pocklington`, both parts.** Pass A: for each prime l|n, Fermat-test congruence ensures a is a unit; e=ord_l(a) divides n−1 and l−1. If q^g exactly divides n−1 and q|F, failing q^g|e would give e|(n−1)/q, contradicting the gcd1 hypothesis modulo l. Thus F|e|l−1, even when F shares primes with (n−1)/F. Every l>F; if F>sqrt(n), the elementary small-prime-factor lemma forces n prime. For Proth with positive k,m, odd k<2^m, the half-power−1 gives full-power1 and gcd(−2,N)=1. Also N<2^(2m), so F=2^m is enough. Pass B independently use the cyclic subgroup of the residue field generated by a: the element a^((n−1)/q) is not1 for each q|F, so its order contains the full q-primary part of n−1. The l−1 divisibility follows either from the earlier order theorem or coset counting. For Proth directly, writing N−1=k2^m, the half-power−1 forces the order modulo each l|N to contain2^m, since an order with smaller 2-adic valuation would divide k2^(m−1). Hence every l>=2^m+1, whose square exceeds N. Repeated-squaring residues3^128=565,3^512=2379,3^1024=341 mod3329 multiply to−1:341*2379=2292 mod3329,2292*565=−1. Added these intermediate values to the writeup and specified positive F,k,m to close domain ambiguities. F=1 is vacuous but cannot satisfy F>sqrt(n) for n>1. Hint and idea match both proofs.

**`pb:fermatquot`, all four parts.** Pass A: expand (1+p*q(a))(1+p*q(b)) modulo p² for additivity and (a+pt)^(p−1) modulo p² for the shift; coefficient(p−1)a^(p−2)t reduces to−t/a. For each1<=k<p, binom(p,k)/p reduces to(−1)^(k−1)/k; summing the binomial expansion2^p−2 and dividing the exact integer identity by p yields2q_p(2). Applying additivity to (p−1)! and writing it as−1+pW gives q_p((p−1)!)=W mod p, since p−1 is even. For orders e mod p,E mod p², reduction gives e|E and (1+cp)^p=1 mod p² gives E|pe. Thus E=e or pe; the full Fermat congruence modulo p² is equivalent to E|p−1, excluding pe. Pass B independently use the first-order binomial coefficient rule f(a+pt)=f(a)+pt*f'(a) mod p² for the polynomial X^(p−1); its derivative is−a^(-1) mod p. Multiplication of the exact lifts gives the additive coefficient directly. For (b), the coefficient identity k*binom(p,k)=p*binom(p−1,k−1) and binom(p−1,j)=(−1)^j mod p independently give each summand. For Lerch lift each residue a to T(a)=a+p*a*q_p(a), for which T(a)^(p−1)=1 mod p². Their product equals (p−1)!*(1+p*sum q_p(a))=−1+p*(W−sum q_p(a)) mod p² and has (p−1)-st power1. Expanding around−1 forces W−sum q=0 mod p. Finally, if2^(p−1)=1 mod p², write p−1=e*h with p not dividing h; from2^e=1+cp, the equality1+hcp=1 mod p² forces c=0 and hence E=e. Conversely E=e implies the Fermat congruence directly. This independently proves the equivalence without requiring the E=e/pe classification. Clarified p not dividing ab in the prompt; negative t/representatives are valid. All denominators are nonzero mod p; p=3 and Wieferich examples1093,3511 covered by exact regressions. Hints and tools match.

## DEP-058 — final five exercise solutions (4 November 2026)

**`pb:mullin`, both parts.** Pass A: a new selected prime divides P+1 and so divides none of P's previous prime factors; every term is distinct. Exact least-factor steps give2,3,7,43,13,53,5, with1807=13*139,23479=53*443,1244335=5*248867. Trial primality of13,53,139,443 and the small excluded primes validates the least choices. For the largest-factor sequence,2 occurs once and3 is the second term. For every later P,6|P and v2(P)=1. If its largest new prime were5, P+1 would be5^c, forcing4|P, contradiction. Initial terms2,3 are not5. Pass B independently reason modulo each old prime P+1=1, hence no repetitions and P=2 times an odd number. After3 appears P+1 has no factor2 or3; a number with largest factor5 must be a power of5, congruent1 mod4, whereas P+1=3 mod4. The exact recursion with independently factored integers reproduces all seven requested terms. The value5 can divide a later candidate without being its largest factor (251035=5*50207); the proof does not incorrectly exclude that event. Hint/idea/writeup align, and no claim that every prime appears is made.

**`pb:artin`.** Pass A: with safe prime p=2q+1>7, q is odd and possible orders are1,2,q,2q. For q=1 mod4, p=3 mod8 and (2/p)=−1, so Euler excludes q; orders1,2 would require p|1 or3. For q=3 mod4, p=7 mod8, so(−2/p)=(−1/p)(2/p)=−1; orders1,2 for−2 would require p|3, again impossible. Thus the chosen element has order2q. Pass B independently use the cyclic generator g: nonsquares have odd exponent, so their order has its full factor2. As q is prime, only2 or2q remain; squaring the candidate gives4!=1 mod p for p>7. This forces2q. The supplementary laws give exactly the two stated signs. Finite complete-orbit enumeration for safe primes below2000 corroborates. Aligned the writeup's bound with p>7; corrected the forward-looking Artin-conjecture statement to exclude−1 as well as perfect squares. The former nonsquare-only wording was false for−1. Earlier order/cyclicity and reciprocity proofs suffice; hint and idea match.

**`pb:euclidgame`, both parts.** Pass A uses strong induction on the positive sum. Divisibility gives an immediate winning move; otherwise b=qa+s with0<s<a. If b/a<phi, q=1 is forced and the unique successor has ratio a/s>phi. If phi<b/a<2, that successor instead has ratio belowphi. If q>=2, legal subtraction can leave either(s,a) or(a,a+s); with t=s/a their ratios1/t and1+t lie on opposite sides ofphi because phi−1=1/phi. One successor is losing. No rational ratio equals irrational phi, all successor sums decrease and no zero division occurs. For uniform1<=a<b<=N, the exact count is sum_{a<N/phi}(N−floor(phi*a)); replacing floors and using the arithmetic progression yields N²/(2phi)+O(N), over N(N−1)/2 total pairs. Pass B independently derive the recursive outcome rule: q>=2 wins by offering two consecutive Euclidean remainders of opposite status; q=1 reverses the outcome. The proposed losing interval1<b/a<phi obeys this recursion since the involution r->1/(r−1) swaps the two ratio intervals until the division terminal. Exhaustive all-legal-move dynamic programming through b=180 checks the result against the exact inequality(2b−a)²>5a², with no floating threshold. For probability independently slice by b: winning choices of a satisfy a<b/phi, so the count is sum_{b=2}^N floor(b/phi)=N²/(2phi)+O(N), the same limit. Specified the probability model in the prompt; other random-pair models are not tacitly claimed. Golden-ratio and finite-count tools precede the problem; hint/idea/full solution align.

**`pb:pascalthree`, all three parts.** Pass A: Lucas factors each coefficient by base-p digits; nonzero coefficients correspond bijectively to0<=k_i<=n_i, giving product(n_i+1). The mod3 sign sum factors too: rows of one digit0,1,2 have signed totals1,2,1, so the difference is2^(number of1 digits). Summing over all length-k ternary strings gives nonzero total6^k and signed difference4^k. Thus N1=(6^k+4^k)/2,N2=(6^k−4^k)/2,N0=3^k(3^k+1)/2−6^k. Their proportions among all entries tend to1,0,0; among nonzero entries the two proportions tend to1/2. Pass B independently use the polynomial congruence(1+X)^n=product_i(1+X^(p^i))^(n_i) mod p: choosing small digit exponents has no carries and exactly determines coefficients. For p3, the local coefficient lists[1],[1,1],[1,2,1] have (nonzero count,signed count)=(1,1),(2,2),(3,1). Appending each ternary digit multiplies these statistics; summing all three choices recursively multiplies totals by6 and4, starting at row0. This independently proves the displayed totals without presupposing Lucas's count formula. k=0 includes one entry1, and leading zeros change nothing. Explicitly stated both zero limiting proportions in the source. Exact binomial enumeration checks p2,3,5 and first243 ternary rows. All tools local; hints/ideas agree.

**`pb:powerful`, all three parts.** Pass A: every permitted positive prime exponent e>=2 has a unique decomposition2u+3v,u>=0,v=0 or1, with v=e mod2; e0 givesu=v0. This constructs unique positive a,b with b squarefree (including n1). Writing B=floor(x^(1/3)), each squarefree b<=B gives floor(sqrt(x)/b^(3/2)) choices. Floor error is at most B. Completing the series costs at most2sqrt(x)/sqrt(B) by the printed integral tail, hence O(x^(1/3)) for x>=1. Expanding finite factors1+p^(-t) counts squarefree integers, and the absolute tail tends to0 for real t>1; factor identity1+u=(1−u²)/(1−u) and nonzero Euler products give sum mu²(b)b^(-t)=zeta(t)/zeta(2t). This supplies the constant at t3/2. Positive comparison series in a^(-2s),b^(-3s) justify the powerful series for real s>1/2. Added the finite-expansion/tail explanation, explicit error estimate and complex extension definitions: n^(-s)=n^(-Re s)*exp(−i Im s log n). The real absolute majorants apply; the prior complete-multiplicative Euler theorem for this phase proves nonzero zeta factors. Thus the same identity holds for Re s>1/2 without silently importing complex analysis. Finally Pell X²−8Y²=1 gives powerful consecutive8Y²,X², since each prime valuation of the first is even except its2-adic valuation3+2v2(Y). First pairs8/9 and288/289 agree.

Pass B independently compare local power series: (1−z^6)/((1−z²)(1−z³))=1+z²/(1−z), with coefficient1 at e0 and every e>=2,0 at e1. Multiplication over primes gives precisely the same powerful indicator, uniqueness and Euler factors. For the asymptotic audit, the exact counting bijection plus |floor y−y|<=1 and the dyadic tail bound sum_{b>B}b^(-3/2)<=C/sqrt(B) independently reproduce the order of error without using the integral estimate; B is comparable to x^(1/3) once x>=8, with1<=x<8 bounded separately. The squarefree constant also follows by its detector: sum_b mu²(b)b^(-t)=sum_d mu(d)d^(-2t)*sum_c c^(-t), whose absolute double majorant is zeta(2Re t)zeta(Re t). The earlier mu–zeta identity gives the quotient. For consecutive pairs no Pell classification is necessary: start(X,Y)=(3,1) and apply(X,Y)->(3X+8Y,X+3Y). Expanding preserves X²−8Y²=1, keeps both positive and strictly increases X, yielding infinitely many distinct pairs directly. Finite independent factorization through10000 checks the representation bijection, exact local Euler coefficients through degree30 and four recurrence pairs. Clarified positive a,b in the prompt; hints/idea/tool references agree.

## Structured verification entries

**Result/proof entry template:** ID; file:line or exact label; statement/hypotheses; use-site/prerequisites; reconstructed proof; independent second test/proof/reference; edge cases; fixes + commit; status. Add *one row per actual theorem/proof*, including unnamed arguments.

**Exercise entry template:** key, stars, printed chapter; precise problem and edge conditions; independent solution without trusting \writeup; separate adversarial second derivation; hint/idea/writeup alignment; tools introduced before exercise; possible source change + commit; status. The 144 keys are already listed in docs/reviews/volume2-exercise-solution-audit-2026-11-03.md, but that ledger cannot be used to pre-fill VERIFIED_TWICE.

**Example/figure entry template:** stable key, formula/data, independently recalculated result, printed page and visual check when produced, status.

## Checkpoint protocol (mandatory on each active work session)

1. Read PR title/body, comments, this ledger, and full plan; inspect branch head and open CI; compare with main.
2. Pick one specific result/proof or exercise that is UNREVIEWED/BLOCKED. Keep source text plus your independent argument; cite exact local previous proof rather than saying “well known”.
3. Record the finding and write a focused code+test+ledger commit. Never mark a row VERIFIED_TWICE on a single explanation or finite sample.
4. Update PR description/comment with **what changed**, exact SHA and checked tests, **what is not checked**, and the **next exact task**.
5. At session end include the next location and proof question. Leave draft, do not merge or publish before P13.
